title: "BIB322 — full lesson text"

# BIB322 — The Poetic Books

## Course objectives

- 1. Apply principles of sound biblical interpretation.
- 2. Identify literary devices found in the Poetic Books and explain their significance.
- 3. Summarize the contributions of wisdom writings to living a full Christian life in contemporary cultures and contexts.
- 4. Identify the form, content, and social context of wisdom writings.
- 5. Compare and contrast Israel’s wisdom tradition with that of her neighbors.
- 6. Describe the compilation of Job, Psalms, and Proverbs.
- 7. Explain the fundamental tension in wisdom writings between doctrinal belief and life experiences.
- 8. Identify major themes of wisdom tradition and ways they, at times, stand in tension with one another.
- 9. Summarize the special contributions of individual wisdom writers.
- 10. Identify ways wisdom tradition influenced and shaped New Testament faith.

## 1 - Introduction to Hebrew Poetry

### Reading the Bible

#### 1.1.1 — Explain why the Bible is our guide for living.

The Bible, the best-selling book of all time, is unique. Millions of people look to it for guidance every day as the Word of God, and it has been translated into many different languages. Most people who read the Bible must read a translation because they cannot read the biblical languages: Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek. We will briefly consider principles of interpreting this captivating Book as a background to its Poetic Books.

The Authority of the Bible

The Bible is a book, but not the same kind of book as, say, a contemporary novel, a biography about Abraham Lincoln, or a geography textbook. The Bible is not a single book but a collection of sixty-six books written by a variety of authors over one thousand years. The first five books of the Bible, also known as the Pentateuch, were written by Moses, the leader of a young nation recently delivered from Egyptian slavery by the power of God. The final book, Revelation, was written in exile by the elderly John, one of Jesus’ disciples. The span of time between Moses and John was well over a thousand years, and the difference in cultures between the leader of a nation of shepherds and a man of Roman Palestine* was significant.

As one would expect, the Bible includes many types of literature. Much of Moses’ writings are legal codes and the early history of Israel. Joshua through 2 Chronicles contain the history of the nation from Moses’ death through the exile of the southern tribes of Israel known as the Kingdom of Judah. In the middle of the Bible are the Poetic Books. The Old Testament ends with several prophetic books warning Israel to return to the Lord. The New Testament begins with the Gospels, which are historical accounts of Jesus’ life. The book of Acts gives an account of the early years of the church. Then follow several letters, both to churches and individuals, and the Revelation, which serves as a conclusion to the Bible.

The Bible is unique because of the diversity of its authors and genres , but what most sets it apart from other books is its inspiration . We can speak of any gifted writer as being inspired. William Shakespeare, one of the greatest English writers, wrote plays and poems that are often described as inspired. But only the Bible is the authoritative Word of God, written under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit. In his second letter to Timothy, Paul wrote, “All Scripture is God-breathed and is useful for teaching, rebuking, correcting and training in righteousness, so that the man of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work” (3:16–17). The Holy Spirit guided the writing in such a way that the authors wrote exactly what God intended. Thus we rely on the Bible as our infallible rule of faith and conduct.

Because God inspired the Bible, we must read it differently than we would any other book. We read the Bible not only to learn information and to be entertained but to hear God’s voice, so to speak, as He communicates to us through the Bible. The purpose of Bible study is to discover what God desires to teach us and to submit ourselves to His will.

The Progression of Revelation

Because the Bible was written over such a long period of time, some of the biblical writers understood God’s revelation more fully than others. For example, Moses prophesied that Eve’s offspring would crush the serpent and the serpent would crush his heel (Genesis 3:15), but he did not know the details of the crucifixion and resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth. Similarly, Isaiah prophesied the virgin birth of the Messiah (Isaiah 7:14), but he did not know the identity of Jesus’ earthly parents, Mary and Joseph. In contrast, John the Revelator, who wrote several hundred years later, knew the details of Jesus’ birth, ministry, death, and resurrection. He also understood the presence of the Holy Spirit in the church after Jesus’ ascension, and he knew Jesus would return in power to usher His people into God’s presence forever. Due to his vantage point late in biblical history, John had a much clearer view of God’s plan for the salvation of humanity.

Therefore, God’s revelation progressed throughout biblical history. Now we have the benefit of all sixty-six books of the Bible and two thousand years of church history to help us develop correct theology. But Joshua, for instance, had only five biblical books, and he lived centuries before God revealed Himself through Jesus Christ. As the history of God’s people unfolded, God revealed more and more of His plans for us.

It is important to understand the progression of God’s revelation to help put the Bible in context . When we read the biblical books of poetry, we must understand that the writers did not have access to the New Testament or even all of the Old Testament. Neither did Paul have access to the book of Revelation. Each biblical book has its own place in time in God’s history.

This truth has important implications concerning how we interpret the Bible. We must always consider the context of each passage as we study it. We cannot take a verse from its biblical context and treat it as if it were written yesterday, forgetting that every line of Scripture was written first to people who lived thousands of years before us. An example from the Pentateuch will help us understand the dangers of such interpretation. Leviticus 19:27 reads, “Do not cut the hair at the sides of your head or clip off the edges of your beard.” Isolated from its biblical and historical contexts, this verse would seem to convict the clean-cut men of today who trim their hair every few weeks and shave their faces every day. However, studying the context of the verse reveals that the law against trimming a person’s hair has to do with worshipping idols. In the time this law was written, men trimmed their hair as part of idol worship and service. Such idolatry was what the law prohibited. Trimming one’s hair is not wrong except when it is done to honor false gods.

Scripture Interprets Scripture

No passage of Scripture can be correctly interpreted without comparing it to the rest of the Bible. In his introduction to biblical interpretation, Grant Osborne writes:

Terry’s dictum still stands: “No single statement or obscure passage of one book can be allowed to set aside a doctrine which is clearly established by many passages” (1890:579). I would add that doctrines should not be built upon a single passage but rather should summarize all that Scripture says on that topic. If there are no clarifying passages (for example, on baptism for the dead in 1 Corinthians 15:29 or a compartmentalized Hades in Luke 16:22–26), we must be careful about seeing a statement of dogma. (Osborne 1991, 11)

Thus, when interpreting a biblical poem, we must read it in light of what the entire Bible says about that topic.

For example, Psalm 1 contrasts the way of the righteous with the way of the wicked. Psalm 1:3 reads, “Whatever he does prospers.” At face value, this statement could be understood to mean that the righteous will prosper in everything they undertake. Taken to an extreme, this thought could become the basis for a teaching that people who are poor, suffering, or sick are unrighteous.

If the teaching of Psalm 1 is interpreted by looking at all the Bible teaches, however, we see that such an interpretation is wrong. In the psalms themselves, we see David in distress: “My God, my God, why have you forsaken me? Why are you so far from saving me, so far from the words of my groaning? O my God, I cry out by day, but you do not answer, by night, and am not silent” (Psalm 22:1–2). This lament was written by a man “after God’s own heart.” It was also quoted by Jesus while suffering on the cross. Another example of the suffering of the righteous can be found in Job. A righteous man, Job suffered although he followed the law of the Lord and lived a godly life. Thus, teaching about the prosperity of the righteous must include teaching found in the rest of the Bible, which clearly shows that the righteous often suffer and are not necessarily prosperous here on earth.

**Q:** What do we mean when we say the Bible is inspired?

**A:** The Holy Spirit superintended the writing in such a way that the authors wrote exactly what God intended. Although each writer’s personality and style comes through, the message God intended is overseen by the Holy Spirit.

**Q:** Describe the progression of revelation.

**A:** Over time, God revealed more and more of His plans for the redemption of humankind from sin. Depending on when they lived, people of God would know more or less about salvation and the Bible. The patriarchs did not have the Bible as we know it. New Testament writers wrote from the perspective of knowing Jesus personally, but they did not have access to all the letters and writings now available to contemporary audiences. Such knowledge, or lack thereof, could make a difference in a writer’s or prophet’s perspective.

**Q:** How can using Scripture to interpret Scripture keep us from faulty interpretation?

**A:** Isolated Scripture verses are best understood in light of the full teaching of the Bible. By comparing brief passages with the treatment of similar topics elsewhere in Scripture, we get a balanced and more reliable idea of what Scripture intends on a particular subject.

### Understanding Poetic Genre

#### 1.2.1 — Define literary genre, and explain its importance in biblical interpretation.

Understanding genre is important no matter what we read. Genre describes a type of literature that has a particular style, form, or content. For instance, when we flip through the pages of a newspaper, we expect to find unbiased, factual information about current events. When we look for a number in a telephone book, we know the columns of information on the pages are lists of the people in the area and their addresses and telephone numbers. When we open a novel, we know that the plot is fiction, and when we read a poem, we recognize the language as being metaphorical.

When we study the Poetic Books of the Bible, we need to be aware of the genres they represent. All of the books of Job, Psalms, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and Song of Solomon are poetry or contain poetry. Therefore, we must read and interpret what they are trying to say in light of their poetic style or form.

Characteristics of Poetry

What is the difference between poetry and prose? Prose is “ordinary” writing similar to everyday speech. In contrast, poetry is terse (short, concise, no unnecessary words) and imaginative and usually has a regular rhythm.

In many languages, such as English, French, Latin, and Greek, poetry has uniform meter and often rhyme. Each line of such poetry has a regular number of stressed and unstressed syllables. Additionally, the words at the ends of lines tend to rhyme in regular patterns. Indeed, meter and rhyme traditionally have been two of the most distinguishing characteristics of English poetry.

Hebrew poetry, on the other hand, does not generally use rhyme for poetic effect. Although not altogether absent, meter is also apparently not essential to Hebrew poetry. For years, scholars have attempted to discover the key to metrical patterns in Hebrew poetry, but “in our judgment, however, no attempt . . . to identify the presence of something like classic Greek metric forms in Hebrew poetry has proved successful” (Petersen and Richards 1992, 39). In fact, many scholars now contend that meter is not a characteristic of Hebrew poetry at all.

If Hebrew poetry has neither rhyme nor meter, how can we be sure a particular text is poetry? The answer is not exactly black and white; some degree of uncertainty exists. The manuscripts on which our modern Hebrew texts and English translations are based were not written in verse, so a person cannot tell the difference between poetry and prose in these texts just by looking at them. At times, such as in Isaiah 4:2, scholars disagree about whether the text is poetry or prose. Thus, those who edit our contemporary English translations make decisions as to which portions of the text are poetry and which are prose. The scholars who produce the translations separate poetry from prose by looking for elements that are characteristic of poetry.

Sections of poetry are easy to spot in these translations because they are set in verse. The lines are of roughly equal length, and they are not organized into paragraphs as are sections of prose. The first English translation to set all the poetic portions of the Bible in verse was the Revised Standard Version of 1952.

Besides verse format, poetry has other identifying characteristics. One trait of Hebrew poetry is terseness. That is, Hebrew poetry is compact and uses only words that are necessary. For example, Exodus 15:3 reads simply, “The Lord is a warrior; the Lord is his name.” The original Hebrew is even more terse than the English translation because it has no verb.

Another element of Hebrew poetry is its use of vivid imagery . Tremper Longman defines an image as a “sensation evoked in the mind of the reader by the language of a text” (1988, 128–129). Psalm 104:3 describes God’s greatness with striking imagery: “He makes the clouds his chariot and rides on the wings of the wind.” In another psalm, the writer uses the pleasant sensation of taste to describe how desirable the Lord is, saying, “Taste and see that the Lord is good” (Psalm 34:8).

Another important characteristic of Hebrew poetry is parallelism . Parallelism uses two lines to present and then develop an image. It may repeat a thought or the way the lines are structured. Sometimes the correlation between the lines is so close that the second line seems merely to paraphrase the first. Psalm 1:1 contains an example of parallelism that involves three lines:

Who does not walk in the counsel of the wicked or stand in the way of sinners or sit in the seat of mockers.

Each of these lines says essentially the same thing using a slightly different nuance. We will discuss more about parallelism in the next lesson.

The Poetry/Prose Continuum

Although terseness, imagery, and parallelism are characteristics of Hebrew poetry, the presence of these and other poetic devices in a text does not necessarily make the text poetry. Conversely, the absence of one or more of these elements does not necessarily mean the text is not poetry. Rather, poetry tends to have more of these elements than does prose. In other words, if a text is terse, has a great deal of imagery, and uses a lot of parallelism, it is probably poetry. If the text is wordy, void of imagery, and without parallelism, it is probably prose.

We can think of poetry and prose as being on the ends of a continuum. Poetry has a great number of poetic elements, while prose does not. Texts with fewer poetic elements are difficult to categorize and may or may not be poetry.

**Q:** Why is understanding literary genre foundational to interpreting the Poetic Books?

**A:** When we understand the genre of what we are reading, we know what to expect in its style, form, and content. Because the Poetic Books are written in the style of ancient Hebrew poetry, we will understand the writers’ use of such devices as repetition and parallelism and expect to have ideas conveyed through imagery and patterns.

**Q:** How do English and Hebrew poetry differ?

**A:** English poetry is often characterized by uniform meter and rhyme. Hebrew poetry, on the other hand, does not generally use rhyme and meter for poetic effect. Elements characteristic of Hebrew poetry include terseness, imagery, and parallelism.

**Q:** How can understanding the poetry/prose continuum help us identify text as prose or poetry?

**A:** On a continuum of the number of poetic elements, poetry is on one end and prose on the other. Poetry has a great number of poetic elements; prose does not. Texts can be evaluated by the amount of such elements to determine whether they are mainly poetry or prose.

#### 1.2.2 — Identify characteristics of Hebrew poetry.

Understanding genre is important no matter what we read. Genre describes a type of literature that has a particular style, form, or content. For instance, when we flip through the pages of a newspaper, we expect to find unbiased, factual information about current events. When we look for a number in a telephone book, we know the columns of information on the pages are lists of the people in the area and their addresses and telephone numbers. When we open a novel, we know that the plot is fiction, and when we read a poem, we recognize the language as being metaphorical.

When we study the Poetic Books of the Bible, we need to be aware of the genres they represent. All of the books of Job, Psalms, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and Song of Solomon are poetry or contain poetry. Therefore, we must read and interpret what they are trying to say in light of their poetic style or form.

Characteristics of Poetry

What is the difference between poetry and prose? Prose is “ordinary” writing similar to everyday speech. In contrast, poetry is terse (short, concise, no unnecessary words) and imaginative and usually has a regular rhythm.

In many languages, such as English, French, Latin, and Greek, poetry has uniform meter and often rhyme. Each line of such poetry has a regular number of stressed and unstressed syllables. Additionally, the words at the ends of lines tend to rhyme in regular patterns. Indeed, meter and rhyme traditionally have been two of the most distinguishing characteristics of English poetry.

Hebrew poetry, on the other hand, does not generally use rhyme for poetic effect. Although not altogether absent, meter is also apparently not essential to Hebrew poetry. For years, scholars have attempted to discover the key to metrical patterns in Hebrew poetry, but “in our judgment, however, no attempt . . . to identify the presence of something like classic Greek metric forms in Hebrew poetry has proved successful” (Petersen and Richards 1992, 39). In fact, many scholars now contend that meter is not a characteristic of Hebrew poetry at all.

If Hebrew poetry has neither rhyme nor meter, how can we be sure a particular text is poetry? The answer is not exactly black and white; some degree of uncertainty exists. The manuscripts on which our modern Hebrew texts and English translations are based were not written in verse, so a person cannot tell the difference between poetry and prose in these texts just by looking at them. At times, such as in Isaiah 4:2, scholars disagree about whether the text is poetry or prose. Thus, those who edit our contemporary English translations make decisions as to which portions of the text are poetry and which are prose. The scholars who produce the translations separate poetry from prose by looking for elements that are characteristic of poetry.

Sections of poetry are easy to spot in these translations because they are set in verse. The lines are of roughly equal length, and they are not organized into paragraphs as are sections of prose. The first English translation to set all the poetic portions of the Bible in verse was the Revised Standard Version of 1952.

Besides verse format, poetry has other identifying characteristics. One trait of Hebrew poetry is terseness. That is, Hebrew poetry is compact and uses only words that are necessary. For example, Exodus 15:3 reads simply, “The Lord is a warrior; the Lord is his name.” The original Hebrew is even more terse than the English translation because it has no verb.

Another element of Hebrew poetry is its use of vivid imagery . Tremper Longman defines an image as a “sensation evoked in the mind of the reader by the language of a text” (1988, 128–129). Psalm 104:3 describes God’s greatness with striking imagery: “He makes the clouds his chariot and rides on the wings of the wind.” In another psalm, the writer uses the pleasant sensation of taste to describe how desirable the Lord is, saying, “Taste and see that the Lord is good” (Psalm 34:8).

Another important characteristic of Hebrew poetry is parallelism . Parallelism uses two lines to present and then develop an image. It may repeat a thought or the way the lines are structured. Sometimes the correlation between the lines is so close that the second line seems merely to paraphrase the first. Psalm 1:1 contains an example of parallelism that involves three lines:

Who does not walk in the counsel of the wicked or stand in the way of sinners or sit in the seat of mockers.

Each of these lines says essentially the same thing using a slightly different nuance. We will discuss more about parallelism in the next lesson.

The Poetry/Prose Continuum

Although terseness, imagery, and parallelism are characteristics of Hebrew poetry, the presence of these and other poetic devices in a text does not necessarily make the text poetry. Conversely, the absence of one or more of these elements does not necessarily mean the text is not poetry. Rather, poetry tends to have more of these elements than does prose. In other words, if a text is terse, has a great deal of imagery, and uses a lot of parallelism, it is probably poetry. If the text is wordy, void of imagery, and without parallelism, it is probably prose.

We can think of poetry and prose as being on the ends of a continuum. Poetry has a great number of poetic elements, while prose does not. Texts with fewer poetic elements are difficult to categorize and may or may not be poetry.

**Q:** Why is understanding literary genre foundational to interpreting the Poetic Books?

**A:** When we understand the genre of what we are reading, we know what to expect in its style, form, and content. Because the Poetic Books are written in the style of ancient Hebrew poetry, we will understand the writers’ use of such devices as repetition and parallelism and expect to have ideas conveyed through imagery and patterns.

**Q:** How do English and Hebrew poetry differ?

**A:** English poetry is often characterized by uniform meter and rhyme. Hebrew poetry, on the other hand, does not generally use rhyme and meter for poetic effect. Elements characteristic of Hebrew poetry include terseness, imagery, and parallelism.

**Q:** How can understanding the poetry/prose continuum help us identify text as prose or poetry?

**A:** On a continuum of the number of poetic elements, poetry is on one end and prose on the other. Poetry has a great number of poetic elements; prose does not. Texts can be evaluated by the amount of such elements to determine whether they are mainly poetry or prose.

### Recognizing Poetic Devices

#### 1.3.1 — Describe poetic devices of Hebrew poetry.

Praise the Lord.

Praise God in his sanctuary; praise him in his mighty heavens. Praise him for his acts of power; praise him for his surpassing greatness. Praise him with the sounding of the trumpet, praise him with the harp and lyre, praise him with tambourine and dancing, praise him with the strings and flute, praise him with the clash of cymbals, praise him with resounding cymbals.

Let everything that has breath praise the Lord.

Praise the Lord. (Psalm 150)

What a resounding call to praise God! This wonderful psalm closes the Psalter (book of Psalms) on a note of exhortation to praise the Lord with every possible means. It is a beautiful poem; it is also very repetitious.

Repetition

Repetition is a prominent characteristic of Hebrew literature. We can find a good example in Numbers 7:12–83, which includes a tedious repetition of the offerings that representatives of each of Israel’s twelve tribes brought to the tabernacle. The list is repeated almost verbatim over and over and over. In English literature, this would be considered redundant and nonpoetic, but such repetition is common in the Old Testament.

It is not surprising, therefore, that repetition is common in Hebrew poetry. For instance, as seen at the beginning of this lesson, Psalm 150 begins with the call to “Praise the Lord” (v. 1). The verb praise occurs thirteen times in this short psalm, and the psalm ends with the same statement: “Praise the Lord.” This repetition is a poetic call to praise that brings the book of Psalms to a fitting conclusion. This bracketing of a poem by beginning and ending it with a similar word, phrase, or clause is a poetic device called inclusio (sometimes called inclusion ).

Sometimes the repeated words vary. For example, the second part of Psalm 70:1 reads:

“O Lord, come quickly to help me.”

The second part of the last verse of the psalm begins,

“Come quickly to me, O God.” (70:5)

The title “God” in verse 5 replaces the name “Lord” used in verse 1. Such variation is common and adds to the beauty of the poem.

Parallelism

Repetition is common to Hebrew poetry. In fact, a certain kind of repetition, parallelism, has often been upheld as the most important characteristic of Hebrew poetry. This view somewhat overstates the importance of parallelism; however, the poetic device is found in abundance throughout the Poetic Books. Still, while parallelism is not the only or most important characteristic of Hebrew poetry, it is so crucial to the genre that every serious student of the Poetic Books must understand it and how it affects the way biblical poetry is interpreted.

Parallelism is the relationship between two phrases of a poetic line or lines. A poetic phrase, also known as a colon (plural: cola), is the building block of a poem. It is the smallest poetic unit that makes up a line. A line of poetry can have one or more phrases. Psalm 1:2, for example, has two such phrases: “But his delight is in the law of the Lord, and on his law he meditates day and night.” Parallelism describes the relationship between the phrases in a line. Hebrew authors made use of several types of parallelism, four of which were more common: (1) synonymous, (2) antithetic, (3) synthetic, and (4) emblematic.

Synonymous Parallelism

Synonyms are two or more words that have the same, or nearly the same, meaning. The words pastor and shepherd can be synonyms, as can trust and believe . The Poetic Books make use of many poetic synonyms, both as single words and as phrases. An example of this is found in Job 5:2:

Resentment kills a fool , and envy slays the simple .

The underlined words in the first line mean the same as underlined words in the second line. In Job 38:7, “angels” in the second line echoes the meaning of “morning stars” in the first line.

While the morning stars sang together and all the angels shouted for joy?

Also, note that “sang together” means about the same as “shouted for joy.” These are parallel synonyms or similar ways of saying the same thing. Thus, in synonymous parallelism , the same thought is repeated using two closely related but slightly different words or phrases.

Antithetic Parallelism

The antithesis of something is its opposite. The prefix anti means “against.” The antichrist is against Christ; they are opposites. Hatred is the antithesis of love.

In antithetic parallelism , then, the second idea presented is the opposite of the first. For example, the writer of Proverbs contrasts righteousness and sin in the following passage.

Righteousness exalts a nation, but sin is a disgrace to any people. (Proverbs 14:34)

Synthetic Parallelism

In this type of poetry, also known as completion parallelism, one line builds on the previous line. To synthesize means to blend together as a whole. In synthetic parallelism , all of the lines together give the whole thought or big picture. Consider this example:

He is like a tree planted by streams of water, which yields its fruit in season and whose leaf does not wither. Whatever he does prospers. (Psalm 1:3)

Emblematic Parallelism

Emblematic parallelism is a type of synonymous parallelism in which one line contains a simile or metaphor . A simile is a comparison using the word like or as . A metaphor is a word or phrase, meaning an object or idea, that is used in place of another to suggest they are similar. An example is found in Job 5:7 and 5:26:

Yet man is born to trouble as surely as sparks fly upward. (5:7)

You will come to the grave in full vigor, like sheaves gathered in season. (5:26)

Chiasm

In the poetic device known as chiasm , the order of a line is reversed in the following line. If a diagram were made of a chiasm, it would resemble the letter x . Psalm 2:5, for example, would read:

Then he rebukes them in his anger (verb, direct object) (prepositional phrase)

and in his wrath he terrifies them (prepositional phrase) (verb, direct object)

Chiasm can be difficult to see in English translations, however, because they are often glossed over in translation. For instance, in the New International Version, Psalm 2:5 does not demonstrate the chiasm found in the Hebrew text. The NIV reads, “Then he rebukes them in his anger and terrifies them in his wrath.”

Some translations are more true to the syntax of the original languages, Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek. Yet this does not make them more correct. English syntax is considerably different from Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek syntax. The word order is generally different, and the way ideas are expressed is different. For example, in Hebrew the subject generally comes after the verb, whereas English word order usually places the subject before the verb. Also, “a person became angry” in English is expressed “a person’s nose grew hot” in Hebrew. “The middle of the day” in English is expressed “the bone of the day” in Hebrew.

Because of these differences, translators must decide whether to follow the conventions of the source languages (Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek) or those of English. Following the conventions of the source language results in what is called a literal translation , such as the King James Version and the New American Standard Bible. Following English conventions is known as dynamic equivalence , of which the New International Version is an example. Many students of the Bible prefer a literal translation for close study of a biblical passage and a dynamic equivalence translation for daily Bible reading.

**Q:** How important is parallelism to Hebrew poetry?

**A:** Parallelism has often been upheld as the defining characteristic of Hebrew poetry. It is of such importance that every serious student of the Poetic Books must understand parallelism and how it affects interpretation of biblical poetry.

**Q:** Why is chiasm easy to identify in some English translations but not in others?

**A:** Languages have great differences, including grammar, sentence structure, and the manner in which ideas are expressed. Translators must decide which rules of language to follow, whether Hebrew or English. This affects word choices and the arrangement of words in a sentence. Depending on the translator’s choices, poetic devices may or may not come through intact. 2 CHAPTER

He is like a tree planted by streams of water, which yields its fruit in season and whose leaf does not wither. Whatever he does prospers. (Psalm 1:3)

Emblematic Parallelism

Emblematic parallelism is a type of synonymous parallelism in which one line contains a simile or metaphor . A simile is a comparison using the word like or as . A metaphor is a word or phrase, meaning an object or idea, that is used in place of another to suggest they are similar. An example is found in Job 5:7 and 5:26:

Yet man is born to trouble as surely as sparks fly upward. (5:7)

You will come to the grave in full vigor, like sheaves gathered in season. (5:26)

Chiasm

In the poetic device known as chiasm , the order of a line is reversed in the following line. If a diagram were made of a chiasm, it would resemble the letter x . Psalm 2:5, for example, would read:

Then he rebukes them in his anger (verb, direct object) (prepositional phrase)

and in his wrath he terrifies them (prepositional phrase) (verb, direct object)

Chiasm can be difficult to see in English translations, however, because they are often glossed over in translation. For instance, in the New International Version, Psalm 2:5 does not demonstrate the chiasm found in the Hebrew text. The NIV reads, “Then he rebukes them in his anger and terrifies them in his wrath.”

Some translations are more true to the syntax of the original languages, Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek. Yet this does not make them more correct. English syntax is considerably different from Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek syntax. The word order is generally different, and the way ideas are expressed is different. For example, in Hebrew the subject generally comes after the verb, whereas English word order usually places the subject before the verb. Also, “a person became angry” in English is expressed “a person’s nose grew hot” in Hebrew. “The middle of the day” in English is expressed “the bone of the day” in Hebrew.

Because of these differences, translators must decide whether to follow the conventions of the source languages (Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek) or those of English. Following the conventions of the source language results in what is called a literal translation , such as the King James Version and the New American Standard Bible. Following English conventions is known as dynamic equivalence , of which the New International Version is an example. Many students of the Bible prefer a literal translation for close study of a biblical passage and a dynamic equivalence translation for daily Bible reading.

**Q:** How important is parallelism to Hebrew poetry?

**A:** Parallelism has often been upheld as the defining characteristic of Hebrew poetry. It is of such importance that every serious student of the Poetic Books must understand parallelism and how it affects interpretation of biblical poetry.

**Q:** Why is chiasm easy to identify in some English translations but not in others?

**A:** Languages have great differences, including grammar, sentence structure, and the manner in which ideas are expressed. Translators must decide which rules of language to follow, whether Hebrew or English. This affects word choices and the arrangement of words in a sentence. Depending on the translator’s choices, poetic devices may or may not come through intact. 2 CHAPTER

#### 1.3.4 — Explain the challenges of translating chiasm.

Praise the Lord.

Praise God in his sanctuary; praise him in his mighty heavens. Praise him for his acts of power; praise him for his surpassing greatness. Praise him with the sounding of the trumpet, praise him with the harp and lyre, praise him with tambourine and dancing, praise him with the strings and flute, praise him with the clash of cymbals, praise him with resounding cymbals.

Let everything that has breath praise the Lord.

Praise the Lord. (Psalm 150)

What a resounding call to praise God! This wonderful psalm closes the Psalter (book of Psalms) on a note of exhortation to praise the Lord with every possible means. It is a beautiful poem; it is also very repetitious.

Repetition

Repetition is a prominent characteristic of Hebrew literature. We can find a good example in Numbers 7:12–83, which includes a tedious repetition of the offerings that representatives of each of Israel’s twelve tribes brought to the tabernacle. The list is repeated almost verbatim over and over and over. In English literature, this would be considered redundant and nonpoetic, but such repetition is common in the Old Testament.

It is not surprising, therefore, that repetition is common in Hebrew poetry. For instance, as seen at the beginning of this lesson, Psalm 150 begins with the call to “Praise the Lord” (v. 1). The verb praise occurs thirteen times in this short psalm, and the psalm ends with the same statement: “Praise the Lord.” This repetition is a poetic call to praise that brings the book of Psalms to a fitting conclusion. This bracketing of a poem by beginning and ending it with a similar word, phrase, or clause is a poetic device called inclusio (sometimes called inclusion ).

Sometimes the repeated words vary. For example, the second part of Psalm 70:1 reads:

“O Lord, come quickly to help me.”

The second part of the last verse of the psalm begins,

“Come quickly to me, O God.” (70:5)

The title “God” in verse 5 replaces the name “Lord” used in verse 1. Such variation is common and adds to the beauty of the poem.

Parallelism

Repetition is common to Hebrew poetry. In fact, a certain kind of repetition, parallelism, has often been upheld as the most important characteristic of Hebrew poetry. This view somewhat overstates the importance of parallelism; however, the poetic device is found in abundance throughout the Poetic Books. Still, while parallelism is not the only or most important characteristic of Hebrew poetry, it is so crucial to the genre that every serious student of the Poetic Books must understand it and how it affects the way biblical poetry is interpreted.

Parallelism is the relationship between two phrases of a poetic line or lines. A poetic phrase, also known as a colon (plural: cola), is the building block of a poem. It is the smallest poetic unit that makes up a line. A line of poetry can have one or more phrases. Psalm 1:2, for example, has two such phrases: “But his delight is in the law of the Lord, and on his law he meditates day and night.” Parallelism describes the relationship between the phrases in a line. Hebrew authors made use of several types of parallelism, four of which were more common: (1) synonymous, (2) antithetic, (3) synthetic, and (4) emblematic.

Synonymous Parallelism

Synonyms are two or more words that have the same, or nearly the same, meaning. The words pastor and shepherd can be synonyms, as can trust and believe . The Poetic Books make use of many poetic synonyms, both as single words and as phrases. An example of this is found in Job 5:2:

Resentment kills a fool , and envy slays the simple .

The underlined words in the first line mean the same as underlined words in the second line. In Job 38:7, “angels” in the second line echoes the meaning of “morning stars” in the first line.

While the morning stars sang together and all the angels shouted for joy?

Also, note that “sang together” means about the same as “shouted for joy.” These are parallel synonyms or similar ways of saying the same thing. Thus, in synonymous parallelism , the same thought is repeated using two closely related but slightly different words or phrases.

Antithetic Parallelism

The antithesis of something is its opposite. The prefix anti means “against.” The antichrist is against Christ; they are opposites. Hatred is the antithesis of love.

In antithetic parallelism , then, the second idea presented is the opposite of the first. For example, the writer of Proverbs contrasts righteousness and sin in the following passage.

Righteousness exalts a nation, but sin is a disgrace to any people. (Proverbs 14:34)

Synthetic Parallelism

In this type of poetry, also known as completion parallelism, one line builds on the previous line. To synthesize means to blend together as a whole. In synthetic parallelism , all of the lines together give the whole thought or big picture. Consider this example:

He is like a tree planted by streams of water, which yields its fruit in season and whose leaf does not wither. Whatever he does prospers. (Psalm 1:3)

Emblematic Parallelism

Emblematic parallelism is a type of synonymous parallelism in which one line contains a simile or metaphor . A simile is a comparison using the word like or as . A metaphor is a word or phrase, meaning an object or idea, that is used in place of another to suggest they are similar. An example is found in Job 5:7 and 5:26:

Yet man is born to trouble as surely as sparks fly upward. (5:7)

You will come to the grave in full vigor, like sheaves gathered in season. (5:26)

Chiasm

In the poetic device known as chiasm , the order of a line is reversed in the following line. If a diagram were made of a chiasm, it would resemble the letter x . Psalm 2:5, for example, would read:

Then he rebukes them in his anger (verb, direct object) (prepositional phrase)

and in his wrath he terrifies them (prepositional phrase) (verb, direct object)

Chiasm can be difficult to see in English translations, however, because they are often glossed over in translation. For instance, in the New International Version, Psalm 2:5 does not demonstrate the chiasm found in the Hebrew text. The NIV reads, “Then he rebukes them in his anger and terrifies them in his wrath.”

Some translations are more true to the syntax of the original languages, Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek. Yet this does not make them more correct. English syntax is considerably different from Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek syntax. The word order is generally different, and the way ideas are expressed is different. For example, in Hebrew the subject generally comes after the verb, whereas English word order usually places the subject before the verb. Also, “a person became angry” in English is expressed “a person’s nose grew hot” in Hebrew. “The middle of the day” in English is expressed “the bone of the day” in Hebrew.

Because of these differences, translators must decide whether to follow the conventions of the source languages (Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek) or those of English. Following the conventions of the source language results in what is called a literal translation , such as the King James Version and the New American Standard Bible. Following English conventions is known as dynamic equivalence , of which the New International Version is an example. Many students of the Bible prefer a literal translation for close study of a biblical passage and a dynamic equivalence translation for daily Bible reading.

**Q:** How important is parallelism to Hebrew poetry?

**A:** Parallelism has often been upheld as the defining characteristic of Hebrew poetry. It is of such importance that every serious student of the Poetic Books must understand parallelism and how it affects interpretation of biblical poetry.

**Q:** Why is chiasm easy to identify in some English translations but not in others?

**A:** Languages have great differences, including grammar, sentence structure, and the manner in which ideas are expressed. Translators must decide which rules of language to follow, whether Hebrew or English. This affects word choices and the arrangement of words in a sentence. Depending on the translator’s choices, poetic devices may or may not come through intact. 2 CHAPTER

## 2 - Wisdom in Poetry

### Introduction to Wisdom

#### 2.1.1 — Explain why wisdom literature is valuable for believers.

Why Study Wisdom Literature?

Why should Christians study wisdom writings? The basic answer is that the writings discuss living a full and happy life. Wisdom writers promote life. They take for granted a central truth that is often overlooked in western societies: life is lived and happiness is found (or lost) in the context of community and our relationships with others. Central to our lives are our relationships, both with the people around us and with God. More than any other part of the Bible, wisdom writings focus on trying to live out biblical faith in daily life. We can summarize, then, by saying, “Wisdom teaches us to handle our relationships in such a way as to promote harmony, contentment, and fullness of life.”

The study of wisdom is also important for the Christian because it was important for Jesus and other writers of the New Testament. Some believers tend to assume that the New Testament has replaced the Old and that the Gospels and Epistles contain all we need to live as Christians. Nothing could be further from the truth. This view presents at least two problems when it comes to the Old Testament in general and wisdom writings in particular.

First, the New Testament writers looked at the Old Testament as God’s Word to them. For Jesus, the writers of the New Testament, and the first-century church, the Old Testament was the Bible. The writings that would eventually become the New Testament were built on the foundation of Old Testament Scriptures. Because of this, numerous truths God revealed in the Old Testament receive either less attention in the New Testament or are left out entirely. We could no more replace the Old Testament with the New than we could remove the lower rungs of a ladder as we climb higher on it and still have a fully functional tool. Much of what it means to be a Christian and to live a Christ-centered life is found primarily in the wisdom writings.

Second, New Testament writers used wisdom frequently in communicating their messages. This is particularly true of Jesus, who adopted the style and form of wisdom teaching in His own teaching. He often used proverbs (short, self-contained sayings) in the Gospels, such as “For where your treasure is, there your heart will be also” (Matthew 6:21), and “All who draw the sword will die by the sword” (26:52). Robert Stein has made an extensive study of Jesus’ methods of teaching and concludes, “The evidence in the Gospels that Jesus taught as a wise man . . . is impressive. His abundant use of proverbs, parables, paradox, metaphor, and so forth witnesses to a similarity between the form of His teachings and that of other wise men” (1994, 2).

A third reason the study of wisdom is important is that it has immediate, practical use in our lives. Wisdom writings talk about ordinary living. They speak of work, play, love, marriage, raising children, aging, dying, and many other important aspects of daily life. Wisdom literature also explores a number of vitally important life questions that receive little or no attention in the New Testament. The wisdom writers explored the ambiguity and uncertainty of life. While the writers of Proverbs describe the prosperity of living a godly life and the Song of Solomon celebrates both human and divine love, Job and Ecclesiastes expose us to life’s inconsistencies and inequities. Such questions as the suffering of the innocent, the justice of God, and the meaning of sickness and death are met head-on by these writers. Other topics, such as the sacredness of human sexuality and the glory of God’s creation, are most fully thought out and discussed in the wisdom writings.

What Is Wisdom?

Biblical wisdom is the place where, more than anywhere else, the Bible meets the world. In some ways, wisdom writings are the most multicultural portions of the Old Testament. For instance, proverbs that give advice about common problems and make observations on shared experiences can be found the world over. Consider the following sample of proverbs taken from cultures around the world, and notice how they are practical and easily understandable:

When there is more than enough, more than enough is wasted. (Bantu. Among the Bantu in Africa, such proverbs are used in judicial proceedings and sometimes the first half of a riddle is given as a greeting and the answer in parting.)

Keep five yards from a carriage, ten yards from a horse, a hundred yards from an elephant; but the distance one should keep from a wicked man cannot be measured. (India)

Until the lions have their historian, tales of the hunt shall always glorify the hunter. (Azerbaijan)

Between two friends even water drunk together is sweet enough. (Zimbabwe)

Keep your shop and your shop will keep you. (Jamaica)

Everyone is the age of their heart. (Guatemala) (Crenshaw 1981, 13)

American culture also has its wisdom tradition. Benjamin Franklin, one of the U.S. founding fathers, published Poor Richard’s Almanac, which included many succinct wisdom sayings. Poor Richard says: “Early to bed and early to rise makes a man healthy, wealthy, and wise”; “Fish and visitors stink after three days”; “Work as if you were to live one hundred years; pray as if you were to die tomorrow”; and “He that lies down with dogs shall rise up with fleas.”

Biblical wisdom speaks of daily, common experiences that a person can see and understand whether or not he or she is particularly spiritual, or even necessarily Christian. For example, when we read Proverbs 10:4, “Lazy hands make a man poor, but diligent hands bring wealth,” or Proverbs 15:17, “Better a meal of vegetables where there is love than a fattened calf with hatred,” we hear truths that are just as valid for the believer as for the nonbeliever. They hold true in every culture and context throughout the world at any point in history.

The subject matter of wisdom writing is very different from the rest of the Old Testament. Important themes found throughout the rest of the Old Testament, such as covenant , the temple, the nation of Israel, the Exodus, Davidic kingship, and worship, are virtually absent from the wisdom writings. Wisdom speaks of the natural world and of things anyone can observe and find to be true and does not require special revelation to be understood.

The location of biblical wisdom is the real world of ordinary people. Much of the rest of the Old Testament is set in temples and palaces and has to do with prophets, priests, and kings. But wisdom finds its home among ordinary people in home and field and street. Kathleen O’Connor calls biblical wisdom writing “spirituality for the marketplace,” and asserts that it performs two functions: “To uncover the sacred in life’s confusion and ambiguity, and to make possible prayer and praise in the tumbling profusion of daily existence” (1993, 14). Biblical wisdom assumes that God is found not only in the temple but also in our homes, schools, and places of work.

The Hebrew word translated “wisdom” has a somewhat broader sense than the English equivalent. It includes a wide range of social and ethical issues that distinguish the wise from the fool. It includes special skills and abilities, education, discretion, morality, and faithfulness. For instance, skilled craftsmen were said to be wise:

Then wrought Bezaleel and Aholiab, and every wise hearted man, in whom the Lord put wisdom and understanding to know how to work all manner of work for the service of the sanctuary, according to all that the Lord had commanded. And Moses called Bezaleel and Aholiab, and every wise hearted man, in whose heart the Lord had put wisdom, even every one whose heart stirred him up to come unto the work to do it. (Exodus 36:1–2, KJV)

Furthermore, wisdom is only wise when put into use. Someone who possesses the correct knowledge or a special skill but does not act on it is considered a fool. Thus, the ideas of shrewdness and prudence are part of wisdom. 2 Samuel 20:14–22 tells the story of a certain “wise woman,” who through her understanding and shrewdness was able to save a city from destruction. When Joseph revealed the meaning of Pharaoh’s dream in Genesis 41, Pharaoh called him “discerning and wise” and placed him in charge of all of Egypt. Wisdom can be defined as “knowledge and skill put to good use through wise action.”

**Q:** How do wisdom writings benefit believers?

**A:** Wisdom writings promote life by offering ways to live healthfully in the context of community and our relationships with others and God. They focus on trying to live out biblical faith in daily life. Wisdom teaches how to handle relationships in ways that promote harmony, contentment, and fullness of life.

**Q:** What makes wisdom writings relevant to all people?

**A:** Wisdom is where the Bible meets the world. It speaks of daily, common experiences that we do not have to be particularly spiritual, or even necessarily Christian, to see and understand. Wisdom speaks of the natural world and of things anyone can observe and find to be true; it does not require special revelation to be understood. Wisdom finds its home among ordinary people in home, field, and street.
