### Wisdom’s Form and Function

#### 2.2.1 — Identify the two forms of wisdom literature, and describe wisdom’s content.

What Wisdom Looks Like

Wisdom literature can be identified and classified in two ways: by form and by content.

Form

Form is the structure, method, or style used to present wisdom concepts. Wisdom literature of the Old Testament exists in two basic forms: (1) proverbial wisdom and (2) contemplative (sometimes called speculative) wisdom.

Proverbial wisdom consists of short, concise sayings that give guidelines for happy and prosperous living, such as, “A prudent man sees danger and takes refuge, but the simple keep going and suffer for it” (Proverbs 22:3).

Contemplative wisdom consists of longer writings, often written as narratives or dialogues, that present a basic teaching: “Does not wisdom call out? Does not understanding raise her voice? On the heights along the way, where the paths meet, she takes her stand” (Proverbs 8:1–2). Proverbs 31, which describes the traits of a virtuous woman, is an example of contemplative wisdom.

Another example is the American legend of George Washington and a cherry tree. Anxious to try out his new hatchet, the future first president of the United States allegedly chopped down his father’s favorite cherry tree. When his father asked who had destroyed the tree, little George responded, “I cannot tell a lie. I did it with my hatchet.” Whether this story is true or not, it and others like it are the basis for a strong emphasis in American culture on honesty and telling the literal truth.

Content

As previously stated, wisdom writing is also distinct because of its content. Wisdom focuses on themes that are important for living successfully in community, such as choosing between right and wrong, establishing relationships, developing virtue versus wickedness, handling wealth and power, or showing mercy to the poor.

While we can find wisdom sayings throughout Scripture, some books of the Bible contain a great deal of wisdom literature and are thus classified as wisdom books. The best examples of this occur in Job, Proverbs, and Ecclesiastes. In addition, many of the psalms have been strongly influenced by the wisdom tradition and reflect important wisdom themes. Wisdom themes are also present in the Song of Solomon.

The purpose of wisdom sayings is educational. Throughout the ancient world, adults commonly used wisdom sayings to prepare the youth to assume responsibility and positions of leadership in society. For example, Proverbs 1:8 states, “Listen, my son, to your father’s instruction and do not forsake your mother’s teaching” (1:8), and the last chapter of the book is introduced as “the sayings of King Lemuel—an oracle his mother taught him” (31:1). In Psalms we read, “Come, my children, listen to me; I will teach you the fear of the Lord” (34:11). Ecclesiastes opens with, “The words of the Teacher” (1:1).

In this way, wisdom teaching does not simply pass on information but includes moral and ethical issues to help a child take his or her place in society. It is not just about individual happiness; instead, it recognizes the need for society to function smoothly so all its members have opportunity for a full life.

Solomon was largely responsible for making wisdom an important part of the royal court in Jerusalem, personally writing some three thousand proverbs (1 Kings 4:32). Traditionally, he is credited with writing all or part of three of the wisdom books in the Old Testament: Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and the Song of Solomon.

The Meeting Place of Belief and Experience

Perhaps more than any other part of the Bible, the wisdom books help us recognize and express the possible tension between belief and experience. By belief, we mean those things we have come to believe about God through what we have been taught, what the Bible tells us, and what we have experienced in our own relationship with God. Yet not all experiences confirm our beliefs. Sometimes our experiences seem to directly challenge what we believe to be true about God. On one hand, God reveals Himself as a God of justice and power and makes certain claims about the rewards of obedience and the judgment that results from disobedience. On the other hand, we sometimes experience events that raise questions either about God’s control or about His essential goodness. In the twentieth and early twenty-first centuries, such events as the Holocaust; the wars of genocide in Eastern Europe, Asia, and Africa; famines that have swept through Africa; and terrible tsunamis in the Asian basin raise questions about God’s goodness and/or His dominion in the world.

Old Testament wisdom literature addresses the fundamental debate between our understanding of God’s justice—that is, that He always rewards obedience and punishes disobedience—and experiences that challenge such an understanding of God. Whereas Proverbs and such Psalms as 1, 19, and 119 support the former view, Job, Ecclesiastes, and Psalm 73 directly question and even challenge it. The wisdom writers will neither abandon their belief in God nor gloss over the reality of their experiences when God does not act as His covenant says He will. As we shall see, the wisdom writers use this confrontation between belief and experience to explore and deepen their faith rather than abandon or destroy it.

As the result of wisdom’s bringing together belief and experience, the writers offer us some of the best biblical resources for when we find our own faith challenged by the tragedies and hardships of life. As you continue through the course, pay special attention to the ways the various writers grow in their faith and understanding of God in and through the life experiences that challenge what they believe.

**Q:** How do proverbial wisdom and contemplative wisdom differ?

**A:** Proverbial wisdom consists of short guidelines for happy and prosperous living. One example is Proverbs 22:3: “A prudent man sees danger and takes refuge, but the simple keep going and suffer for it.” In contrast, contemplative wisdom consists of longer writings that often appear in narrative or dialogue form and provide basic teaching. For example, Proverbs 31 describes the traits of a virtuous woman.

**Q:** How can wisdom writing help when we face hardships?

**A:** The wisdom books help us recognize and express the possible tension between things we have come to believe about God and what we experience. The writers offer some of the best biblical resources for when we find our faith challenged by the hardships of life. Through their experiences, we learn and are comforted and guided by the ways they grew in their faith and understanding of God.

#### 2.2.2 — Describe how wisdom writers responded to tension between experience and belief.

What Wisdom Looks Like

Wisdom literature can be identified and classified in two ways: by form and by content.

Form

Form is the structure, method, or style used to present wisdom concepts. Wisdom literature of the Old Testament exists in two basic forms: (1) proverbial wisdom and (2) contemplative (sometimes called speculative) wisdom.

Proverbial wisdom consists of short, concise sayings that give guidelines for happy and prosperous living, such as, “A prudent man sees danger and takes refuge, but the simple keep going and suffer for it” (Proverbs 22:3).

Contemplative wisdom consists of longer writings, often written as narratives or dialogues, that present a basic teaching: “Does not wisdom call out? Does not understanding raise her voice? On the heights along the way, where the paths meet, she takes her stand” (Proverbs 8:1–2). Proverbs 31, which describes the traits of a virtuous woman, is an example of contemplative wisdom.

Another example is the American legend of George Washington and a cherry tree. Anxious to try out his new hatchet, the future first president of the United States allegedly chopped down his father’s favorite cherry tree. When his father asked who had destroyed the tree, little George responded, “I cannot tell a lie. I did it with my hatchet.” Whether this story is true or not, it and others like it are the basis for a strong emphasis in American culture on honesty and telling the literal truth.

Content

As previously stated, wisdom writing is also distinct because of its content. Wisdom focuses on themes that are important for living successfully in community, such as choosing between right and wrong, establishing relationships, developing virtue versus wickedness, handling wealth and power, or showing mercy to the poor.

While we can find wisdom sayings throughout Scripture, some books of the Bible contain a great deal of wisdom literature and are thus classified as wisdom books. The best examples of this occur in Job, Proverbs, and Ecclesiastes. In addition, many of the psalms have been strongly influenced by the wisdom tradition and reflect important wisdom themes. Wisdom themes are also present in the Song of Solomon.

The purpose of wisdom sayings is educational. Throughout the ancient world, adults commonly used wisdom sayings to prepare the youth to assume responsibility and positions of leadership in society. For example, Proverbs 1:8 states, “Listen, my son, to your father’s instruction and do not forsake your mother’s teaching” (1:8), and the last chapter of the book is introduced as “the sayings of King Lemuel—an oracle his mother taught him” (31:1). In Psalms we read, “Come, my children, listen to me; I will teach you the fear of the Lord” (34:11). Ecclesiastes opens with, “The words of the Teacher” (1:1).

In this way, wisdom teaching does not simply pass on information but includes moral and ethical issues to help a child take his or her place in society. It is not just about individual happiness; instead, it recognizes the need for society to function smoothly so all its members have opportunity for a full life.

Solomon was largely responsible for making wisdom an important part of the royal court in Jerusalem, personally writing some three thousand proverbs (1 Kings 4:32). Traditionally, he is credited with writing all or part of three of the wisdom books in the Old Testament: Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and the Song of Solomon.

The Meeting Place of Belief and Experience

Perhaps more than any other part of the Bible, the wisdom books help us recognize and express the possible tension between belief and experience. By belief, we mean those things we have come to believe about God through what we have been taught, what the Bible tells us, and what we have experienced in our own relationship with God. Yet not all experiences confirm our beliefs. Sometimes our experiences seem to directly challenge what we believe to be true about God. On one hand, God reveals Himself as a God of justice and power and makes certain claims about the rewards of obedience and the judgment that results from disobedience. On the other hand, we sometimes experience events that raise questions either about God’s control or about His essential goodness. In the twentieth and early twenty-first centuries, such events as the Holocaust; the wars of genocide in Eastern Europe, Asia, and Africa; famines that have swept through Africa; and terrible tsunamis in the Asian basin raise questions about God’s goodness and/or His dominion in the world.

Old Testament wisdom literature addresses the fundamental debate between our understanding of God’s justice—that is, that He always rewards obedience and punishes disobedience—and experiences that challenge such an understanding of God. Whereas Proverbs and such Psalms as 1, 19, and 119 support the former view, Job, Ecclesiastes, and Psalm 73 directly question and even challenge it. The wisdom writers will neither abandon their belief in God nor gloss over the reality of their experiences when God does not act as His covenant says He will. As we shall see, the wisdom writers use this confrontation between belief and experience to explore and deepen their faith rather than abandon or destroy it.

As the result of wisdom’s bringing together belief and experience, the writers offer us some of the best biblical resources for when we find our own faith challenged by the tragedies and hardships of life. As you continue through the course, pay special attention to the ways the various writers grow in their faith and understanding of God in and through the life experiences that challenge what they believe.

**Q:** How do proverbial wisdom and contemplative wisdom differ?

**A:** Proverbial wisdom consists of short guidelines for happy and prosperous living. One example is Proverbs 22:3: “A prudent man sees danger and takes refuge, but the simple keep going and suffer for it.” In contrast, contemplative wisdom consists of longer writings that often appear in narrative or dialogue form and provide basic teaching. For example, Proverbs 31 describes the traits of a virtuous woman.

**Q:** How can wisdom writing help when we face hardships?

**A:** The wisdom books help us recognize and express the possible tension between things we have come to believe about God and what we experience. The writers offer some of the best biblical resources for when we find our faith challenged by the hardships of life. Through their experiences, we learn and are comforted and guided by the ways they grew in their faith and understanding of God.

### The Wisdom Tradition

#### 2.3.1 — Identify ways wisdom writers emphasize the importance of this world.

This World in Wisdom Tradition

Wisdom literature is often misinterpreted because it seems to contradict a teaching that sees this world as evil and an enemy to faith. It is true that Paul says, “Our citizenship is in heaven,” showing we are strangers in this world (Philippians 3:20). However, he also criticizes those who try to reject the created world, telling Timothy that “everything God created is good, and nothing is to be rejected if it is received with thanksgiving, because it is consecrated by the word of God and prayer” (1 Timothy 4:4–5). Wisdom writing focuses not on escaping the world but on living a godly life in the world. “The wise in Israel characteristically appreciate life, love life, value it, and enjoy it. They appropriate the best learning, newest knowledge, and most ingenious cultural achievements” (O’Connor 1993, 14).

Wisdom also provides a needed counterbalance to a kind of Christian fatalism that says, “Because everything is in God’s hands, there is nothing I can or should do to change my life and the lives of those around me. Everything that happens to me is God’s will.” On the contrary, wisdom affirms that our choices shape our lives. Thus, it is true to say both that our prosperity and happiness come from God and that we have a lot to do with its realization.

The wisdom tradition emphasizes the importance of this world in a number of ways. First, wisdom sees life as a basic meaning of human existence. The goal of much wisdom writing is to guide readers into full, abundant living. An abundant life means not simply length of years but a fullness of emotional, physical, social, and spiritual health. Wisdom seeks the well-being of both the individual and the community as a whole. The wisdom writers did not concern themselves with life in heaven but with life here and now.

Second, the source of life wisdom is found not in a special divine revelation but in common experiences that all can share and examine. No despising of self or of one’s ability to discern the truth occurs. Wisdom’s teachings are attainable by all.

Third, wisdom writings emphasize the world’s importance by assuming that human beings have primary responsibility for their own destinies. Wisdom affirms that people are both able to choose wisely and responsible to do so. This runs counter to theologies that focus on the fallenness and sinfulness of humanity and our incapability of any good act or decision. Instead, wisdom affirms that humans are trusted by God.

Fourth, wisdom affirms that humanity is meant to live in an ordered universe. It is not just the “mighty deeds of God” but our own choices that maintain the order of creation.

Fifth, wisdom celebrates humanity’s dominion over creation. God created all that is, but humanity serves as God’s stewards over creation. An attitude that says, “What does it matter whether we protect the environment and limit our use of its resources? It’s all going to burn up in the judgment anyway!” both abuses our position and ignores our responsibility.

Solomon as Wise Man of Israel

Solomon is the central figure in Israel’s wisdom tradition. As we have seen, he was personally responsible for promoting wisdom in Israel, writing a good deal of wisdom himself. But Solomon’s life serves as an example not only of wisdom but also of folly. Five stories or biblical references create a portrait of Solomon. As we move through these stories, we will see a subtle change take place in Solomon’s life:

• 1 Kings 3:4–9: Solomon has a dream in which God offers him the freedom to choose whatever blessing he wishes. While he can have anything he wants, he chooses to ask for the wisdom to rule effectively. This story emphasizes wisdom as greater than the wealth, fame, and power normally associated with kings.

• 1 Kings 3:16–28: This story demonstrates Solomon’s ability to judge the common people wisely. Here he relies on his knowledge of the maternal instinct to discern which of two women is the mother of a child. He brings out in the women the noblest and basest human attitudes.

• 1 Kings 4:29–34: In this passage, Solomon’s wisdom is compared with that of the world at large, particularly with Egyptian wisdom traditions. He is personally credited with having written three thousand proverbs and fifteen hundred songs. He seems particularly interested in the natural sciences.

• 1 Kings 10:6–13: This story occurs at the peak of Solomon’s wealth and wisdom. For the reader, the Queen of Sheba’s visit to Solomon essentially demonstrates the extent of his greatness. Notice, though, the contrast between Solomon’s humility at the beginning of his life and the self-glorification of this episode.

• Deuteronomy 17:14–20: Although Solomon is not specifically mentioned in this passage, its prohibitions seem tailor-made to describe his reign. In 1 Kings 4:28; 10:28–29, Solomon is said to be an arms merchant. First Kings 11:1–4 says that his marriages served to strengthen political alliances and trade agreements. The person of Solomon models for us obedience, humility, and wisdom on one hand and disobedience, pride, and folly on the other.

Wisdom writing impacts the life of every believer who takes time to read and absorb its messages. In these writings, we often find our own struggles as well as reassurance that we do not walk alone but in community with other believers. Wisdom embraced leads to a full and meaningful life.

Wisdom of Israel’s Neighbors

Israel produced their wisdom writings from within a long, international tradition of wisdom. Wisdom writing dealt with the concrete things of daily life, and its content did not address revelation, monotheism, the Exodus, the Law, and so forth. Thus, Hebrew wisdom (as opposed to other genres of Hebrew writing) was perhaps most free to be compared with the writings of other nations.

The development of a wisdom tradition in Egypt predates Joseph’s arrival. Indeed, Joseph’s position in Pharaoh’s court as an interpreter of dreams and advisor in public policy is similar to the role of a wise man or sage. The Egyptians produced a number of wisdom writings that contain proverbs similar to some of those found in the Old Testament.

Mesopotamian wisdom texts have been found in three civilizations: Sumerian, Babylonian, and Assyrian. The oldest documents come from Sumer and date to around 2500 BC. The Sumerians produced a story similar in some ways to the book of Job. “Man and His God” tells the story of a completely just man who suffers and cries out to his god continually until the god responds, changing his suffering into joy. The Sumerians also wrote proverbial wisdom.

Canaanite wisdom literature is less common and depends, in part, on Mesopotamian influences. The most important Canaanite wisdom text is Ahiqar, the story of a man falsely accused by his nephew and removed from power, only to be restored. His story is accompanied by some one hundred wisdom sayings. Some, like the book of Proverbs, support the office of king (Berry 1995).

Uniqueness of Israel’s Wisdom

In spite of the similarities to the wisdom traditions of surrounding cultures, Israel did more than simply share in the wisdom traditions of her neighbors. For Israel, wisdom came from God. This emphasis on God as the center and source of wisdom is expressed in several different ways:

1. A central emphasis throughout Hebrew wisdom literature is “the fear of the Lord.” The fear of the Lord is described as the beginning of both wisdom (Psalm 111:10; Proverbs 9:10; 15:33) and knowledge (Proverbs 1:7; 2:5). It is also closely tied to the promotion of life (Proverbs 10:27; 14:26–27; 19:23; 22:4).

2. Israel understood God to be the source of wisdom, giving and withholding it as seems best to Him (Job 39:17; Proverbs 2:6; Ecclesiastes 2:26). Job maintains, “To God belong wisdom and power; counsel and understanding are his” (Job 12:13).

3. God’s centrality is seen in the way wisdom addresses His acts of creation. We are told that God created the universe by His wisdom (Psalm 104:24; Proverbs 3:19). In Proverbs 8, wisdom is personified as a woman standing beside God at the beginning of creation. She was the “craftsman at his side” (v. 30) who was “brought forth” and “given birth” as God’s first act of creation (vv. 22–26). The writers of Proverbs are careful not to make wisdom equal with God, for God alone is Creator. Nevertheless, wisdom is God’s first work and the standard for all that follows.

Development of Israel’s Wisdom Tradition

Wisdom probably existed before the monarchy in the form of folk wisdom sayings. These were prevalent in family, clan, and tribe as a way of preserving and passing along basic instruction. Examples of proverbial wisdom that predate Solomon are preserved in Scripture: “He was a mighty hunter before the Lord; that is why it is said, ‘Like Nimrod, a mighty hunter before the Lord’” (Genesis 10:9), and “As the old saying goes, ‘From evildoers come evil deeds’” (1 Samuel 24:13).

With the establishment of a stable monarchy, however, wisdom became far more important, being associated with the nation’s political life. A favorite theme of both Proverbs and the wisdom psalms is the king. Wisdom was offered both to guide the king in carrying out his duties and to teach his people how to act in his presence. It was during this time that wisdom writing reached its most sophisticated form.

Later, the Exile brought with it the destruction of the temple and the loss of the Davidic monarchy. After this, wisdom took on a new and important role in the nation. Ronald Clements (1992) suggests that wisdom literature was uniquely suited to survive the Exile. With time, it easily adapted to meet the changing needs of Israelite society. In this third phase of development, Clements sees wisdom truly coming into its own. With the monarchy lost and the influence of other cultures greatly reduced, the Torah (the five books written by Moses) began to play a more central role in Israel’s life, binding together the dispersed nation and preserving a sense of religious and national identity. Clements argues that wisdom writing helped Israel transition from one form of society, monarchical and temple-centered, to another, which was dispersed and Torah-centered.

Wisdom’s new function in the Diaspora of Israel (Jews who lived outside of Palestine, scattered throughout the ancient world) can be seen in its three primary characteristics: (1) It focused on the individual’s internal reasoning. In the Old Testament, an emphasis on personal happiness and individual life goals is found almost exclusively in the wisdom writings. (2) A rethinking of moral boundaries occurred. It was no longer so easy to distinguish between the Israelite, the neighbor, the sojourner among the people, and the stranger or alien. Neither a double standard nor a strong sense of nationalism existed for Israelites and Gentiles. (3) Wisdom was universal. It functioned equally well in any social, political, or ethical environment. Clements underscores that the wisdom writings are among the most adaptable of the biblical writings, adjusting easily to shifting cultures and social contexts (1992, 13–39).

**Q:** According to wisdom literature, what is the individual’s responsibility in the world?

**A:** Wisdom assumes everyone is able to discern truth and act on it. Human beings have primary responsibility for their own destinies; we are both able to choose wisely and responsible to do so. The “mighty deeds of God” coupled with our own choices maintain the order of creation.

**Q:** How is Solomon’s life both an example of and a contradiction to wisdom?

**A:** Solomon models obedience, humility, and wisdom on one hand and disobedience, pride, and folly on the other. He began strong, but his experiences and position affected his ability to maintain a wise lifestyle. From his experience, we can learn to set clear goals for our lives, regularly check our progress, and be willing to make adjustments to move back into line with wise living.

**Q:** What similar stories are found in the wisdom literature of Israel’s neighbors?

**A:** Egyptian wisdom writings contained proverbs similar to some in the Old Testament. The Sumerians not only produced a story with parallels to the book of Job but also wrote proverbial wisdom. Canaanite wisdom literature includes the story of a man falsely accused by his nephew and removed from power, only to be restored. His story comes with about one hundred wisdom sayings, some of which, like Proverbs, support the office of king.

**Q:** How does Israel’s wisdom emphasize God as the center and source of wisdom?

**A:** For Israel, wisdom came from God. Wisdom literature emphasized “the fear of the Lord,” which is closely tied to the promotion of life. God is considered the source of wisdom; He created the universe by His wisdom. Proverbs emphasizes wisdom as God’s first work and the standard for all that follows.

**Q:** How did the importance of Israel’s wisdom change with the rise and fall of the monarchy?

**A:** Before kings ruled Israel, wisdom existed in the form of folk wisdom sayings. These were common in families, clans, and tribes as a way of passing on basic teaching. When kings began to rule, wisdom became associated with political life. Wisdom guided the king as well as his people. During the reign of the kings, wisdom reached its highest form. After the Exile, wisdom became more important in Israel’s life, binding together the scattered people and preserving a sense of religious and national identity. It helped Israel move from being monarchical and temple-centered to being scattered and centered around the Torah. UNIT 2

When kings began to rule, wisdom became associated with political life. Wisdom guided the king as well as his people. During the reign of the kings, wisdom reached its highest form. After the Exile, wisdom became more important in Israel’s life, binding together the scattered people and preserving a sense of religious and national identity. It helped Israel move from being monarchical and temple-centered to being scattered and centered around the Torah. UNIT 2

#### 2.3.3 — Compare and contrast Israel’s wisdom with that of her neighbors.

This World in Wisdom Tradition

Wisdom literature is often misinterpreted because it seems to contradict a teaching that sees this world as evil and an enemy to faith. It is true that Paul says, “Our citizenship is in heaven,” showing we are strangers in this world (Philippians 3:20). However, he also criticizes those who try to reject the created world, telling Timothy that “everything God created is good, and nothing is to be rejected if it is received with thanksgiving, because it is consecrated by the word of God and prayer” (1 Timothy 4:4–5). Wisdom writing focuses not on escaping the world but on living a godly life in the world. “The wise in Israel characteristically appreciate life, love life, value it, and enjoy it. They appropriate the best learning, newest knowledge, and most ingenious cultural achievements” (O’Connor 1993, 14).

Wisdom also provides a needed counterbalance to a kind of Christian fatalism that says, “Because everything is in God’s hands, there is nothing I can or should do to change my life and the lives of those around me. Everything that happens to me is God’s will.” On the contrary, wisdom affirms that our choices shape our lives. Thus, it is true to say both that our prosperity and happiness come from God and that we have a lot to do with its realization.

The wisdom tradition emphasizes the importance of this world in a number of ways. First, wisdom sees life as a basic meaning of human existence. The goal of much wisdom writing is to guide readers into full, abundant living. An abundant life means not simply length of years but a fullness of emotional, physical, social, and spiritual health. Wisdom seeks the well-being of both the individual and the community as a whole. The wisdom writers did not concern themselves with life in heaven but with life here and now.

Second, the source of life wisdom is found not in a special divine revelation but in common experiences that all can share and examine. No despising of self or of one’s ability to discern the truth occurs. Wisdom’s teachings are attainable by all.

Third, wisdom writings emphasize the world’s importance by assuming that human beings have primary responsibility for their own destinies. Wisdom affirms that people are both able to choose wisely and responsible to do so. This runs counter to theologies that focus on the fallenness and sinfulness of humanity and our incapability of any good act or decision. Instead, wisdom affirms that humans are trusted by God.

Fourth, wisdom affirms that humanity is meant to live in an ordered universe. It is not just the “mighty deeds of God” but our own choices that maintain the order of creation.

Fifth, wisdom celebrates humanity’s dominion over creation. God created all that is, but humanity serves as God’s stewards over creation. An attitude that says, “What does it matter whether we protect the environment and limit our use of its resources? It’s all going to burn up in the judgment anyway!” both abuses our position and ignores our responsibility.

Solomon as Wise Man of Israel

Solomon is the central figure in Israel’s wisdom tradition. As we have seen, he was personally responsible for promoting wisdom in Israel, writing a good deal of wisdom himself. But Solomon’s life serves as an example not only of wisdom but also of folly. Five stories or biblical references create a portrait of Solomon. As we move through these stories, we will see a subtle change take place in Solomon’s life:

• 1 Kings 3:4–9: Solomon has a dream in which God offers him the freedom to choose whatever blessing he wishes. While he can have anything he wants, he chooses to ask for the wisdom to rule effectively. This story emphasizes wisdom as greater than the wealth, fame, and power normally associated with kings.

• 1 Kings 3:16–28: This story demonstrates Solomon’s ability to judge the common people wisely. Here he relies on his knowledge of the maternal instinct to discern which of two women is the mother of a child. He brings out in the women the noblest and basest human attitudes.

• 1 Kings 4:29–34: In this passage, Solomon’s wisdom is compared with that of the world at large, particularly with Egyptian wisdom traditions. He is personally credited with having written three thousand proverbs and fifteen hundred songs. He seems particularly interested in the natural sciences.

• 1 Kings 10:6–13: This story occurs at the peak of Solomon’s wealth and wisdom. For the reader, the Queen of Sheba’s visit to Solomon essentially demonstrates the extent of his greatness. Notice, though, the contrast between Solomon’s humility at the beginning of his life and the self-glorification of this episode.

• Deuteronomy 17:14–20: Although Solomon is not specifically mentioned in this passage, its prohibitions seem tailor-made to describe his reign. In 1 Kings 4:28; 10:28–29, Solomon is said to be an arms merchant. First Kings 11:1–4 says that his marriages served to strengthen political alliances and trade agreements. The person of Solomon models for us obedience, humility, and wisdom on one hand and disobedience, pride, and folly on the other.

Wisdom writing impacts the life of every believer who takes time to read and absorb its messages. In these writings, we often find our own struggles as well as reassurance that we do not walk alone but in community with other believers. Wisdom embraced leads to a full and meaningful life.

Wisdom of Israel’s Neighbors

Israel produced their wisdom writings from within a long, international tradition of wisdom. Wisdom writing dealt with the concrete things of daily life, and its content did not address revelation, monotheism, the Exodus, the Law, and so forth. Thus, Hebrew wisdom (as opposed to other genres of Hebrew writing) was perhaps most free to be compared with the writings of other nations.

The development of a wisdom tradition in Egypt predates Joseph’s arrival. Indeed, Joseph’s position in Pharaoh’s court as an interpreter of dreams and advisor in public policy is similar to the role of a wise man or sage. The Egyptians produced a number of wisdom writings that contain proverbs similar to some of those found in the Old Testament.

Mesopotamian wisdom texts have been found in three civilizations: Sumerian, Babylonian, and Assyrian. The oldest documents come from Sumer and date to around 2500 BC. The Sumerians produced a story similar in some ways to the book of Job. “Man and His God” tells the story of a completely just man who suffers and cries out to his god continually until the god responds, changing his suffering into joy. The Sumerians also wrote proverbial wisdom.

Canaanite wisdom literature is less common and depends, in part, on Mesopotamian influences. The most important Canaanite wisdom text is Ahiqar, the story of a man falsely accused by his nephew and removed from power, only to be restored. His story is accompanied by some one hundred wisdom sayings. Some, like the book of Proverbs, support the office of king (Berry 1995).

Uniqueness of Israel’s Wisdom

In spite of the similarities to the wisdom traditions of surrounding cultures, Israel did more than simply share in the wisdom traditions of her neighbors. For Israel, wisdom came from God. This emphasis on God as the center and source of wisdom is expressed in several different ways:

1. A central emphasis throughout Hebrew wisdom literature is “the fear of the Lord.” The fear of the Lord is described as the beginning of both wisdom (Psalm 111:10; Proverbs 9:10; 15:33) and knowledge (Proverbs 1:7; 2:5). It is also closely tied to the promotion of life (Proverbs 10:27; 14:26–27; 19:23; 22:4).

2. Israel understood God to be the source of wisdom, giving and withholding it as seems best to Him (Job 39:17; Proverbs 2:6; Ecclesiastes 2:26). Job maintains, “To God belong wisdom and power; counsel and understanding are his” (Job 12:13).

3. God’s centrality is seen in the way wisdom addresses His acts of creation. We are told that God created the universe by His wisdom (Psalm 104:24; Proverbs 3:19). In Proverbs 8, wisdom is personified as a woman standing beside God at the beginning of creation. She was the “craftsman at his side” (v. 30) who was “brought forth” and “given birth” as God’s first act of creation (vv. 22–26). The writers of Proverbs are careful not to make wisdom equal with God, for God alone is Creator. Nevertheless, wisdom is God’s first work and the standard for all that follows.

Development of Israel’s Wisdom Tradition

Wisdom probably existed before the monarchy in the form of folk wisdom sayings. These were prevalent in family, clan, and tribe as a way of preserving and passing along basic instruction. Examples of proverbial wisdom that predate Solomon are preserved in Scripture: “He was a mighty hunter before the Lord; that is why it is said, ‘Like Nimrod, a mighty hunter before the Lord’” (Genesis 10:9), and “As the old saying goes, ‘From evildoers come evil deeds’” (1 Samuel 24:13).

With the establishment of a stable monarchy, however, wisdom became far more important, being associated with the nation’s political life. A favorite theme of both Proverbs and the wisdom psalms is the king. Wisdom was offered both to guide the king in carrying out his duties and to teach his people how to act in his presence. It was during this time that wisdom writing reached its most sophisticated form.

Later, the Exile brought with it the destruction of the temple and the loss of the Davidic monarchy. After this, wisdom took on a new and important role in the nation. Ronald Clements (1992) suggests that wisdom literature was uniquely suited to survive the Exile. With time, it easily adapted to meet the changing needs of Israelite society. In this third phase of development, Clements sees wisdom truly coming into its own. With the monarchy lost and the influence of other cultures greatly reduced, the Torah (the five books written by Moses) began to play a more central role in Israel’s life, binding together the dispersed nation and preserving a sense of religious and national identity. Clements argues that wisdom writing helped Israel transition from one form of society, monarchical and temple-centered, to another, which was dispersed and Torah-centered.

Wisdom’s new function in the Diaspora of Israel (Jews who lived outside of Palestine, scattered throughout the ancient world) can be seen in its three primary characteristics: (1) It focused on the individual’s internal reasoning. In the Old Testament, an emphasis on personal happiness and individual life goals is found almost exclusively in the wisdom writings. (2) A rethinking of moral boundaries occurred. It was no longer so easy to distinguish between the Israelite, the neighbor, the sojourner among the people, and the stranger or alien. Neither a double standard nor a strong sense of nationalism existed for Israelites and Gentiles. (3) Wisdom was universal. It functioned equally well in any social, political, or ethical environment. Clements underscores that the wisdom writings are among the most adaptable of the biblical writings, adjusting easily to shifting cultures and social contexts (1992, 13–39).

**Q:** According to wisdom literature, what is the individual’s responsibility in the world?

**A:** Wisdom assumes everyone is able to discern truth and act on it. Human beings have primary responsibility for their own destinies; we are both able to choose wisely and responsible to do so. The “mighty deeds of God” coupled with our own choices maintain the order of creation.

**Q:** How is Solomon’s life both an example of and a contradiction to wisdom?

**A:** Solomon models obedience, humility, and wisdom on one hand and disobedience, pride, and folly on the other. He began strong, but his experiences and position affected his ability to maintain a wise lifestyle. From his experience, we can learn to set clear goals for our lives, regularly check our progress, and be willing to make adjustments to move back into line with wise living.

**Q:** What similar stories are found in the wisdom literature of Israel’s neighbors?

**A:** Egyptian wisdom writings contained proverbs similar to some in the Old Testament. The Sumerians not only produced a story with parallels to the book of Job but also wrote proverbial wisdom. Canaanite wisdom literature includes the story of a man falsely accused by his nephew and removed from power, only to be restored. His story comes with about one hundred wisdom sayings, some of which, like Proverbs, support the office of king.

**Q:** How does Israel’s wisdom emphasize God as the center and source of wisdom?

**A:** For Israel, wisdom came from God. Wisdom literature emphasized “the fear of the Lord,” which is closely tied to the promotion of life. God is considered the source of wisdom; He created the universe by His wisdom. Proverbs emphasizes wisdom as God’s first work and the standard for all that follows.

**Q:** How did the importance of Israel’s wisdom change with the rise and fall of the monarchy?

**A:** Before kings ruled Israel, wisdom existed in the form of folk wisdom sayings. These were common in families, clans, and tribes as a way of passing on basic teaching. When kings began to rule, wisdom became associated with political life. Wisdom guided the king as well as his people. During the reign of the kings, wisdom reached its highest form. After the Exile, wisdom became more important in Israel’s life, binding together the scattered people and preserving a sense of religious and national identity. It helped Israel move from being monarchical and temple-centered to being scattered and centered around the Torah. UNIT 2

In spite of the similarities to the wisdom traditions of surrounding cultures, Israel did more than simply share in the wisdom traditions of her neighbors. For Israel, wisdom came from God. This emphasis on God as the center and source of wisdom is expressed in several different ways:

1. A central emphasis throughout Hebrew wisdom literature is “the fear of the Lord.” The fear of the Lord is described as the beginning of both wisdom (Psalm 111:10; Proverbs 9:10; 15:33) and knowledge (Proverbs 1:7; 2:5). It is also closely tied to the promotion of life (Proverbs 10:27; 14:26–27; 19:23; 22:4).

2. Israel understood God to be the source of wisdom, giving and withholding it as seems best to Him (Job 39:17; Proverbs 2:6; Ecclesiastes 2:26). Job maintains, “To God belong wisdom and power; counsel and understanding are his” (Job 12:13).

3. God’s centrality is seen in the way wisdom addresses His acts of creation. We are told that God created the universe by His wisdom (Psalm 104:24; Proverbs 3:19). In Proverbs 8, wisdom is personified as a woman standing beside God at the beginning of creation. She was the “craftsman at his side” (v. 30) who was “brought forth” and “given birth” as God’s first act of creation (vv. 22–26). The writers of Proverbs are careful not to make wisdom equal with God, for God alone is Creator. Nevertheless, wisdom is God’s first work and the standard for all that follows.

Development of Israel’s Wisdom Tradition

Wisdom probably existed before the monarchy in the form of folk wisdom sayings. These were prevalent in family, clan, and tribe as a way of preserving and passing along basic instruction. Examples of proverbial wisdom that predate Solomon are preserved in Scripture: “He was a mighty hunter before the Lord; that is why it is said, ‘Like Nimrod, a mighty hunter before the Lord’” (Genesis 10:9), and “As the old saying goes, ‘From evildoers come evil deeds’” (1 Samuel 24:13).

With the establishment of a stable monarchy, however, wisdom became far more important, being associated with the nation’s political life. A favorite theme of both Proverbs and the wisdom psalms is the king. Wisdom was offered both to guide the king in carrying out his duties and to teach his people how to act in his presence. It was during this time that wisdom writing reached its most sophisticated form.

Later, the Exile brought with it the destruction of the temple and the loss of the Davidic monarchy. After this, wisdom took on a new and important role in the nation. Ronald Clements (1992) suggests that wisdom literature was uniquely suited to survive the Exile. With time, it easily adapted to meet the changing needs of Israelite society. In this third phase of development, Clements sees wisdom truly coming into its own. With the monarchy lost and the influence of other cultures greatly reduced, the Torah (the five books written by Moses) began to play a more central role in Israel’s life, binding together the dispersed nation and preserving a sense of religious and national identity. Clements argues that wisdom writing helped Israel transition from one form of society, monarchical and temple-centered, to another, which was dispersed and Torah-centered.

Wisdom’s new function in the Diaspora of Israel (Jews who lived outside of Palestine, scattered throughout the ancient world) can be seen in its three primary characteristics: (1) It focused on the individual’s internal reasoning. In the Old Testament, an emphasis on personal happiness and individual life goals is found almost exclusively in the wisdom writings. (2) A rethinking of moral boundaries occurred. It was no longer so easy to distinguish between the Israelite, the neighbor, the sojourner among the people, and the stranger or alien. Neither a double standard nor a strong sense of nationalism existed for Israelites and Gentiles. (3) Wisdom was universal. It functioned equally well in any social, political, or ethical environment. Clements underscores that the wisdom writings are among the most adaptable of the biblical writings, adjusting easily to shifting cultures and social contexts (1992, 13–39).

**Q:** According to wisdom literature, what is the individual’s responsibility in the world?

**A:** Wisdom assumes everyone is able to discern truth and act on it. Human beings have primary responsibility for their own destinies; we are both able to choose wisely and responsible to do so. The “mighty deeds of God” coupled with our own choices maintain the order of creation.

**Q:** How is Solomon’s life both an example of and a contradiction to wisdom?

**A:** Solomon models obedience, humility, and wisdom on one hand and disobedience, pride, and folly on the other. He began strong, but his experiences and position affected his ability to maintain a wise lifestyle. From his experience, we can learn to set clear goals for our lives, regularly check our progress, and be willing to make adjustments to move back into line with wise living.

**Q:** What similar stories are found in the wisdom literature of Israel’s neighbors?

**A:** Egyptian wisdom writings contained proverbs similar to some in the Old Testament. The Sumerians not only produced a story with parallels to the book of Job but also wrote proverbial wisdom. Canaanite wisdom literature includes the story of a man falsely accused by his nephew and removed from power, only to be restored. His story comes with about one hundred wisdom sayings, some of which, like Proverbs, support the office of king.

**Q:** How does Israel’s wisdom emphasize God as the center and source of wisdom?

**A:** For Israel, wisdom came from God. Wisdom literature emphasized “the fear of the Lord,” which is closely tied to the promotion of life. God is considered the source of wisdom; He created the universe by His wisdom. Proverbs emphasizes wisdom as God’s first work and the standard for all that follows.

**Q:** How did the importance of Israel’s wisdom change with the rise and fall of the monarchy?

**A:** Before kings ruled Israel, wisdom existed in the form of folk wisdom sayings. These were common in families, clans, and tribes as a way of passing on basic teaching. When kings began to rule, wisdom became associated with political life. Wisdom guided the king as well as his people. During the reign of the kings, wisdom reached its highest form. After the Exile, wisdom became more important in Israel’s life, binding together the scattered people and preserving a sense of religious and national identity. It helped Israel move from being monarchical and temple-centered to being scattered and centered around the Torah. UNIT 2

## 3 - The Book of Job

### Introduction to Job

#### 3.1.1 — Explain the importance of Job for believers.

Introduction

The book of Job is both interesting and difficult and may be one of the most important books in the Bible. In this account of Job’s experience with intense suffering, we recognize many of our own questions and fears regarding our understanding of God. Why is suffering such a major part of life? Why do evil people live in defiance of God and yet seem to prosper while the good suffer? Doesn’t God’s Word declare that sinners will be judged and the righteous blessed? Isn’t God committed to answering our prayers for healing, for deliverance, for justice? Or is there more to God than we are sometimes willing to see or are able to comprehend?

As we read Job, we are challenged to reexamine all of our general statements about God’s goodness, power, justice, and love and to consider the ways in which such statements, while true to a point, fail to fully describe God’s character. Moreover, the church has traditionally seen Job as a man of unending patience in suffering. But an honest reading of the entire book shows this to be true only in the opening chapters. Job makes no attempt to hide his anguish, questions, frustration, or indignation.

The book of Job is important to our understanding of God because it refuses to allow us to reduce God to a set of rules and principles. We are reminded that our theology of God will always be incomplete and, of necessity, open to contradiction, so we must return to Him for fresh insights and a deeper relationship. When we, like Job, encounter the living God, we are so changed that our questions cease to haunt us. We are satisfied.

Setting and Origin

Job lived in Uz (Job 1:1), a large area east of Jordan. It included Edom in the south (Genesis 36:19, 20, 28; Lamentations 4:21) and Syrian lands in the north (Genesis 10:23). Thus, Job’s story took place in Arabia, not Israel.

What do we know about Job? Who was he? How did he learn about God? These are hard questions. The Bible tells us Job “was the greatest man among all the people of the East” (Job 1:3). This verse reminds us of a passage in Genesis: “Abraham left everything he owned to Isaac. But while he was still living, he gave gifts to the sons of his concubines and sent them away from his son Isaac to the land of the east” (Genesis 25:5–6). Was Job related to Abraham? Was he or his father among those Abraham sent east? We do not know. But Job used the same name for God that Abraham used, and he lived in the East. Therefore, it is possible that his knowledge of God came through Abraham.

Job’s three friends—Eliphaz, Bildad, and Zophar—lived near him:

• Eliphaz was an Edomite name that may have meant “God is refined gold” or “God concedes” (Jensen 1975, 17). This man was from Teman, a city in Edom south of the Dead Sea (Genesis 36:11; Jeremiah 49:7; Ezekiel 25:13; Amos 1:12; Obadiah 9), and he was the oldest of Job’s visiting friends. According to ancient tradition, the eldest spoke first. Eliphaz appears to be a mature, noble man. Some have defined him as the scientist of the group, because his reasoning displays a more orderly, thoughtful view than the other two.

• Bildad was a Shuhite. This may mean he was a son of Shuah, the youngest son of Abraham and Keturah (Genesis 25:2). Bildad was the traditionalist and was more argumentative. His name may have meant “son of contention” (Jensen 1975, 17–18).

• We know little of Zophar. Zophar could mean “hairy,” “rough,” or “young bird” (Jensen 1975, 18). He was the moralist, and his views were more dogmatic.

The identity and origin of the book of Job remains a mystery. The fact that Job was a herdsman and offered his own sacrifices (Job 1:5) suggests he lived before either the priesthood or the temple were introduced. However, Job and his friends share an understanding of divine and human justice that points to a knowledge of Mosaic law and perhaps even the later wisdom tradition of Proverbs. The theology of Job suggests an even later time, after the Exile, when the Israelites were forced to reexamine their understanding of God in light of their recent suffering and loss. Suggested dates for the writing of Job therefore range from the time of the judges until well after the return from the Exile; yet there is no way of fixing a date with confidence.

Overview of Structure

The book of Job is highly structured, showing a high degree of artistry and planning on the writer’s part. The most obvious structural feature is the inclusion, or bracketing, at the beginning and end of the book. This bracketing takes a number of forms:

• At a simpler level, the opening prologue (1:1–2:13) and closing epilogue (42:7–17) are written in prose, while the rest of the book appears in the form of poetry.

• The introduction (1:1–5) and conclusion (42:7–17) both describe Job as happy, blessed, and content, while the speeches between (1:6–42:6) describe his experience of suffering and loss.

• The cycles of speeches between Job and his three friends (3:1–37:24) are bracketed by two interviews of God with Satan (1:6–2:13) and two interviews of God with Job (38:1–42:6).

The poetry section, which makes up the bulk of the book, is organized into three cycles of speeches between Job and his three friends. First, Job speaks in chapter 3, and then begin the cycles of speeches in which Eliphaz, Bildad, and Zophar speak with Job responding to each in turn. In the third cycle, Zophar’s speech is missing, and we are then introduced instead to Elihu. Elihu’s extensive speech recaps what the friends have said and prepares the way for God’s speeches that follow.

The contrast between the prose and poetry in Job is so sharp that it has led many scholars to question whether the two pieces were written by the same person. They wonder if the more comforting introduction and conclusion were added later to try to soften some of the sharpness of Job’s speech. The opening prose shows only patient faith: “Naked I came from my mother’s womb, and naked I shall depart. The Lord gave and the Lord has taken away; may the name of the Lord be praised” (1:21). “In all this, Job did not sin in what he said” (2:10). But in the poetic body of the book, Job carries on long and increasingly harsh protests: “After this Job opened his mouth and cursed the day of his birth” (3:1).

**Q:** Why is Job possibly one of the most important books in the Bible?

**A:** In Job’s story, we face our own questions and fears about our understanding of God. We are challenged to reexamine our ideas about God. We are also reminded that although we cannot know the answers to all of life’s questions, God’s sufficiency is more real and more powerful than all our questions.

**Q:** What is known about Job, the man?

**A:** Job lived in Uz, a large area east of Jordan. Thus, Job’s story took place in Arabia. Job “was the greatest man among all the people of the East” (Job 1:3). Job used the same name for God that Abraham used, and he lived in the East. It is possible that his knowledge of God came through Abraham. Job was a herdsman and offered his own sacrifices. His understanding of divine and human justice points to a knowledge of Mosaic law and perhaps even the later wisdom tradition of Proverbs. His theology is from a still later time, after the Exile.

**Q:** How does the structure of the book of Job show careful planning?

**A:** The author’s planning is most obvious in the inclusion or bracketing at the beginning and end of the book. Both tell of God in conversation, first with Satan and finally with Job. Further evidence of planning is that the book is divided into distinct segments: a prologue, dialogue, and a conclusion.

### Content of Job

#### 3.2.1 — Identify the question Satan raised about Job’s fear of God.

Prologue (1:1–2:13)

The prologue opens with a description of Job’s goodness. Job was “blameless and upright; he feared God and shunned evil” (1:1), a statement God later confirmed in His conversation with Satan (1:8). These opening verses make it clear that what follows can in no sense be seen as deserved. The only hint we get of potential sin is found with Job’s children; he offers regular sacrifices on the chance that one may have sinned.

The reader is then moved to heaven where God is holding court. Each of his angels comes forward to report on their activities; included is a figure identified simply as Satan, meaning “the accuser.” The Hebrew in Job uses the definite article the to describe him (that is, “the Satan”). In two interviews with God, the Satan raises the question that is explored throughout the book: “Does Job fear God for nothing?” Put another way, “When we love God, is it really God or ourselves we are loving?” or “Do we love God for Himself or for the protection and blessings we receive when we obey Him?”

The peaceful scene that opens the book is then shattered by a series of four disasters, two with a human cause (Sabeans, Chaldeans) and two with natural causes (fire, wind). Each is announced by a servant who ends by saying, “I alone have escaped to tell you.” This is followed by Job’s being covered from head to toe with painful boils. As a result of the Satan’s attacks, Job is stripped of any indication that he is blessed or protected by God.

Dialogue (3:1–42:6)

The dialogue is by far the largest section of the book, which suggests its importance for the writer. It consists of three cycles in which each of Job’s three friends speaks and Job responds (3–14; 15–21; 22–31). However, the pattern is disrupted in the third cycle by the absence of a response by Zophar. A speech by Elihu (32–37) follows the third cycle, and afterward, God breaks His apparent silence to address Job (38:1–42:6).

Job’s Friends Speak

None of Job’s friends are Israelites, strengthening the case for a setting during the time of the judges. They begin well by seeking to comfort him in the midst of his trials. They sit with him in silence for seven days (2:13), and when they do speak, they encourage him to be honest with himself and with God concerning the hidden sin they believe is causing Job’s suffering. Eliphaz begins by drawing on personal religious experience to confirm the doctrine of retribution . Bildad calls on Job to repent. He maintains that the distance between God and people is so great that all are sinful when compared with God. Zophar suggests that God’s principles of wisdom are so far above humanity that they cannot really be known.

Soon, though, their conversation degenerates into a harsh attack on what they believe is Job’s stubbornness. Their passion indicates that they are acting not out of concern for Job but out of their own need for a secure understanding of who God is and how He always acts. They rigidly defend a traditional concept of God, found in traditional wisdom literature, as always rewarding the just and punishing the wicked. The idea that Job may be innocent threatens their security in this belief. They are motivated by fear and try to reshape the facts to fit their theology of God.

Job Speaks

Job replies to his friends with growing impatience and sarcasm. “Doubtless you are the people, and wisdom will die with you! But I have a mind as well as you; I am not inferior to you. Who does not know all these things?” (12:2–3).

However, the bulk of Job’s words are directed not to his three friends, but to God. Job questions God’s fairness and seeks an opportunity to defend himself. But God has “hidden his face” from Job. The “hiding of God’s face” is a common image in the Psalms that refers to God’s seeming abandonment (Psalm 13:1), rejection (27:9), silence (69:17), and anger (89:46). When God turns His face toward the psalmist, life is restored (4:6).

To further communicate his distress, Job goes so far as to directly question God’s goodness. Recognizing his own weakness and need for help, he sees God as standing against him. He develops the idea of a mediator, someone to stand between him and God and to intercede on his behalf. “He is not a man like me that I might answer him, that we might confront each other in court. If only there were someone to arbitrate between us, to lay his hand upon us both” (Job 9:32–33).

Chapter 28 provides a sharp change of direction for Job. It is set between his declaration that the wicked will be punished (27) and the memories of his former reputation now lost (29–30). In chapter 28, Job searches the depths of the earth, but wisdom is not to be found. Ultimately wisdom is hidden in God. “The fear of the Lord—that is wisdom, and to shun evil is understanding” (28:28). Here Job recognizes the element of mystery and of God’s sovereignty in the search for wisdom. Wisdom cannot be reduced to a series of ethical laws because, at the end of the day, it is hidden in God.

A popular brand of teaching in the church today emphasizes God’s desire to prosper us. As in the case of Job’s friends, such teaching is founded on biblical principles, but some tend to try to bracket God out of an active role in the situation. This perspective claims, “If we have enough faith and use it correctly, prosperity will always be ours. If I am not blessed, I must lack faith.” But such a view subjects God to the status of a slave to our understanding of His Word.

Chapter 31 contains Job’s final defense of his righteousness. The theme of a lawsuit brought against God that Job began in chapter 10 reappears here at the close of his speeches. He concludes, “Oh, that I had someone to hear me! I sign now my defense—let the Almighty answer me; let my accuser put his indictment in writing” (31:35).

Elihu Speaks

The logical next piece in the story would be God’s response to Job. Instead, Elihu appears, a figure mentioned nowhere else in the book. He is introduced in 32:1–5 and bombasts Job and his friends in 32:6–22. This is followed by four speeches. Elihu appears to have carefully considered the statements of Job’s three friends and responds to certain statements specifically. He contradicts Job, claiming that God does answer and that Job’s suffering leads to healing (33:19–33; 36:8–15). Elihu’s speeches also seek to show that God’s actions and motives cannot be judged. This contradicts everything Job has said.

The last speech of Elihu (36–37) is particularly interesting in that it foreshadows what God says in 38–41. In this speech, Elihu honors God’s creation and His creative power.

Elihu’s speeches act as both a conclusion to the speeches of the friends and as an introduction to the speeches of God that follow. Interestingly, both God and Job completely ignore Elihu’s speeches.

God Speaks

God does appear to Job, granting the hearing Job requested. But He does not directly address Job’s questions. Instead, He talks about His might and mastery in creation, comparing it with Job’s limited perspective. He underscores Job’s helplessness by contrasting him to the power of the behemoth and leviathan.

According to Kidner, God accomplishes at least two things in His response. First, He changes the subject. “The inference could hardly be plainer: that Job and his friends have not only found the wrong answers; they have been asking the wrong questions” (1985, 70). Second, God compares Job’s reasoning with the Creator’s.

Two divine speeches and two responses from Job are made. The first divine speech (Job 38–39) challenges Job to respond, but there is nothing he can say (40:1–4). His response to the second speech (42:1–6) is different. In it he repents; that is, he expresses a fundamental change of attitude.

Epilogue (42:7–17)

Job’s change of heart centers on his acknowledgement in 42:5, “My ears had heard of you but now my eyes have seen you.” Here we see the difference between doctrinal belief and direct experience. Job receives not an answer but an encounter. And it is enough.

The friends’ roles are then reversed. Job’s friends are the ones in need of intercession, while the supposedly guilty Job is the means of their restoration. God says to them, “I am angry with you and your two friends, because you have not spoken of me what is right, as my servant Job has” (42:7).

**Q:** How did God describe Job?

**A:** God saw Job as “blameless and upright; he feared God and shunned evil.” God’s view of Job caused Him to draw particular attention to Job when Satan came for an audience.

**Q:** What motivated Job’s friends to attack his claim to innocence?

**A:** Job’s friends needed to believe God always rewards the just and punishes only the wicked. They responded out of fear, out of their own desire for a secure understanding of who God is and how He always acts. If they had believed Job innocent, they also would have needed to accept that God sometimes allows the innocent to suffer without obvious cause.

**Q:** How is Job’s attitude different from common ideas of his patience in the face of suffering?

**A:** Bible teachers often portray Job as showing great patience in suffering. Yet we seldom hear the rest of the story. Job expressed his agony, frustration, and indignation—his feelings of being treated unfairly. He went so far as to directly question God’s goodness. While recognizing his own weakness and need for help, he saw God as standing against him. He requested an audience with God so he could defend himself from unjust treatment.

**Q:** How did God respond to Job?

**A:** First, God changed the subject, implying that the wrong questions were being asked. Then He compared Job’s reasoning with His own.

**Q:** What caused Job’s change of heart?

**A:** Job’s perspective changed dramatically when he personally encountered God. His direct experience replaced incomplete doctrinal belief. He expressed this by saying, “My ears had heard of you but now my eyes have seen you.”

#### 3.2.2 — Compare Job’s response to his suffering with his friends’ response.

Prologue (1:1–2:13)

The prologue opens with a description of Job’s goodness. Job was “blameless and upright; he feared God and shunned evil” (1:1), a statement God later confirmed in His conversation with Satan (1:8). These opening verses make it clear that what follows can in no sense be seen as deserved. The only hint we get of potential sin is found with Job’s children; he offers regular sacrifices on the chance that one may have sinned.

The reader is then moved to heaven where God is holding court. Each of his angels comes forward to report on their activities; included is a figure identified simply as Satan, meaning “the accuser.” The Hebrew in Job uses the definite article the to describe him (that is, “the Satan”). In two interviews with God, the Satan raises the question that is explored throughout the book: “Does Job fear God for nothing?” Put another way, “When we love God, is it really God or ourselves we are loving?” or “Do we love God for Himself or for the protection and blessings we receive when we obey Him?”

The peaceful scene that opens the book is then shattered by a series of four disasters, two with a human cause (Sabeans, Chaldeans) and two with natural causes (fire, wind). Each is announced by a servant who ends by saying, “I alone have escaped to tell you.” This is followed by Job’s being covered from head to toe with painful boils. As a result of the Satan’s attacks, Job is stripped of any indication that he is blessed or protected by God.

Dialogue (3:1–42:6)

The dialogue is by far the largest section of the book, which suggests its importance for the writer. It consists of three cycles in which each of Job’s three friends speaks and Job responds (3–14; 15–21; 22–31). However, the pattern is disrupted in the third cycle by the absence of a response by Zophar. A speech by Elihu (32–37) follows the third cycle, and afterward, God breaks His apparent silence to address Job (38:1–42:6).

Job’s Friends Speak

None of Job’s friends are Israelites, strengthening the case for a setting during the time of the judges. They begin well by seeking to comfort him in the midst of his trials. They sit with him in silence for seven days (2:13), and when they do speak, they encourage him to be honest with himself and with God concerning the hidden sin they believe is causing Job’s suffering. Eliphaz begins by drawing on personal religious experience to confirm the doctrine of retribution . Bildad calls on Job to repent. He maintains that the distance between God and people is so great that all are sinful when compared with God. Zophar suggests that God’s principles of wisdom are so far above humanity that they cannot really be known.

Soon, though, their conversation degenerates into a harsh attack on what they believe is Job’s stubbornness. Their passion indicates that they are acting not out of concern for Job but out of their own need for a secure understanding of who God is and how He always acts. They rigidly defend a traditional concept of God, found in traditional wisdom literature, as always rewarding the just and punishing the wicked. The idea that Job may be innocent threatens their security in this belief. They are motivated by fear and try to reshape the facts to fit their theology of God.

When God turns His face toward the psalmist, life is restored (4:6).

To further communicate his distress, Job goes so far as to directly question God’s goodness. Recognizing his own weakness and need for help, he sees God as standing against him. He develops the idea of a mediator, someone to stand between him and God and to intercede on his behalf. “He is not a man like me that I might answer him, that we might confront each other in court. If only there were someone to arbitrate between us, to lay his hand upon us both” (Job 9:32–33).

In chapter 28, Job searches the depths of the earth, but wisdom is not to be found. Ultimately wisdom is hidden in God. “The fear of the Lord—that is wisdom, and to shun evil is understanding” (28:28). Here Job recognizes the element of mystery and of God’s sovereignty in the search for wisdom. Wisdom cannot be reduced to a series of ethical laws because, at the end of the day, it is hidden in God.

As in the case of Job’s friends, such teaching is founded on biblical principles, but some tend to try to bracket God out of an active role in the situation. This perspective claims, “If we have enough faith and use it correctly, prosperity will always be ours.
