He whose walk is blameless and who does what is righteous.”

Forgiveness

Another prominent theme in Psalms is forgiveness. The finest example of forgiveness is found in Psalm 51, which David composed after Nathan confronted him about his sins of adultery and murder. In a moving supplication, David writes, “Create in me a pure heart, O God, and renew a steadfast spirit within me. Do not cast me from your presence or take your Holy Spirit from me. Restore to me the joy of your salvation and grant me a willing spirit, to sustain me” (vv. 10–12).

Covenantal Language in the Psalter

Several Hebrew terms are so closely associated with covenants that they are often referred to as “covenantal language.” The most obvious is covenant , and one of the most common is God’s loving faithfulness .

Longman (1988) mentions God’s love and faithfulness in keeping the covenants He makes with His people. This is found in many Old Testament books, but it occurs 127 times in the Psalter. Psalm 145:8 lists it as one of God’s attributes: “The Lord is gracious and compassionate, slow to anger and rich in love.” When God promised to establish David’s kingdom forever, He assured David His love would never be removed (2 Samuel 7:15). The Lord provides every possible way for His people to be reconciled to Him. The ultimate manifestation of this is the sacrifice of Jesus on the cross to fulfill our covenant obligations toward Him.

Closely related to love is righteousness: “The Lord is righteousness in all his ways and loving toward all he has made” (Psalm 145:17). Righteousness involves fidelity in keeping God’s commandments. God is true to His own holy nature—not violating His own laws—and everything He does is right.

Several words speak of God’s laws. The concept of law is so central to the first five books of the Bible that they are often referred to as the law of Moses or simply the Law . Commandment , judgment , word , and testimony are other covenant words found in the Psalms, especially in Psalm 119, which praises the goodness of God’s laws.

Messiah

Along with covenant, Messiah is another major concept in Psalms. The entire Old Testament looks toward the coming of Christ. The sacrificial system of the Mosaic law pointed to Jesus as the ultimate sacrifice. Isaiah predicted Jesus’ virgin birth and described the suffering the Messiah would endure. Daniel described the Messiah’s coming in glory, and many other Old Testament prophets contributed bits of messianic prophecy. Yet predictions about the Messiah are also found throughout the book of Psalms. Psalm 8 predicts His suffering and glory. Psalm 40 speaks of His incarnation, and Psalm 110 speaks of His ascension and eternal priesthood. J. Barton Payne writes that the messianic prophecies in Psalms “constitute the greatest single block of predictive matter concerning the Savior to be found anywhere in the Old Testament” (1980, 257).

That the psalms are a source of prophecy regarding the Messiah is evident in Jesus’ references to them. In the days following Jesus’ crucifixion, His disciples were confronted with the disappearance of His body from the tomb in which He had been buried. Two of Jesus’ disciples were walking to Emmaus, discussing what the events might mean. Jesus joined them and explained how His death and resurrection fulfilled prophecies “in all the Scriptures” (Luke 24:27). Later that same evening, Jesus appeared to the Eleven and other gathered disciples, reminding them, “This is what I told you while I was still with you: Everything must be fulfilled that is written about me in the Law of Moses, the Prophets and the Psalms” (v. 44).

Jesus also quoted from the Psalms to challenge opponents to His deity. After they agreed the Christ was to be the son of David, Jesus challenged their theological assumptions about the Messiah by quoting Psalm 110:1: “The Lord says to my Lord: ‘Sit at my right hand until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet.’” How could the Messiah be David’s son if David referred to Him as Lord? (See Matthew 22:41–45.)

The value of these prophecies for defending the Christian faith was not lost on New Testament writers. Psalms is quoted in the New Testament with greater frequency than any other book. As Longman points out, Paul was responsible for about one-third of the Old Testament quotations found in the New Testament, and about one-fifth of his quotations were taken from the Psalms (1988, 65).

Kidner (1973) discusses several messianic terms found in the Psalms. The first term, Anointed One , has close connection to King David. This expression was first used in Hannah’s prophetic prayer of thanksgiving after dedicating her son Samuel to the Lord’s service in the tabernacle: “He will give strength to his king and exalt the throne of his anointed” (1 Samuel 2:10). When Samuel later anointed David as future king of Israel, the Holy Spirit came upon David and remained with him from that day forward (16:13).

Psalm 2:2 mentions the Anointed One, referring not only to King David but to Jesus Christ. The early church quoted Psalm 2:1–2 in a prayer of thanksgiving after Peter and John’s release from the Sanhedrin, applying it to Jesus (Acts 4:25–26). Jesus, who was anointed by the Holy Spirit at His baptism, fulfilled the prophecies concerning the Anointed One: “God anointed Jesus of Nazareth with the Holy Spirit and power” (10:38).

A second messianic term found in the Psalms is Son . This title was applied to the Davidic kings in God’s promise to establish David’s dynasty forever. In Psalm 2:7–8, the Son is promised “the ends of the earth” as His “possession,” referring to the divine Son who would rule the entire earth. The scope of this prophecy goes beyond the accomplishments of any of the earthly Davidic kings. Luke applies Psalm 2:7 to Jesus, demonstrating that Jesus fulfilled the prophecy of the psalm more fully than David (Acts 13:32–41). Paul further asserts that Jesus fulfilled the prophecies concerning the Son (Romans 1:2–4).

In Psalm 45:6, the king is called God . That this verse refers to the Messiah is made explicit in Hebrews 1:8, which prefaces the quotation of the verse with this statement: “About the Son he says, ‘Your throne, O God, will last for ever and ever.’”

The title Servant is applied to the Messiah in Isaiah 52:13 in a description of the suffering He would endure. The imagery of the suffering Servant is also found in Psalm 69 where the Messiah is described as being hated, insulted, and mocked or scorned.

**Q:** Who could make use of a covenant?

**A:** Covenants could be established between equals or between parties of unequal status. The unequal status type of covenant is most significant for Christian theology.

**Q:** How have the psalms reminded you of God’s presence?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How do the titles King and Shepherd help explain God’s relationship with His people?

**A:** As divine King, God acts as shepherd to His people. As King, He has total authority over His subjects, yet His attitude toward them is that of a shepherd caring for sheep.

**Q:** How did New Testament characters and writers use psalms to refer to the Messiah?

**A:** The New Testament quotes Psalms more frequently than any other book. Jesus used the Psalms to explain prophecies about Himself and to challenge opponents to His deity. Paul included about one-third of the Old Testament quotations found in the New Testament, and about one-fifth of his quotations were taken from the Psalms. 5 CHAPTER

#### 4.4.4 — Describe Psalms as a source of messianic prophecy.

When Abraham’s son Isaac was twelve years old, the Lord told Abraham to offer him as a sacrifice. This must have been difficult for the aged patriarch to hear, because Isaac was a miracle child—supernaturally provided by God. Abraham’s wife, Sarah, had been infertile until she bore Isaac at the age of ninety.

Abraham rose early the next morning and saddled his donkey. After a three-day journey, he and Isaac reached the place where God had instructed Abraham to sacrifice his son. Observing that they had not brought a sacrificial animal, Isaac asked where the lamb was. Abraham’s faith is apparent in his reply, “God himself will provide the lamb” (Genesis 22:8).

After preparing an altar and placing Isaac on it, Abraham raised his knife to slay Isaac. At the last moment, God called out to Abraham, freeing him from his obligation to sacrifice his son. The Lord had indeed provided a sacrifice.

Much of the Bible, and especially of the Old Testament, conveys its theological message through stories such as this. The message of redemption is told through such images as Noah and his family’s rescue from the Flood and the Israelites’ deliverance from slavery in Egypt.

Likewise, scholars note that the psalms are rich in theological images found elsewhere in the Bible. For example, the first psalm pictures godly people as fruitful trees planted by streams of water, while the ungodly are like chaff blown away by the wind.

Covenant

A cornerstone of theology in the Bible is the concept of covenant. A covenant is a binding agreement between two parties, typical of legal contracts. The Hebrew expression of entering into a covenant is literally “to cut a covenant.” This probably dates back to the practice of sealing a covenant by sacrificing and cutting up an animal. Besides honoring God, who oversaw the covenant, this served as a solemn warning that the one breaking the covenant would deserve the same fate as the sacrificial animal. Cutting up sacrificial animals can be seen in God’s establishing a covenant with Abraham that Abraham would have many offspring who would inherit the land of Canaan (Genesis 15:1–10).

Covenants could be established between equals, such as the covenants between Abraham and the Amorites (Genesis 14:13) and between Jacob and Laban (31:44). Covenants could also be established between parties of unequal status. This type of covenant is most significant for Christian theology.

Covenantal Themes in the Psalter

A Personal Relationship

In establishing a covenant with His people, God enters a personal relationship with them: “I will be their God, and they will be my people” (Jeremiah 31:33). The created is invited into covenant relationship with the Creator.

God is present with His covenant people. As the Israelites fled Egypt, God made His presence visible in the form of a pillar of fire by night and a cloud by day. At Mount Sinai, His presence was evidenced in thunder and lightning. The ark of the covenant symbolized His presence in the tabernacle and later in the temple. God cares for His people and is with them wherever He leads them.

The praises of the psalmists reflect God’s presence with His people. In times of trouble, the psalmist cries out to God with the expectation of being heard and delivered. During times of judgment, the psalmist cries out to God with a contrite heart, seeking forgiveness and restoration.

Several psalms recall God’s presence in Israel’s history. One of these, Psalm 136, reads, “Give thanks to the Lord . . . to him who struck down the firstborn of Egypt” (vv. 1, 10). Psalm 106 recalls God’s patience with Israel during various rebellions on their journey to the Promised Land. In Psalm 83, the psalmist petitions God to deliver him as He had during the time of the judges: “Do to them as you did to Midian, as you did to Sisera and Jabin at the river Kishon, who perished at Endor and became like refuse on the ground” (vv. 9–10).

King and Shepherd

Another important theological theme is that of God as divine King. Psalm 93 portrays the Lord as the eternal King, ultimate in royal power and splendor. Psalm 47 calls on the nations to clap their hands and shout for joy before the Lord, King of the whole earth.

A related theme is God as divine Shepherd. The image of a king acting as shepherd to his people is common in ancient Near Eastern literature. Thus, the image of David’s being called from his duties of shepherding his father’s flock to shepherd the nation of Israel is fitting. David himself develops this imagery in Psalm 23:1: “The Lord is my shepherd, I shall not be in want.” Jesus is the ultimate shepherd and identifies Himself in this role in John 10:11: “I am the good shepherd. The good shepherd lays down his life for the sheep.”

Law and Blessings

God’s law and the blessings that result from obeying it, or the curses that result from defying it, are additional covenant themes in Psalms. The connection between obeying God’s laws and enjoying a covenant relationship with Him are stated explicitly in Psalm 15:1–2: “Lord, who may dwell in your sanctuary? Who may live on your holy hill? He whose walk is blameless and who does what is righteous.”

Forgiveness

Another prominent theme in Psalms is forgiveness. The finest example of forgiveness is found in Psalm 51, which David composed after Nathan confronted him about his sins of adultery and murder. In a moving supplication, David writes, “Create in me a pure heart, O God, and renew a steadfast spirit within me. Do not cast me from your presence or take your Holy Spirit from me. Restore to me the joy of your salvation and grant me a willing spirit, to sustain me” (vv. 10–12).

Covenantal Language in the Psalter

Longman (1988) mentions God’s love and faithfulness in keeping the covenants He makes with His people. This is found in many Old Testament books, but it occurs 127 times in the Psalter. Psalm 145:8 lists it as one of God’s attributes: “The Lord is gracious and compassionate, slow to anger and rich in love.” When God promised to establish David’s kingdom forever, He assured David His love would never be removed (2 Samuel 7:15). The Lord provides every possible way for His people to be reconciled to Him. The ultimate manifestation of this is the sacrifice of Jesus on the cross to fulfill our covenant obligations toward Him.

Commandment , judgment , word , and testimony are other covenant words found in the Psalms, especially in Psalm 119, which praises the goodness of God’s laws.

## 5 - Wisdom in the Psalms

### Wisdom in Psalms, Part 1

#### 5.1.1 — Define a wisdom psalm.

What Is a Wisdom Psalm?

Determining the degree to which a psalm of Israel has been influenced by wisdom tradition is not always easy. Certainly we find elements in individual psalms that sound much like other wisdom writings. Statements such as, “Refrain from anger and turn from wrath; do not fret–it leads only to evil. For evil men will be cut off, but those who hope in the Lord will inherit the land” (Psalm 37:8–9) sound as though they were taken directly from the book of Proverbs. But what elements specifically identify material as being shaped by the wisdom tradition? And to what extent must they be present in a particular psalm before it can be classified as a “wisdom psalm”?

The classification of a wisdom psalm is determined in part by the way we understand the wisdom tradition to have influenced the psalmists. W. H. Bellinger identifies wisdom psalms by their intended function as instructional tools: “The Wisdom psalms give instruction for living and thus reflect the function of the wisdom circles” (1990, 125). Therefore, Bellinger maintains that if a psalm teaches, it is a wisdom psalm. Yet C. Hassell Bullock focuses more on the distinctive worldview of wisdom writers: “The wisdom psalms represent influences of a certain way of conceptualizing life and faith” (2001, 200). In other words, Bullock uses the writer’s view of life and faith to determine whether a psalm is a wisdom psalm. With these views in mind, it is fair to say that not everyone agrees as to what exactly constitutes a wisdom psalm.

Form

As we discussed in Chapter 2, wisdom writings—including wisdom psalms—are identified by form and content. Wisdom literature contains certain characteristic forms that are used frequently. Roland Murphy identifies seven such characteristic wisdom forms in the Psalms (1990, 103):

1. “Blessed be” formulas

2. Numerical sayings

3. “Better” sayings

4. The address of a teacher or father to a “son”

5. Alphabetic acrostics, where the first letter of each line is a successive letter in the Hebrew alphabet

6. Admonitions

7. Comparisons

Psalm 1:1 is an example of the “blessed be” formula: “Blessed is the man who does not walk in the counsel of the wicked or stand in the way of sinners or sit in the seat of mockers.”

Psalm 37:1–2 illustrates comparison: “Do not fret because of evil men or be envious of those who do wrong; for like the grass they will soon wither, like green plants they will soon die away.”

Content

In contrast to form, content focuses on the appearance of themes central to wisdom writing. Important wisdom themes in Psalms include the following:

• The problem of retribution

• The “two ways” of good and evil

• The rich and the poor

• The fear of Yahweh

• The centrality of the Torah

• God as creator and master of the universe

When we look at both form and content, we can begin to identify the influence of wisdom throughout the Psalter. Some Psalms that contain evidence of a wisdom influence include 1, 8, 14, 19, 32, 33, 34, 37, 49, 73, 104, 112, 119, and 127. In some of these, the wisdom influence is more easily recognized by the form, in others by the content, but usually by a combination of the two.

The Problem of Retribution

The question of whether God really rewards the just and punishes the wicked is explored throughout the Old Testament. Abraham raises the question with God in Genesis 18 when the Lord intends to destroy Sodom and Gomorrah. Abraham asks, “Will not the Judge of all the earth do right?” (v. 25). In Jeremiah 12:1, the prophet asks if God really does judge the wicked as He promised: “Why does the way of the wicked prosper?” Habakkuk explores the theme of God’s using an evil people, the Babylonians, to punish the less evil people of Judah: “Your eyes are too pure to look on evil; you cannot tolerate wrong. Why then do you tolerate the treacherous?” (Habakkuk 1:13).

Walter Brueggemann describes two basic retributional systems in the Old Testament, one “hot” and the other “cool” (2002, 174–176). In the “hot” system of retribution, God is an active agent, personally and directly acting to ensure that the good are rewarded while the wicked are punished. The blessings and curses of the covenant found in Deuteronomy best exemplify this way of understanding God’s justice. The “cool” pattern of retribution focuses more on the natural outcomes of relationships and the idea of reaping what we sow (see Galatians 6:7–8). For instance, as Proverbs emphasizes, liars, cheaters, and adulterers destroy their fundamental relationships in life and eventually suffer from their choices.

In some ways, Psalm 112 reflects the “cool” rhetoric of God’s retribution. Here, the psalmist describes the righteous as gracious, compassionate, and generous. As a result, they are promised descendants, wealth, and security. The wicked, by contrast, will waste away, and their longings will come to nothing.

The “hotter” rhetoric of God as active agent can be seen in the contrasting reflections of Psalms 37 and 73. Psalm 37 describes God’s direct actions toward the righteous and the wicked. Six times, we are told the righteous will inherit the land. In contrast, the writer of Psalm 73 struggles to understand the gap between God’s promise and its fulfillment. He goes to the temple and there receives a fresh revelation of God’s purposes. Interestingly, both psalms give the same explanation for the prosperity of the wicked: wickedness brings a reward, but it is fleeting, whereas its destruction is both sure and final (37:35–36; 73:18–20).

Two Ways: Good and Evil

Closely related to God’s retribution is the idea of only two basic courses we can set for our lives, both with an assured outcome. According to Psalm 1, the wicked path differs significantly from the righteous path. The righteous should choose to not walk or stand or sit in the way of the wicked, for in the end, both ways produce a harvest: The righteous are like a well-watered tree that brings forth fruit, while the wicked are like the husk around a grain of wheat that is blown away by the dry desert wind. The final destiny of each course is also made clear: “For the Lord watches over the way of the righteous, but the way of the wicked will perish” (vv. 6).

In some ways, Psalm 1 stands in tension with Psalm 73. While Psalm 1 affirms God’s justice, both in the present and as the ultimate outcome of choosing one way or another, Psalm 73 indicates the “slippage” we experience while waiting for God’s judgment. This slippage exists between what we know to be true about God and the times when our current experience does not reflect God’s ultimate goodness and justice. Psalm 1 is a true description of the end result of righteousness and wickedness in this life and in the life to come, but Psalm 73 briefly questions that: “For I envied the arrogant when I saw the prosperity of the wicked. They have no struggles; their bodies are healthy and strong” (vv. 3–5). It is precisely this gap between promise and fulfillment that the wisdom psalms explore. Yes, God protects and provides for His own in this life, but at times, justice is still a future reality to be longed for and sought after in prayer. It would be naive to try to understand the ways of the righteous and the wicked in terms of the journey alone. The wisdom psalms ultimately point to the eventual destination of the two journeys.

**Q:** How do Bellinger and Bullock identify wisdom psalms?

**A:** Bellinger identifies psalms that teach as wisdom psalms. Bullock focuses more on the way writers view life and faith to identify a wisdom psalm.

**Q:** Briefly research (a) “better” sayings and (b) acrostics in Psalms and give an example of each.

**A:** Answers will vary but could include any of these: (a) Psalms 37:16–17; 63:3; 84:10; 118:8–9; (b) Psalms 9–10; 25; 34; 37; 111; 112; 119; 145.

**Q:** What major wisdom themes does the book of Psalms address?

**A:** (1) The problem of retribution, (2) good and evil, (3) rich and poor, (4) the fear of Yahweh, (5) the centrality of the Torah or Law, and (6) God as creator and master of the universe

**Q:** On what retributional system does Proverbs focus?

**A:** Proverbs focuses more on the cool approach to retribution in which we reap the natural consequences of our decisions. In other words, liars, cheaters, and adulterers destroy their fundamental relationships in life and eventually suffer from their choices.

**Q:** What do the psalmists conclude about God’s justice?

**A:** God does protect and provide for His own, but justice is often still a future reality we pray for. We should not view the ways of the righteous and the wicked in terms of the journey alone. The wisdom psalms point to the eventual destination of the two journeys: “The Lord watches over the way of the righteous, but the way of the wicked will perish” (Psalm 1:6).

#### 5.1.2 — Describe the forms and themes of the wisdom psalms.

What Is a Wisdom Psalm?

Determining the degree to which a psalm of Israel has been influenced by wisdom tradition is not always easy. The Problem of Retribution

In Jeremiah 12:1, the prophet asks if God really does judge the wicked as He promised: “Why does the way of the wicked prosper?” Habakkuk explores the theme of God’s using an evil people, the Babylonians, to punish the less evil people of Judah: “Your eyes are too pure to look on evil; you cannot tolerate wrong. Why then do you tolerate the treacherous?” (Habakkuk 1:13).

Walter Brueggemann describes two basic retributional systems in the Old Testament, one “hot” and the other “cool” (2002, 174–176). In the “hot” system of retribution, God is an active agent, personally and directly acting to ensure that the good are rewarded while the wicked are punished. The blessings and curses of the covenant found in Deuteronomy best exemplify this way of understanding God’s justice. The “cool” pattern of retribution focuses more on the natural outcomes of relationships and the idea of reaping what we sow (see Galatians 6:7–8). For instance, as Proverbs emphasizes, liars, cheaters, and adulterers destroy their fundamental relationships in life and eventually suffer from their choices.

In some ways, Psalm 112 reflects the “cool” rhetoric of God’s retribution. Here, the psalmist describes the righteous as gracious, compassionate, and generous. As a result, they are promised descendants, wealth, and security. The wicked, by contrast, will waste away, and their longings will come to nothing.

The “hotter” rhetoric of God as active agent can be seen in the contrasting reflections of Psalms 37 and 73. Psalm 37 describes God’s direct actions toward the righteous and the wicked. Six times, we are told the righteous will inherit the land. In contrast, the writer of Psalm 73 struggles to understand the gap between God’s promise and its fulfillment. He goes to the temple and there receives a fresh revelation of God’s purposes. Interestingly, both psalms give the same explanation for the prosperity of the wicked: wickedness brings a reward, but it is fleeting, whereas its destruction is both sure and final (37:35–36; 73:18–20).

Two Ways: Good and Evil

Closely related to God’s retribution is the idea of only two basic courses we can set for our lives, both with an assured outcome. According to Psalm 1, the wicked path differs significantly from the righteous path. The righteous should choose to not walk or stand or sit in the way of the wicked, for in the end, both ways produce a harvest: The righteous are like a well-watered tree that brings forth fruit, while the wicked are like the husk around a grain of wheat that is blown away by the dry desert wind. The final destiny of each course is also made clear: “For the Lord watches over the way of the righteous, but the way of the wicked will perish” (vv. 6).

In some ways, Psalm 1 stands in tension with Psalm 73. While Psalm 1 affirms God’s justice, both in the present and as the ultimate outcome of choosing one way or another, Psalm 73 indicates the “slippage” we experience while waiting for God’s judgment. This slippage exists between what we know to be true about God and the times when our current experience does not reflect God’s ultimate goodness and justice. Psalm 1 is a true description of the end result of righteousness and wickedness in this life and in the life to come, but Psalm 73 briefly questions that: “For I envied the arrogant when I saw the prosperity of the wicked. They have no struggles; their bodies are healthy and strong” (vv. 3–5). It is precisely this gap between promise and fulfillment that the wisdom psalms explore. Yes, God protects and provides for His own in this life, but at times, justice is still a future reality to be longed for and sought after in prayer. It would be naive to try to understand the ways of the righteous and the wicked in terms of the journey alone. The wisdom psalms ultimately point to the eventual destination of the two journeys.

**Q:** How do Bellinger and Bullock identify wisdom psalms?

**A:** Bellinger identifies psalms that teach as wisdom psalms. Bullock focuses more on the way writers view life and faith to identify a wisdom psalm.

**Q:** Briefly research (a) “better” sayings and (b) acrostics in Psalms and give an example of each.

**A:** Answers will vary but could include any of these: (a) Psalms 37:16–17; 63:3; 84:10; 118:8–9; (b) Psalms 9–10; 25; 34; 37; 111; 112; 119; 145.

**Q:** What major wisdom themes does the book of Psalms address?

**A:** (1) The problem of retribution, (2) good and evil, (3) rich and poor, (4) the fear of Yahweh, (5) the centrality of the Torah or Law, and (6) God as creator and master of the universe

**Q:** On what retributional system does Proverbs focus?

**A:** Proverbs focuses more on the cool approach to retribution in which we reap the natural consequences of our decisions. In other words, liars, cheaters, and adulterers destroy their fundamental relationships in life and eventually suffer from their choices.

**Q:** What do the psalmists conclude about God’s justice?

**A:** God does protect and provide for His own, but justice is often still a future reality we pray for. We should not view the ways of the righteous and the wicked in terms of the journey alone. The wisdom psalms point to the eventual destination of the two journeys: “The Lord watches over the way of the righteous, but the way of the wicked will perish” (Psalm 1:6).

#### 5.1.3 — Discuss the two retribution systems in the Old Testament.

What Is a Wisdom Psalm?

Determining the degree to which a psalm of Israel has been influenced by wisdom tradition is not always easy. Certainly we find elements in individual psalms that sound much like other wisdom writings. Statements such as, “Refrain from anger and turn from wrath; do not fret–it leads only to evil. For evil men will be cut off, but those who hope in the Lord will inherit the land” (Psalm 37:8–9) sound as though they were taken directly from the book of Proverbs. But what elements specifically identify material as being shaped by the wisdom tradition? And to what extent must they be present in a particular psalm before it can be classified as a “wisdom psalm”?

The classification of a wisdom psalm is determined in part by the way we understand the wisdom tradition to have influenced the psalmists. W. H. Bellinger identifies wisdom psalms by their intended function as instructional tools: “The Wisdom psalms give instruction for living and thus reflect the function of the wisdom circles” (1990, 125). Therefore, Bellinger maintains that if a psalm teaches, it is a wisdom psalm. Yet C. Hassell Bullock focuses more on the distinctive worldview of wisdom writers: “The wisdom psalms represent influences of a certain way of conceptualizing life and faith” (2001, 200). In other words, Bullock uses the writer’s view of life and faith to determine whether a psalm is a wisdom psalm. With these views in mind, it is fair to say that not everyone agrees as to what exactly constitutes a wisdom psalm.

Form

As we discussed in Chapter 2, wisdom writings—including wisdom psalms—are identified by form and content. Wisdom literature contains certain characteristic forms that are used frequently. Roland Murphy identifies seven such characteristic wisdom forms in the Psalms (1990, 103):

1. “Blessed be” formulas

2. Numerical sayings

3. “Better” sayings

4. The address of a teacher or father to a “son”

5. Alphabetic acrostics, where the first letter of each line is a successive letter in the Hebrew alphabet

6. Admonitions

7. Comparisons

Psalm 1:1 is an example of the “blessed be” formula: “Blessed is the man who does not walk in the counsel of the wicked or stand in the way of sinners or sit in the seat of mockers.”

Psalm 37:1–2 illustrates comparison: “Do not fret because of evil men or be envious of those who do wrong; for like the grass they will soon wither, like green plants they will soon die away.”

Content

In contrast to form, content focuses on the appearance of themes central to wisdom writing. Important wisdom themes in Psalms include the following:

• The problem of retribution

• The “two ways” of good and evil

• The rich and the poor

• The fear of Yahweh

• The centrality of the Torah

• God as creator and master of the universe

When we look at both form and content, we can begin to identify the influence of wisdom throughout the Psalter. Some Psalms that contain evidence of a wisdom influence include 1, 8, 14, 19, 32, 33, 34, 37, 49, 73, 104, 112, 119, and 127. In some of these, the wisdom influence is more easily recognized by the form, in others by the content, but usually by a combination of the two.

The Problem of Retribution

The question of whether God really rewards the just and punishes the wicked is explored throughout the Old Testament. Abraham raises the question with God in Genesis 18 when the Lord intends to destroy Sodom and Gomorrah. Abraham asks, “Will not the Judge of all the earth do right?” (v. 25). In Jeremiah 12:1, the prophet asks if God really does judge the wicked as He promised: “Why does the way of the wicked prosper?” Habakkuk explores the theme of God’s using an evil people, the Babylonians, to punish the less evil people of Judah: “Your eyes are too pure to look on evil; you cannot tolerate wrong. Why then do you tolerate the treacherous?” (Habakkuk 1:13).

Walter Brueggemann describes two basic retributional systems in the Old Testament, one “hot” and the other “cool” (2002, 174–176). In the “hot” system of retribution, God is an active agent, personally and directly acting to ensure that the good are rewarded while the wicked are punished. The blessings and curses of the covenant found in Deuteronomy best exemplify this way of understanding God’s justice. The “cool” pattern of retribution focuses more on the natural outcomes of relationships and the idea of reaping what we sow (see Galatians 6:7–8). For instance, as Proverbs emphasizes, liars, cheaters, and adulterers destroy their fundamental relationships in life and eventually suffer from their choices.

In some ways, Psalm 112 reflects the “cool” rhetoric of God’s retribution. Here, the psalmist describes the righteous as gracious, compassionate, and generous. As a result, they are promised descendants, wealth, and security. The wicked, by contrast, will waste away, and their longings will come to nothing.

The “hotter” rhetoric of God as active agent can be seen in the contrasting reflections of Psalms 37 and 73. Psalm 37 describes God’s direct actions toward the righteous and the wicked. Six times, we are told the righteous will inherit the land. In contrast, the writer of Psalm 73 struggles to understand the gap between God’s promise and its fulfillment. He goes to the temple and there receives a fresh revelation of God’s purposes. Interestingly, both psalms give the same explanation for the prosperity of the wicked: wickedness brings a reward, but it is fleeting, whereas its destruction is both sure and final (37:35–36; 73:18–20).

Two Ways: Good and Evil

Closely related to God’s retribution is the idea of only two basic courses we can set for our lives, both with an assured outcome. According to Psalm 1, the wicked path differs significantly from the righteous path. The righteous should choose to not walk or stand or sit in the way of the wicked, for in the end, both ways produce a harvest: The righteous are like a well-watered tree that brings forth fruit, while the wicked are like the husk around a grain of wheat that is blown away by the dry desert wind. The final destiny of each course is also made clear: “For the Lord watches over the way of the righteous, but the way of the wicked will perish” (vv. 6).

In some ways, Psalm 1 stands in tension with Psalm 73. While Psalm 1 affirms God’s justice, both in the present and as the ultimate outcome of choosing one way or another, Psalm 73 indicates the “slippage” we experience while waiting for God’s judgment. This slippage exists between what we know to be true about God and the times when our current experience does not reflect God’s ultimate goodness and justice. Psalm 1 is a true description of the end result of righteousness and wickedness in this life and in the life to come, but Psalm 73 briefly questions that: “For I envied the arrogant when I saw the prosperity of the wicked. They have no struggles; their bodies are healthy and strong” (vv. 3–5). It is precisely this gap between promise and fulfillment that the wisdom psalms explore. Yes, God protects and provides for His own in this life, but at times, justice is still a future reality to be longed for and sought after in prayer. It would be naive to try to understand the ways of the righteous and the wicked in terms of the journey alone. The wisdom psalms ultimately point to the eventual destination of the two journeys.

**A:** Bellinger identifies psalms that teach as wisdom psalms. Bullock focuses more on the way writers view life and faith to identify a wisdom psalm.

**Q:** Briefly research (a) “better” sayings and (b) acrostics in Psalms and give an example of each.

**A:** Answers will vary but could include any of these: (a) Psalms 37:16–17; 63:3; 84:10; 118:8–9; (b) Psalms 9–10; 25; 34; 37; 111; 112; 119; 145.

**A:** Proverbs focuses more on the cool approach to retribution in which we reap the natural consequences of our decisions. In other words, liars, cheaters, and adulterers destroy their fundamental relationships in life and eventually suffer from their choices.

### Wisdom in Psalms, Part 2

#### 5.2.1 — Compare and contrast Proverbs’ view of wealth and poverty with Psalms’ view.

Rich and Poor

According to Proverbs, hard work is an important virtue, and those who possess it will prosper and be full. The lazy bring poverty on themselves: “A little sleep, a little slumber, a little folding of the hands to rest—and poverty will come on you like a bandit and scarcity like an armed man” (Proverbs 24:33–34). On the other hand, hard work brings wealth and comfort, which are seen as the just rewards of diligence and obedience. J. David Pleins summarizes the proverbial view of wealth as follows: “Overall, we find three attitudes toward wealth in Proverbs: a respect for wealth; an urging to enjoy abundance; and the suggestion that the rich deserve their wealth” (1993, 162).

Yet Psalms gives the nature of hard work a somewhat different emphasis. Psalms focuses on obedience to God, diligent study of the Torah (a Hebrew word that means “teaching,” “instruction,” or “law”), and the blessings that result. In Psalm 1:3, for example, the person who meditates day and night on the Torah “is like a tree planted by streams of water, which yields its fruit in season and whose leaf does not wither. Whatever he does prospers.” Psalm 37:34 extols righteousness and waiting on the Lord: “Wait for the Lord and keep his way. He will exalt you to inherit the land; when the wicked are cut off, you will see it.” In agrarian societies, land ownership is the primary means of producing wealth. Psalm 112:3 says of the one who fears the Lord, “Wealth and riches are in his house, and his righteousness endures forever.”

Psalm 73 examines the topic of ill-gotten wealth—wealth amassed by oppressing the poor and using dishonest practices. In fact, Psalm 73:12 associates wealth with wickedness: “This is what the wicked are like—always carefree, they increase in wealth.” In this psalm, the psalmist struggles with the observation that sin seems to pay off. The wicked practice violence and deceit, but rather than experiencing a curse, “they have no struggles; their bodies are healthy and strong. They are free from the burdens common to man; they are not plagued by human ills” (vv. 4–5). Wealth here is considered to result not from diligence or laziness but from the willingness to be dishonest and oppress others.

Psalm 49 also associates the accumulation of wealth with wickedness. “Why should I fear when evil days come, when wicked deceivers surround me—those who trust in their wealth and boast of their great riches?” (vv. 5–6). The writer also describes the transitory nature of wealth: “Man, despite his riches, does not endure; he is like the beasts that perish” (v. 12). He finally concludes that wealth is of limited value and not necessarily evidence of righteousness or of God’s blessing.

The wisdom psalms join with the teachings of Proverbs to provide a balanced understanding of wealth and poverty. Some aspects of prosperity are under our control. Hard work and obedience to God are far more likely to result in material blessing than are laziness and a rebellious spirit. However, we live in a fallen world, and sin can also bring prosperity. The wisdom psalms remind us that, in a sinful world, people may obtain wealth by oppressing the poor and that poverty can result from injustice, not just laziness. The difference between the righteous and the wicked is in the object of their trust. Whereas the former trust in the Lord, the latter place their confidence in riches that are transitory and have no power to save them from death.

The Fear of Yahweh

In the introduction to Proverbs, we read, “The fear of the Lord is the beginning of knowledge, but fools despise wisdom and discipline” (1:7). In the wisdom psalms, the fear of the Lord is associated with two concepts: (1) material blessings that result from the fear of the Lord, and (2) material blessings that result from obedience to God’s law.

That is, throughout Psalms, the fear of the Lord is closely associated with material blessing, both in the form of God’s provision and in His protection from enemies. For instance, Psalm 34 testifies of an individual who had been in deep trouble but experienced God’s deliverance. God protects him from enemies and provides for him, even during a famine. Verses 11–14 specifically define what it means to “fear the Lord.” Such fear is not simply an emotion or a way of thinking about God but involves action—speaking only truth, avoiding evil, doing good, and seeking peace. In short, fearing the Lord means obeying God by acting as He would act.

The Centrality of Torah

The book of Psalms further emphasizes the connection between fearing the Lord and obeying the Torah. Clearly, the placement of Psalm 1 at the beginning of the collection of psalms is far from accidental. By emphasizing the centrality of the Torah, it sets the stage for all that is to follow. We must not overlook the fact that the book of Israel’s prayers begins with such a strong affirmation that a balanced, whole, prosperous life is centered on obeying the Torah.

Psalm 1 identifies the righteous person as one who “meditates” on the Torah. The Hebrew word for meditate also means “to mutter, whisper, growl, speak, or roar.” It conveys the sense of an active pondering or ruminating. Thus, the righteous life is spent chewing on the Word of God.

Psalm 119 speaks repeatedly of those who fear the Lord (119:38, 63, 74, 79, 120). The longest and most extensive of the psalms, Psalm 119 can seem tedious and repetitive unless we understand its structure and plan. This psalm is an example of an acrostic psalm that uses the twenty-two letters of the Hebrew alphabet as an organizing structure. Each of the first eight verses begins with the first letter in the Hebrew alphabet; the next eight verses start with the second letter; and so on. As with Psalm 1, Psalm 119 portrays a strong connection between the Torah and the way (vv. 1, 9, 29–30) or path (vv. 33, 35, 101, 104–105, 128) of the righteous.

Yet Psalm 119 is in striking contrast to the perceptions of the New Testament Pharisees regarding the Torah. For them, keeping the Torah was a strict obligation and terrible burden. In contrast, Psalm 119 indicates that keeping the Torah is meant to be a delighted, joyful response to being set free from slavery and given the Promised Land. Walter Brueggemann asserts that once we make a clear commitment to a new allegiance to God, the Torah is transformed from a list of laws to a pathway leading to life. The teachers of Psalm 119 are not bound by legalism. They do not find the commandments restrictive or burdensome. Rather, they recognize a focus on life, an absence of frantic moral dilemma, a sense of priorities matched by an absence of anxiety (Brueggemann 1984, 40). The Torah is not a list of arbitrary rules but instructions for living in harmony with both God and neighbor. Walking in the way of Torah brings joy and fullness of life.

God as Creator and Master of the Universe

Although creation is not a prominent theme in wisdom writing, we include it in our discussion for several reasons: (1) A number of creation psalms reflect on the wonder of God’s creation. (2) God-as-Creator is an important part of the message of the book of Job. (3) Creation is a theme that can be designated as wisdom in Psalms.

We often think of creation only in terms of the Genesis account, primarily as an explanation of the origins of the world. However, creation was not just a one-time event in the past; it is a continual, ongoing work of God. Yahweh not only was Creator of the world in the past, but He is also Creator today. All of life is His ongoing act of creation. Consider the references to creation in Psalm 104:27–30:

These all look to you to give them their food at the proper time. When you give it to them, they gather it up; when you open your hand, they are satisfied with good things. When you hide your face, they are terrified; when you take away their breath, they die and return to the dust. When you send your Spirit, they are created [ bara ], and you renew the face of the earth.

A primary function of creation language in the Old Testament is to affirm and justify God’s mastery over all other powers in the world. Both Isaiah and some psalmists speak of God as Creator to demonstrate that He is master both of the nations and of their gods (Isaiah 40:12–17; 45:18; Psalm 33).

Psalm 8 best exemplifies the use of creation theology in the wisdom tradition. The psalm begins and ends with the proclamation, “O Lord, our Lord, how majestic is your name in all the earth!” (vv. 1, 9). Creation reflects and testifies to the greatness of the Creator. Human beings need to understand their place in the world in relation to God:

When I consider your heavens, the work of your fingers, the moon and the stars, which you have set in place, what is man that you are mindful of him, the son of man that you care for him? You made him a little lower than the heavenly beings and crowned him with glory and honor. (v. 3)

Even with all humanity has accomplished, creation reminds us of our relative smallness in the world. It also affirms that our dominion does not come from our own power but is a God-given gift and responsibility.

**Q:** How do Psalms and Proverbs together give us a balanced view of wealth and poverty?

**A:** We can draw several conclusions by studying both books. Some aspects of prosperity are under our control. Hard work and obedience to God are far more likely to result in material blessing than are laziness and rebellion. However, in a sinful world, wealth can be obtained through wickedness and poverty can result from injustice, not just laziness. The difference between the righteous and the wicked is in the object of their trust.

**Q:** How does Psalm 119 connect God’s law with the believer’s life?

**A:** It portrays obedience to the Torah as a joyful response to freedom from slavery and God’s fulfillment of His promises. The Torah is a pathway to life. It provides instructions for living in harmony with both God and neighbor. Walking in the way of Torah brings joy and fullness of life.

**Q:** What aspects of your life demonstrate that God’s creative work is ongoing?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** What is a primary purpose of creation language in the Old Testament?

**A:** To affirm and justify God’s mastery over all other powers in the world UNIT 3

#### 5.2.2 — Compare and contrast Proverbs’ view of the fear of the Lord with Psalms’ view.

Rich and Poor

According to Proverbs, hard work is an important virtue, and those who possess it will prosper and be full. The lazy bring poverty on themselves: “A little sleep, a little slumber, a little folding of the hands to rest—and poverty will come on you like a bandit and scarcity like an armed man” (Proverbs 24:33–34). On the other hand, hard work brings wealth and comfort, which are seen as the just rewards of diligence and obedience. J. David Pleins summarizes the proverbial view of wealth as follows: “Overall, we find three attitudes toward wealth in Proverbs: a respect for wealth; an urging to enjoy abundance; and the suggestion that the rich deserve their wealth” (1993, 162).

Yet Psalms gives the nature of hard work a somewhat different emphasis. Psalms focuses on obedience to God, diligent study of the Torah (a Hebrew word that means “teaching,” “instruction,” or “law”), and the blessings that result. In Psalm 1:3, for example, the person who meditates day and night on the Torah “is like a tree planted by streams of water, which yields its fruit in season and whose leaf does not wither. Whatever he does prospers.” Psalm 37:34 extols righteousness and waiting on the Lord: “Wait for the Lord and keep his way. He will exalt you to inherit the land; when the wicked are cut off, you will see it.” In agrarian societies, land ownership is the primary means of producing wealth. Psalm 112:3 says of the one who fears the Lord, “Wealth and riches are in his house, and his righteousness endures forever.”

Psalm 73 examines the topic of ill-gotten wealth—wealth amassed by oppressing the poor and using dishonest practices. In fact, Psalm 73:12 associates wealth with wickedness: “This is what the wicked are like—always carefree, they increase in wealth.” In this psalm, the psalmist struggles with the observation that sin seems to pay off. The wicked practice violence and deceit, but rather than experiencing a curse, “they have no struggles; their bodies are healthy and strong. They are free from the burdens common to man; they are not plagued by human ills” (vv. 4–5). Wealth here is considered to result not from diligence or laziness but from the willingness to be dishonest and oppress others.

Psalm 49 also associates the accumulation of wealth with wickedness. “Why should I fear when evil days come, when wicked deceivers surround me—those who trust in their wealth and boast of their great riches?” (vv. 5–6). The writer also describes the transitory nature of wealth: “Man, despite his riches, does not endure; he is like the beasts that perish” (v. 12). He finally concludes that wealth is of limited value and not necessarily evidence of righteousness or of God’s blessing.

The wisdom psalms join with the teachings of Proverbs to provide a balanced understanding of wealth and poverty. Some aspects of prosperity are under our control. Hard work and obedience to God are far more likely to result in material blessing than are laziness and a rebellious spirit. However, we live in a fallen world, and sin can also bring prosperity. The wisdom psalms remind us that, in a sinful world, people may obtain wealth by oppressing the poor and that poverty can result from injustice, not just laziness. The difference between the righteous and the wicked is in the object of their trust. Whereas the former trust in the Lord, the latter place their confidence in riches that are transitory and have no power to save them from death.

The Fear of Yahweh

In the introduction to Proverbs, we read, “The fear of the Lord is the beginning of knowledge, but fools despise wisdom and discipline” (1:7). In the wisdom psalms, the fear of the Lord is associated with two concepts: (1) material blessings that result from the fear of the Lord, and (2) material blessings that result from obedience to God’s law.

That is, throughout Psalms, the fear of the Lord is closely associated with material blessing, both in the form of God’s provision and in His protection from enemies. For instance, Psalm 34 testifies of an individual who had been in deep trouble but experienced God’s deliverance. God protects him from enemies and provides for him, even during a famine. Verses 11–14 specifically define what it means to “fear the Lord.” Such fear is not simply an emotion or a way of thinking about God but involves action—speaking only truth, avoiding evil, doing good, and seeking peace. In short, fearing the Lord means obeying God by acting as He would act.

The Centrality of Torah

The book of Psalms further emphasizes the connection between fearing the Lord and obeying the Torah. Clearly, the placement of Psalm 1 at the beginning of the collection of psalms is far from accidental. By emphasizing the centrality of the Torah, it sets the stage for all that is to follow. We must not overlook the fact that the book of Israel’s prayers begins with such a strong affirmation that a balanced, whole, prosperous life is centered on obeying the Torah.

Psalm 1 identifies the righteous person as one who “meditates” on the Torah. The Hebrew word for meditate also means “to mutter, whisper, growl, speak, or roar.” It conveys the sense of an active pondering or ruminating. Thus, the righteous life is spent chewing on the Word of God.

Psalm 119 speaks repeatedly of those who fear the Lord (119:38, 63, 74, 79, 120). The longest and most extensive of the psalms, Psalm 119 can seem tedious and repetitive unless we understand its structure and plan. This psalm is an example of an acrostic psalm that uses the twenty-two letters of the Hebrew alphabet as an organizing structure. Each of the first eight verses begins with the first letter in the Hebrew alphabet; the next eight verses start with the second letter; and so on. As with Psalm 1, Psalm 119 portrays a strong connection between the Torah and the way (vv. 1, 9, 29–30) or path (vv. 33, 35, 101, 104–105, 128) of the righteous.

Yet Psalm 119 is in striking contrast to the perceptions of the New Testament Pharisees regarding the Torah. For them, keeping the Torah was a strict obligation and terrible burden. In contrast, Psalm 119 indicates that keeping the Torah is meant to be a delighted, joyful response to being set free from slavery and given the Promised Land. Walter Brueggemann asserts that once we make a clear commitment to a new allegiance to God, the Torah is transformed from a list of laws to a pathway leading to life. The teachers of Psalm 119 are not bound by legalism. They do not find the commandments restrictive or burdensome. Rather, they recognize a focus on life, an absence of frantic moral dilemma, a sense of priorities matched by an absence of anxiety (Brueggemann 1984, 40). The Torah is not a list of arbitrary rules but instructions for living in harmony with both God and neighbor. Walking in the way of Torah brings joy and fullness of life.

God as Creator and Master of the Universe

Although creation is not a prominent theme in wisdom writing, we include it in our discussion for several reasons: (1) A number of creation psalms reflect on the wonder of God’s creation. (2) God-as-Creator is an important part of the message of the book of Job. (3) Creation is a theme that can be designated as wisdom in Psalms.

We often think of creation only in terms of the Genesis account, primarily as an explanation of the origins of the world. However, creation was not just a one-time event in the past; it is a continual, ongoing work of God. Yahweh not only was Creator of the world in the past, but He is also Creator today. All of life is His ongoing act of creation. Consider the references to creation in Psalm 104:27–30:

These all look to you to give them their food at the proper time. When you give it to them, they gather it up; when you open your hand, they are satisfied with good things. When you hide your face, they are terrified; when you take away their breath, they die and return to the dust. When you send your Spirit, they are created [ bara ], and you renew the face of the earth.

A primary function of creation language in the Old Testament is to affirm and justify God’s mastery over all other powers in the world. Both Isaiah and some psalmists speak of God as Creator to demonstrate that He is master both of the nations and of their gods (Isaiah 40:12–17; 45:18; Psalm 33).

Psalm 8 best exemplifies the use of creation theology in the wisdom tradition. The psalm begins and ends with the proclamation, “O Lord, our Lord, how majestic is your name in all the earth!” (vv. 1, 9). Creation reflects and testifies to the greatness of the Creator. Human beings need to understand their place in the world in relation to God:

When I consider your heavens, the work of your fingers, the moon and the stars, which you have set in place, what is man that you are mindful of him, the son of man that you care for him? You made him a little lower than the heavenly beings and crowned him with glory and honor. (v. 3)

Even with all humanity has accomplished, creation reminds us of our relative smallness in the world. It also affirms that our dominion does not come from our own power but is a God-given gift and responsibility.

**Q:** How do Psalms and Proverbs together give us a balanced view of wealth and poverty?

**A:** We can draw several conclusions by studying both books. Some aspects of prosperity are under our control. Hard work and obedience to God are far more likely to result in material blessing than are laziness and rebellion. However, in a sinful world, wealth can be obtained through wickedness and poverty can result from injustice, not just laziness. The difference between the righteous and the wicked is in the object of their trust.

**A:** It portrays obedience to the Torah as a joyful response to freedom from slavery and God’s fulfillment of His promises. The Torah is a pathway to life. It provides instructions for living in harmony with both God and neighbor. Walking in the way of Torah brings joy and fullness of life.

**Q:** What aspects of your life demonstrate that God’s creative work is ongoing?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** What is a primary purpose of creation language in the Old Testament?

**A:** To affirm and justify God’s mastery over all other powers in the world UNIT 3

## 6 - The Book of Proverbs

### Introduction to Proverbs

#### 6.1.1 — Define the word proverb and the forms mashal can take in Scripture.

What Is a Proverb?

The term proverb , translated from the Hebrew word mashal , originally referred to a comparison. It meant “to compare” or “to be like.” Webster’s Dictionary focuses more on the process by which a saying or word becomes proverbial through repetition and usage. Webster defines a proverb as “a short sentence often repeated, expressing a well-known truth or common fact, ascertained by experience or observation; a maxim of wisdom” or as “a byword ; a name often repeated; and hence frequently, a reproach or object of contempt.”

Bywords can be limited to a particular cultural context or historical situation. For example, former United States President John F. Kennedy gave many speeches in his life, but his statement, “Ask not what your country can do for you; ask what you can do for your country!” has been so often repeated that it has gained proverbial status in America. In his letter to Titus, Paul speaks of the people from Crete’s reputation for stubbornness and stupidity: “One of them, a prophet of their own said, ‘Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, lazy gluttons’” (1:12). The term cretin is still used today of someone who possesses these traits. A specific event in history can also give birth to a proverb. In North American culture, Benedict Arnold became famous as a traitor for siding with the British in America’s Revolutionary War. Now, to be a “Benedict Arnold” is to be a traitor.

A word in one language rarely has a single, exact translation in another language. This is the case with the word translated as proverb . The Hebrew word mashal refers to a wide variety of forms and meanings, which we can more fully understand by considering mashal ’s definition and usage. The definition, as given above, gives us only a partial picture. To further our understanding, let us look at the different types of writing that the Old Testament refers to as a mashal :

• In Balaam’s oracles concerning God’s plan to bless Israel in Numbers 23–24, the word translated as “oracle” is mashal .

• The discourses of Job are called mashals (Job 27:1; 29:1).

• Ezekiel 17:2 says, “Son of man, set forth an allegory ( mashal ) and tell the house of Israel a parable.” The passage that follows (vv. 2–10) gives a prophetic allegory of an eagle (God) planting a vine (Israel) and the vine’s subsequent unfaithfulness.

• Ezekiel 20:49 says, “Ah, Sovereign Lord! They are saying of me, ‘Isn’t he just telling parables ( mashal )?’”

• In Micah 2:4, a mashal is related to a lament: “In that day shall one take up a parable ( mashal ) against you, and lament with a doleful lamentation, and say, We be utterly spoiled: he hath changed the portion of my people: how hath he removed it from me! turning away he hath divided our fields” (KJV). Here, as in many prophetic writings, the proverb takes on a decidedly negative connotation.

• In Deuteronomy 28:37, the sense of mashal is as a negative example or byword: “Thou shalt become an astonishment, a proverb, and a byword ( mashal ), among all nations whither the Lord shall lead thee” (KJV).

• In Habakkuk 2:6, the sense of mashal is more of an active curse: “Shall not all these take up a parable ( mashal ) against him, and a taunting proverb against him, and say, Woe to him that increaseth that which is not his! how long? and to him that ladeth himself with thick clay!” (KJV).

It soon becomes clear that a proverb can be more than a simple wisdom saying, so that the Hebrew concept of proverb is far broader than the simple, two-line comparisons with which we are most familiar.

Structure of the Book

The book of Proverbs is, in fact, a collection of collections. It begins with a general introduction (1:1–7) followed by seven sections that can be distinguished by either a fresh introduction or a dramatic change in literary style. The general introduction credits Solomon with collecting the sayings and is followed by material credited to him (1:8–9:18). The book is divided as follows:

Proverbs of Solomon 1–9

Proverbs of Solomon 10:1–22:16

Sayings of the wise 22:17–24:22

More sayings of the wise 24:23–34

Proverbs of Solomon copied by King Hezekiah’s men 25–29

Words of Agur 30

Words of the Queen Mother to her son, King Lemuel 31

William Brown suggests that we should understand the structure of Proverbs, as a whole, to represent the journey of life (1996, 20, 47–49). In the beginning, the parents of a young man help prepare him to enter the world. Thus, the goal of Proverbs is to prepare young men to leave their parents’ home and find safety and fulfillment in their own homes with godly and virtuous wives.

**Q:** What types of writings are referred to as mashal in the Old Testament?

**A:** Prophetic allegories and parables in Ezekiel, Balaam’s oracles in Numbers, and the discourses of Job; also, lament in Micah, a negative byword in Deuteronomy, and an active curse in Habakkuk

**Q:** What authors contributed to the book of Proverbs?

**A:** The Proverbs collections were written or compiled by Solomon, “the wise,” Agur, and the Queen Mother.

#### 6.1.2 — Identify the structure of the book of Proverbs.

What Is a Proverb?

The term proverb , translated from the Hebrew word mashal , originally referred to a comparison. It meant “to compare” or “to be like.” Webster’s Dictionary focuses more on the process by which a saying or word becomes proverbial through repetition and usage. Webster defines a proverb as “a short sentence often repeated, expressing a well-known truth or common fact, ascertained by experience or observation; a maxim of wisdom” or as “a byword ; a name often repeated; and hence frequently, a reproach or object of contempt.”

Bywords can be limited to a particular cultural context or historical situation. For example, former United States President John F. Kennedy gave many speeches in his life, but his statement, “Ask not what your country can do for you; ask what you can do for your country!” has been so often repeated that it has gained proverbial status in America. In his letter to Titus, Paul speaks of the people from Crete’s reputation for stubbornness and stupidity: “One of them, a prophet of their own said, ‘Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, lazy gluttons’” (1:12). The term cretin is still used today of someone who possesses these traits. A specific event in history can also give birth to a proverb. In North American culture, Benedict Arnold became famous as a traitor for siding with the British in America’s Revolutionary War. Now, to be a “Benedict Arnold” is to be a traitor.

A word in one language rarely has a single, exact translation in another language. This is the case with the word translated as proverb . The definition, as given above, gives us only a partial picture. To further our understanding, let us look at the different types of writing that the Old Testament refers to as a mashal :

• The discourses of Job are called mashals (Job 27:1; 29:1).

• Ezekiel 17:2 says, “Son of man, set forth an allegory ( mashal ) and tell the house of Israel a parable.” The passage that follows (vv. 2–10) gives a prophetic allegory of an eagle (God) planting a vine (Israel) and the vine’s subsequent unfaithfulness.

• Ezekiel 20:49 says, “Ah, Sovereign Lord! They are saying of me, ‘Isn’t he just telling parables ( mashal )?’”

• In Micah 2:4, a mashal is related to a lament: “In that day shall one take up a parable ( mashal ) against you, and lament with a doleful lamentation, and say, We be utterly spoiled: he hath changed the portion of my people: how hath he removed it from me! turning away he hath divided our fields” (KJV). Here, as in many prophetic writings, the proverb takes on a decidedly negative connotation.

• In Deuteronomy 28:37, the sense of mashal is as a negative example or byword: “Thou shalt become an astonishment, a proverb, and a byword ( mashal ), among all nations whither the Lord shall lead thee” (KJV).

• In Habakkuk 2:6, the sense of mashal is more of an active curse: “Shall not all these take up a parable ( mashal ) against him, and a taunting proverb against him, and say, Woe to him that increaseth that which is not his! how long? and to him that ladeth himself with thick clay!” (KJV).

The general introduction credits Solomon with collecting the sayings and is followed by material credited to him (1:8–9:18). The book is divided as follows:

Proverbs of Solomon 1–9

Proverbs of Solomon 10:1–22:16

Sayings of the wise 22:17–24:22

More sayings of the wise 24:23–34

Proverbs of Solomon copied by King Hezekiah’s men 25–29

Words of Agur 30

Words of the Queen Mother to her son, King Lemuel 31

William Brown suggests that we should understand the structure of Proverbs, as a whole, to represent the journey of life (1996, 20, 47–49). In the beginning, the parents of a young man help prepare him to enter the world. Thus, the goal of Proverbs is to prepare young men to leave their parents’ home and find safety and fulfillment in their own homes with godly and virtuous wives.

**Q:** What types of writings are referred to as mashal in the Old Testament?

**A:** Prophetic allegories and parables in Ezekiel, Balaam’s oracles in Numbers, and the discourses of Job; also, lament in Micah, a negative byword in Deuteronomy, and an active curse in Habakkuk

**Q:** What authors contributed to the book of Proverbs?

**A:** The Proverbs collections were written or compiled by Solomon, “the wise,” Agur, and the Queen Mother.

### Content of Proverbs

#### 6.2.1 — Describe the focus of the opening section of Proverbs.

Attempts to identify how the proverbs were originally organized have been largely unsuccessful. Individual proverbs usually stand on their own, and the final editor of the collections made little or no attempt to group them by subject or style. Therefore, it is more helpful to speak in terms of guiding themes that appear important to the writers.

Introduction (1:1–7)

The first seven verses of Proverbs introduce the book, with verses 2–6 providing the general focus of all that follows. This opening section of Proverbs emphasizes the family more heavily than elsewhere. These parents, particularly the father, seek to guide their young son safely through adolescence into manhood. “Listen, my son, to your father’s instruction and do not forsake your mother’s teaching” (1:8). Derek Kidner points out two principal threats to a young person growing up: (1) the attraction of the gang (1:10–19; 2:12–15; 4:14–19), which appeals to the need for identity and power, and (2) the allure of sex (2:16–19), which demands immediate gratification (1985, 20). The first temptation tears at the fabric of society, the second at that of the home.

Specifically, the temptation of adultery or sex outside of marriage presents itself in terms of isolated pleasure. It promises the satisfaction of sexual pleasure with no accompanying relationship, commitment, obligation, or consequence. The teacher in Proverbs responds to this temptation by putting it back in its social context: sin is relational. No one sins in a vacuum, and sexual sin in particular has a dramatic impact on both family and community. Adultery results in a scattering of illegitimate children instead of a close nuclear family, broken trust, public shame, venereal disease, and the wrath of jealous spouses.

Today, addiction to pornography is a major problem in cultures where the Internet is easily available, even among Christians. One of its most destructive effects is that the person involved in pornography cannot help but bring aspects of this “relationless sexuality” into his or her relationship with a spouse. Thus, the real relationship is damaged and even destroyed for the sake of fantasy.

The teacher in Proverbs emphasizes that a better life option than gangs or fornication exists. That is, he wants his children and his readers to understand that wisdom and the fear of the Lord are viable alternatives to the ways of destruction. Each person must make his or her own choices. Yet this teacher is determined to positively influence those individual decisions.

Solomon’s Proverbs, Part 1 (1–9)

The central issue of Proverbs 1–9 (the first of Solomon’s collections) is the disparity between wisdom and folly, both of which are personified as women. Lady Wisdom is introduced in 1:20–33. Chapter 5 vividly contrasts the adulterous woman with the wife of one’s youth. Then, in chapter 9, Lady Wisdom is shown in opposition to “the woman Folly” (9:13).

This first section of Proverbs also introduces the sluggard or the lazy person. He procrastinates (6:9–10), fails to complete tasks (12:27; 19:24), and avoids anything unpleasant or burdensome (22:13). Consequently, he is restless (13:4; 21:25–26). He stands in sharp contrast to the industrious person. In wisdom literature, blessing is closely tied to prosperity and material fullness; therefore, industry is a key virtue. On the other hand, the lazy person is viewed as his own worst enemy.

Additionally, Proverbs 1–9 demonstrates the poetic device known as inclusion , which ties a group of texts together by repeating certain words, phrases, or ideas at the beginning and end of a book or section (see Lesson 1.3). For example, Proverbs 9:10 repeats a phrase found in 1:7 regarding the “fear of the Lord.” In conjunction with a similar statement in 31:30, this creates inclusion around both the first portion of the book and the book as a whole (Murphy 1990, 16). This establishment of verbal “bookends” is a common device in biblical writing to underscore a central idea that binds the other concepts together. Scripture was normally heard, not read, because books were scarce and costly and because listeners often could neither read nor write. Verbal cues, like inclusions in the text and the repetition of important ideas, helped listeners make sense of and retain what they were hearing.

Solomon’s Proverbs, Parts 2 and 3 (10:1–22:16; 25–29)

The central collections of Proverbs (chapters 10–29) stand in sharp contrast to the collection preceding them (1–9) and the two collections following (30–31). While the opening and closing collections of Proverbs are dominated by contemplative wisdom, these central collections consist entirely of short, concise, often self-contained sayings—that is, of proverbial wisdom. A number of characteristics are common to this kind of writing.

First, such a proverb makes a simple (sometimes overly simplistic) assertion that is unqualified, and the reader is then invited to examine it in light of personal experience.

Second, some proverbs seem to contradict themselves. For example, Proverbs 26:4–5 reads: “Do not answer a fool according to his folly, or you will be like him yourself. Answer a fool according to his folly, or he will be wise in his own eyes.”

In a more subtle and general sense, a degree of tension—almost contradiction—can also exist between two or more proverbial teachings. For example, wisdom writing consistently teaches that the wise will be blessed with prosperity and a good life, whereas the fool will be punished and the sluggard will find poverty. Yet the poor are presented as objects of God’s special concern (Proverbs 14:31; 17:5). This type of tension is inherent in the nature of a proverb, which is too short to encompass all of reality in a single saying.

This apparent tension within wisdom teachings reflects a problem not so much with the proverbs themselves as with our understanding of the truth they intend to convey. Proverbs reflect on the nature of relationships between people. Relationship, by definition, involves multiple wills, desires, levels of maturity, and so forth. Thus, a third characteristic of proverbs is that they are general truths intended as guidelines rather than as universal absolutes.

Put simply, proverbs are not promises. They are wise advice for ordering one’s life and dealings with other people in a way that should result in happiness. Still, following these guidelines faithfully cannot guarantee the outcome. Sin and free will are constant factors affecting the outcomes of our decisions.

I remember growing up in the church and listening to the anguished prayers of parents whose children had abandoned their faith. Often they would quote Proverbs 22:6: “Train a child in the way he should go, and when he is old he will not turn from it.” Then they would say, “I’m hanging on to God’s promise to bring my child back to Him.” The problem with this understanding, of course, is that God refuses to force anyone to accept Him. Our job is to rear our children in a godly fashion and to intercede for them, but neither we nor God Himself can ensure a positive outcome.

In addition to its switch to proverbial wisdom (in contrast to the contemplative discourses in the first collection), Solomon’s second collection utilizes the form of Hebrew poetry known as parallelism. Chapters 10–15 primarily use antithetic parallelism, where the second part of the proverb says the opposite of the first part. On the other hand, Proverbs 16–22:16 primarily use synonymous and synthetic parallelism, in which the second half of the proverb restates and builds on the first half.

The third collection of Solomon’s proverbs, gathered by King Hezekiah’s scribes in chapters 25–29, tends to be more organized. That is, the sayings are clustered according to their topics. We find small groups of proverbs on the nature of kings (25:2–7), fools (26:3–12), the sluggard (26:13–16), and the evil man (26:17–28).

Other Collections

The Two Collections of “the Wise” (22:17–24:34)

Two collections in Proverbs are attributed simply to “the wise.” They are somewhat different in tone and style from Solomon’s collections. Instead of the abstract statements of truth found before and after this section, this segment uses more of the personal appeal of a parent to a child. The admonition to “listen to your father, who gave you life, and do not despise your mother when she is old” (23:22) is reminiscent of Solomon’s writings in the first section of Proverbs. The sayings of the wise also use brief and vivid portraits to illustrate vices to avoid, such as the picture of the drunkard in 23:29–35 and the sluggard in 24:30–34.

Words of Agur (30)

Proverbs 30 begins with a sharp change of both style and tone that indicates a change of writers. Agur, son of Jakeh, offers a wisdom oracle.

Verse 4 proposes a set of questions whose answer can only be God, and verses 7–9 present the only prayer contained in Proverbs. The writer asks two things of the Lord: to be kept from lies and to receive neither wealth nor poverty. While the problems brought by poverty are more obvious, wealth also brings temptations. The wise person knows how to be content with “enough.”

The rest of the chapter consists of wisdom presented in a series of lists. Agur uses a common technique—numerical sayings such as “There are three things . . ., four”—to examine things that are never satisfied, are wonderful, perturb the earth, are little but wise, and are majestic.

A Tale of Two Women (31)

The final chapter of Proverbs may be divided into two sections. In verses 1–9, the Queen Mother advises her son, King Lemuel, on what it means to be a king. A king is ruined by spending his strength on women and drunkenness. Such a lifestyle causes him to “forget the law.” Deuteronomy 17:18–20 indicates that the king of Israel had a personal copy of the books of the Law so he could read it constantly and “learn to fear the Lord his God.” For the king, part of keeping the Law includes showing compassion and bringing justice to the oppressed.

Proverbs 31:10–31 is then a portrait of a godly wife and her administration of the home. It is an alphabetic acrostic poem in which each of the twenty-two lines begins with a successive letter of the Hebrew alphabet. The woman in the poem can be compared to Lady Wisdom in Proverbs 9 and contrasted with the foolish woman in Proverbs 7. In this portrayal, the virtuous wife provides for the needs of her family, is a shrewd and successful businesswoman, and practices godly charity.

**Q:** Compare and contrast the primary threats for Old Testament youth with those for today’s youth.

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How did literary inclusion help laypersons in biblical times?

**A:** Scripture was normally heard, not read, because books were scarce and costly. Often, listeners could neither read nor write. Verbal cues, like inclusions in the text and the repetition of important ideas, helped listeners make sense of and retain what they heard.

**Q:** What are three characteristics of the writing of Proverbs’ central collections?

**A:** (1) A proverb makes a simple, unqualified assertion the reader is invited to examine in light of experience. (2) Some proverbs seem to contradict themselves. (3) Proverbs are general truths intended as guidelines rather than as universal absolutes.

**Q:** How did King Hezekiah’s scribes organize Solomon’s writings in Proverbs 25–29?

**A:** According to topic, such as the nature of kings, fools, the sluggard, and the evil man.

#### 6.2.3 — Explain why the tension between some proverbs reflects our understanding of the truth they convey.

Attempts to identify how the proverbs were originally organized have been largely unsuccessful. Individual proverbs usually stand on their own, and the final editor of the collections made little or no attempt to group them by subject or style. Therefore, it is more helpful to speak in terms of guiding themes that appear important to the writers.
