The first seven verses of Proverbs introduce the book, with verses 2–6 providing the general focus of all that follows. This opening section of Proverbs emphasizes the family more heavily than elsewhere. These parents, particularly the father, seek to guide their young son safely through adolescence into manhood. “Listen, my son, to your father’s instruction and do not forsake your mother’s teaching” (1:8). Derek Kidner points out two principal threats to a young person growing up: (1) the attraction of the gang (1:10–19; 2:12–15; 4:14–19), which appeals to the need for identity and power, and (2) the allure of sex (2:16–19), which demands immediate gratification (1985, 20). The first temptation tears at the fabric of society, the second at that of the home.

Specifically, the temptation of adultery or sex outside of marriage presents itself in terms of isolated pleasure. It promises the satisfaction of sexual pleasure with no accompanying relationship, commitment, obligation, or consequence. The teacher in Proverbs responds to this temptation by putting it back in its social context: sin is relational. No one sins in a vacuum, and sexual sin in particular has a dramatic impact on both family and community. Adultery results in a scattering of illegitimate children instead of a close nuclear family, broken trust, public shame, venereal disease, and the wrath of jealous spouses.

Today, addiction to pornography is a major problem in cultures where the Internet is easily available, even among Christians. One of its most destructive effects is that the person involved in pornography cannot help but bring aspects of this “relationless sexuality” into his or her relationship with a spouse. Thus, the real relationship is damaged and even destroyed for the sake of fantasy.

The teacher in Proverbs emphasizes that a better life option than gangs or fornication exists. That is, he wants his children and his readers to understand that wisdom and the fear of the Lord are viable alternatives to the ways of destruction. Each person must make his or her own choices. Yet this teacher is determined to positively influence those individual decisions.

Solomon’s Proverbs, Part 1 (1–9)

The central issue of Proverbs 1–9 (the first of Solomon’s collections) is the disparity between wisdom and folly, both of which are personified as women. Lady Wisdom is introduced in 1:20–33. Chapter 5 vividly contrasts the adulterous woman with the wife of one’s youth. Then, in chapter 9, Lady Wisdom is shown in opposition to “the woman Folly” (9:13).

This first section of Proverbs also introduces the sluggard or the lazy person. He procrastinates (6:9–10), fails to complete tasks (12:27; 19:24), and avoids anything unpleasant or burdensome (22:13). Consequently, he is restless (13:4; 21:25–26). He stands in sharp contrast to the industrious person. In wisdom literature, blessing is closely tied to prosperity and material fullness; therefore, industry is a key virtue. On the other hand, the lazy person is viewed as his own worst enemy.

Additionally, Proverbs 1–9 demonstrates the poetic device known as inclusion , which ties a group of texts together by repeating certain words, phrases, or ideas at the beginning and end of a book or section (see Lesson 1.3). For example, Proverbs 9:10 repeats a phrase found in 1:7 regarding the “fear of the Lord.” In conjunction with a similar statement in 31:30, this creates inclusion around both the first portion of the book and the book as a whole (Murphy 1990, 16). This establishment of verbal “bookends” is a common device in biblical writing to underscore a central idea that binds the other concepts together. Scripture was normally heard, not read, because books were scarce and costly and because listeners often could neither read nor write. Verbal cues, like inclusions in the text and the repetition of important ideas, helped listeners make sense of and retain what they were hearing.

Solomon’s Proverbs, Parts 2 and 3 (10:1–22:16; 25–29)

The central collections of Proverbs (chapters 10–29) stand in sharp contrast to the collection preceding them (1–9) and the two collections following (30–31). While the opening and closing collections of Proverbs are dominated by contemplative wisdom, these central collections consist entirely of short, concise, often self-contained sayings—that is, of proverbial wisdom. A number of characteristics are common to this kind of writing.

First, such a proverb makes a simple (sometimes overly simplistic) assertion that is unqualified, and the reader is then invited to examine it in light of personal experience.

Second, some proverbs seem to contradict themselves. For example, Proverbs 26:4–5 reads: “Do not answer a fool according to his folly, or you will be like him yourself. Answer a fool according to his folly, or he will be wise in his own eyes.”

In a more subtle and general sense, a degree of tension—almost contradiction—can also exist between two or more proverbial teachings. For example, wisdom writing consistently teaches that the wise will be blessed with prosperity and a good life, whereas the fool will be punished and the sluggard will find poverty. Yet the poor are presented as objects of God’s special concern (Proverbs 14:31; 17:5). This type of tension is inherent in the nature of a proverb, which is too short to encompass all of reality in a single saying.

This apparent tension within wisdom teachings reflects a problem not so much with the proverbs themselves as with our understanding of the truth they intend to convey. Proverbs reflect on the nature of relationships between people. Relationship, by definition, involves multiple wills, desires, levels of maturity, and so forth. Thus, a third characteristic of proverbs is that they are general truths intended as guidelines rather than as universal absolutes.

Put simply, proverbs are not promises. They are wise advice for ordering one’s life and dealings with other people in a way that should result in happiness. Still, following these guidelines faithfully cannot guarantee the outcome. Sin and free will are constant factors affecting the outcomes of our decisions.

I remember growing up in the church and listening to the anguished prayers of parents whose children had abandoned their faith. Often they would quote Proverbs 22:6: “Train a child in the way he should go, and when he is old he will not turn from it.” Then they would say, “I’m hanging on to God’s promise to bring my child back to Him.” The problem with this understanding, of course, is that God refuses to force anyone to accept Him. Our job is to rear our children in a godly fashion and to intercede for them, but neither we nor God Himself can ensure a positive outcome.

In addition to its switch to proverbial wisdom (in contrast to the contemplative discourses in the first collection), Solomon’s second collection utilizes the form of Hebrew poetry known as parallelism. Chapters 10–15 primarily use antithetic parallelism, where the second part of the proverb says the opposite of the first part. On the other hand, Proverbs 16–22:16 primarily use synonymous and synthetic parallelism, in which the second half of the proverb restates and builds on the first half.

The third collection of Solomon’s proverbs, gathered by King Hezekiah’s scribes in chapters 25–29, tends to be more organized. That is, the sayings are clustered according to their topics. We find small groups of proverbs on the nature of kings (25:2–7), fools (26:3–12), the sluggard (26:13–16), and the evil man (26:17–28).

Other Collections

The Two Collections of “the Wise” (22:17–24:34)

Two collections in Proverbs are attributed simply to “the wise.” They are somewhat different in tone and style from Solomon’s collections. Instead of the abstract statements of truth found before and after this section, this segment uses more of the personal appeal of a parent to a child. The admonition to “listen to your father, who gave you life, and do not despise your mother when she is old” (23:22) is reminiscent of Solomon’s writings in the first section of Proverbs. The sayings of the wise also use brief and vivid portraits to illustrate vices to avoid, such as the picture of the drunkard in 23:29–35 and the sluggard in 24:30–34.

Words of Agur (30)

Proverbs 30 begins with a sharp change of both style and tone that indicates a change of writers. Agur, son of Jakeh, offers a wisdom oracle.

Verse 4 proposes a set of questions whose answer can only be God, and verses 7–9 present the only prayer contained in Proverbs. The writer asks two things of the Lord: to be kept from lies and to receive neither wealth nor poverty. While the problems brought by poverty are more obvious, wealth also brings temptations. The wise person knows how to be content with “enough.”

The rest of the chapter consists of wisdom presented in a series of lists. Agur uses a common technique—numerical sayings such as “There are three things . . ., four”—to examine things that are never satisfied, are wonderful, perturb the earth, are little but wise, and are majestic.

A Tale of Two Women (31)

The final chapter of Proverbs may be divided into two sections. In verses 1–9, the Queen Mother advises her son, King Lemuel, on what it means to be a king. A king is ruined by spending his strength on women and drunkenness. Such a lifestyle causes him to “forget the law.” Deuteronomy 17:18–20 indicates that the king of Israel had a personal copy of the books of the Law so he could read it constantly and “learn to fear the Lord his God.” For the king, part of keeping the Law includes showing compassion and bringing justice to the oppressed.

Proverbs 31:10–31 is then a portrait of a godly wife and her administration of the home. It is an alphabetic acrostic poem in which each of the twenty-two lines begins with a successive letter of the Hebrew alphabet. The woman in the poem can be compared to Lady Wisdom in Proverbs 9 and contrasted with the foolish woman in Proverbs 7. In this portrayal, the virtuous wife provides for the needs of her family, is a shrewd and successful businesswoman, and practices godly charity.

**Q:** Compare and contrast the primary threats for Old Testament youth with those for today’s youth.

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How did literary inclusion help laypersons in biblical times?

**A:** Scripture was normally heard, not read, because books were scarce and costly. Often, listeners could neither read nor write. Verbal cues, like inclusions in the text and the repetition of important ideas, helped listeners make sense of and retain what they heard.

**Q:** What are three characteristics of the writing of Proverbs’ central collections?

**A:** (1) A proverb makes a simple, unqualified assertion the reader is invited to examine in light of experience. (2) Some proverbs seem to contradict themselves. (3) Proverbs are general truths intended as guidelines rather than as universal absolutes.

**Q:** How did King Hezekiah’s scribes organize Solomon’s writings in Proverbs 25–29?

**A:** According to topic, such as the nature of kings, fools, the sluggard, and the evil man.

#### 6.2.4 — Compare and contrast the three collections of Solomon’s proverbs.

Attempts to identify how the proverbs were originally organized have been largely unsuccessful. Individual proverbs usually stand on their own, and the final editor of the collections made little or no attempt to group them by subject or style. Therefore, it is more helpful to speak in terms of guiding themes that appear important to the writers.

**Q:** What are three characteristics of the writing of Proverbs’ central collections?

**A:** (1) A proverb makes a simple, unqualified assertion the reader is invited to examine in light of experience. (2) Some proverbs seem to contradict themselves. (3) Proverbs are general truths intended as guidelines rather than as universal absolutes.

**Q:** How did King Hezekiah’s scribes organize Solomon’s writings in Proverbs 25–29?

**A:** According to topic, such as the nature of kings, fools, the sluggard, and the evil man.

### Theology of Proverbs, Part 1

#### 6.3.1 — Define Torah and the concept of retributional justice.

The Reality of Retributional Justice

To properly understand the origins of Proverbs’ wisdom teachings, we must begin with the law of Moses. God gave this law (translated from the Hebrew word Torah ) when He established a nation for Himself by bringing the people of Israel out of captivity in Egypt. For the first time since the Garden of Eden, God came to dwell with human beings. Israel formed a covenant community, a people of God, and the Lord dwelt in their midst.

Yet “law” is only part of the meaning of Torah . Torah can also be defined as the “teaching” and “instruction” for how to live with God in the camp and as a member of the new covenant community. Torah is not burdensome or negative but is the key to successfully living with both God and neighbor. Keeping the Torah is the people’s grateful response to God who freed them from servitude and made a personal covenant with them.

As part of God’s covenant with His people, He outlines the consequences of obedience and disobedience (Deuteronomy 28–30). If they keep the covenant, they will be blessed. If not, they will bring a curse on themselves. Retributional justice is the term for the reality that if individuals are obedient and righteous they will be blessed, but if they are disobedient and evil they will be cursed. Such an understanding of covenant life reveals some basic truths about God’s character: He is just, is reliable, and takes an active hand in our lives.

The covenant and the Torah have to do with two basic, interconnected relationships: (1) our relationship with God and (2) our relationship with our neighbors. The book of Proverbs explores this second relationship and our choices as to how we will live with our neighbors. The clear expectation of Proverbs is that if we make righteous choices, we will be blessed with healthy relationships and a full, prosperous life. However, if we choose to violate our relationship with the community through rebellion, adultery, dishonesty, gossip, or a quarrelsome spirit, we will reap the consequences. The writers of Proverbs believe that true prosperity can never be found alone but always exists in the context of relationships.

Proverbs leaves open the question of how active a part the Lord plays in bringing about the consequences of our choices. On one hand, He clearly takes an active part in some instances; for example, “The Lord’s curse is on the house of the wicked, but he blesses the home of the righteous” (3:33). On the other hand, relationships among people function in such a way that God need not take an active role to produce blessing and cursing. Consider these examples: “One man gives freely, yet gains even more; another withholds unduly, but comes to poverty. A generous man will prosper; he who refreshes others will himself be refreshed. People curse the man who hoards grain, but blessing crowns him who is willing to sell” (11:24–26). “A hot-tempered man stirs up dissension, but a patient man calms a quarrel” (15:18). In this way, blessings and curses result from choices in our relationships.

Stability in an Uncertain World

Identifying the social setting of Proverbs is challenging. This is partly because the book contains at least seven separate collections brought together over an extended period of time. In addition to the varied backgrounds of its different parts, the book also deals with a wide variety of subject matter. Some of the writings focus on the royal court, but other material is set in the home, street, field, or marketplace.

Ellen Davis (2000) suggests that the proverbs were written to provide stability and safety in times of transition and crisis. When the nation shifted from an unstructured tribal system to a formal monarchy, the emphasis was on living peacefully under a king. But with the Exile, the dispersion of the Jewish people, and the destruction of the monarchy, the center of focus returned to the household and local community. We find proverbs on both topics in the book of Proverbs.

Davis also calls attention to the important role that women play in the Proverbs, much more so than in other Old Testament books. Women are prominent throughout the book: the mother guiding her child; Lady Wisdom; the adulterous and foolish woman; the wife worthy of her husband’s loyalty; the Queen Mother instructing her son; and the virtuous woman running the house, contributing to commerce, and practicing charity. The notability of women in the Proverbs, argues Davis, demonstrates that proverbial materials were gathered at the end of the monarchy. Although Solomon played a major role in introducing wisdom as formal study, proverbial wisdom originally arose and finds its most enduring expression in the home and community. With the Exile and the loss of an independent monarchy, the proverb returns to its roots in the home (Davis 2000, 13–18).

**Q:** Does the concept of retributional justice still apply?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** What does God’s covenant with His people reveal about His character?

**A:** Covenant life reveals God as just, reliable, and active in our lives.

**Q:** Why do women play a notable role in Proverbs?

**A:** At the end of the Hebrew monarchy, emphasis turned to home, family, and community, environments in which women have a major part. The collections in Proverbs may have been gathered around this time.

### Theology of Proverbs, Part 2

#### 6.4.1 — Discuss the relationship between our choices and the direction of our lives.

Central to the book of Proverbs is the idea of choice. Readers are presented with two paths between which they are constantly asked to choose: one leads to life, and the other to death (Proverbs 2:21–22). This notion that our choices set the direction of our lives echoes Moses’ summation of the terms of the Torah in Deuteronomy. After laying out all the conditions of the covenant before the people, he called them to make a decision in response: “I have set before you life and death, blessings and curses. Now choose life, so that you and your children may live” (Deuteronomy 30:19).

Reducing life to such simple terms as the choice between good and evil brings basic truths about human nature into sharp relief. However, this reduction also tends to oversimplify. That is, because Proverbs speaks in broad terms, it tends to gloss over the uncertainties and ambiguities of life. While we may categorize people as introverts or extroverts, realists or idealists, optimists or pessimists, dog lovers or cat lovers, emotional individuals or intellectuals, rarely is a person always one or the other. Thus, while Job and Ecclesiastes explore life’s exceptions and ambiguities, Proverbs provides the foundation for those explorations by observing the “either/or” tendencies in life. In Proverbs, a person simply chooses either conduct that leads to life or conduct that leads to death.

Choices That Lead to Life

Honoring Parents

The process of learning wisdom begins in the home with parents preparing their children for life. Proverbs 1–9 takes the form of parents instructing a son, with the phrase “my son” recurring throughout. The central issue in determining the effectiveness of teaching wisdom in the home is that of authority. According to Proverbs, wisdom can be acquired only by children who are obedient and respectful. Honoring one’s parents is a foundation stone of the admonition, “Listen, my son, to your father’s instruction and do not forsake your mother’s teaching” (1:8). Wisdom cannot be passed on if the source of the wisdom is not honored and listened to.

Discipline

A related theme is discipline (Berry 1995, 136). Discipline, including physical punishment, was seen as beneficial for ending folly and promoting wisdom. Acceptance or rejection of discipline, in great part, determines whether a person will be wise or foolish, successful or unsuccessful in life (Proverbs 10:17; 15:10; 19:25).

Fidelity

Fundamental to the process of training a child to be wise is a stable home environment. How can parents teach what they do not know? Proverbs devotes a great deal of time to the relationship between a husband and wife and their need to continue maturing in their relationship. Marital fidelity is central because nothing is more destructive to the marriage relationship than the lack of fidelity. A major portion of Proverbs focuses on helping a young man successfully transition from being an obedient child, to being a wise adult, to choosing and remaining loyal to a wise wife. “He who finds a wife finds what is good and receives favor from the Lord” (18:22; see also 19:14). “May your fountain be blessed, and may you rejoice in the wife of your youth. A loving doe, a graceful deer—may her breasts satisfy you always, may you ever be captivated by her love” (5:18–19). Without fidelity, the home is destroyed and wisdom training breaks down.

Maturity and Self-Control

To be successful in both marriage and life, self-control and subordination of overly zealous passions are essential. “Better a patient man than a warrior, a man who controls his temper than one who takes a city” (16:32). Particularly difficult is the control of the tongue: “When words are many, sin is not absent, but he who holds his tongue is wise” (10:19). “He who guards his mouth and his tongue keeps himself from calamity” (21:23; see also 15:4; 18:21). Much of life comes down to the basic issue of personal maturity and exercise of self-control. This suggests that when we have consistent problems in our relationships, we should look at ourselves as the root cause.

Industry and Integrity

In addition to a successful home life, functioning in society is important. The wise person is commended for honesty: “Truthful lips endure forever, but a lying tongue lasts only a moment” (12:19). The reputation for being a person who keeps his or her word is foundational to prosperity in business. The wise are also encouraged to work hard and be industrious: “Diligent hands will rule, but laziness ends in slave labor” (12:24).

Conduct That Leads to Death

Rebellion

As we might expect from Proverbs’ emphasis on choosing between two ways, many behaviors that lead to life find their opposites in ways that promote death. While honoring parents and accepting godly discipline are crucial for children to acquire wisdom, rebellion is the most destructive sin in a child: “A wise son heeds his father’s instruction, but a mocker does not listen to rebuke” (13:1).

Adultery

Adultery is one of the most destructive sins because it attacks the family, thus undermining the foundation on which society is built. Not surprisingly, in Proverbs the adulterous woman is directly contrasted with Lady Wisdom. “A prostitute is a deep pit and a wayward wife is a narrow well. Like a bandit she lies in wait, and multiplies the unfaithful among men” (23:27–28). Adultery also stirs up jealousy and revenge: “Jealousy arouses a husband’s fury, and he will show no mercy when he takes revenge” (6:34). Even if the couple remain married, trust and mutual respect have been destroyed and are nearly impossible to fully rebuild. In the New Testament, Paul talks about the unique nature of sexual immorality:

Do you not know that he who unites himself with a prostitute is one with her in body? For it is said, “The two will become one flesh.” . . . Flee from sexual immorality. All other sins a man commits are outside his body, but he who sins sexually sins against his own body. (1 Corinthians 6:16, 18)

We should not be surprised that Proverbs gives much attention to adultery. Sexual sin violates the deepest expression of human covenant and relationship, that is, marriage. The person who yields to adultery is “like a bird darting into a snare, little knowing it will cost him his life” (Proverbs 7:23). Solomon says of the adulterous woman, “Her house is a highway to the grave, leading down to the chambers of death” (7:27).

Lack of Self-control

The inability to control emotions, particularly anger, is also highly destructive to a person’s relationships. Because of his lack of self-control, “a hot-tempered man must pay the penalty; if you rescue him, you will have to do it again” (19:19). The wise person is the one who develops self-control.

Drunkenness

Drunkenness also results from a lack of self-restraint. Like wrath, drunkenness has its roots in an unwillingness to exercise self-control.

Laziness

Laziness places a burden on the family and the society as a whole and makes prosperity and blessing impossible. “A little sleep, a little slumber, a little folding of the hands to rest—and poverty will come on you like a bandit and scarcity like an armed man” (6:10–11). “As vinegar to the teeth and smoke to the eyes, so is a sluggard to those who send him” (10:26). If wrath and drunkenness reveal a lack of self-control, laziness shows a lack of self-discipline.

Where Heaven Meets Earth: Lady Wisdom and the Virtuous Woman

Lady Wisdom

One of the most unique and interesting features of Proverbs is the personification of wisdom. When we are first introduced to Lady Wisdom in Proverbs 1:20–33, she is found in public life, waiting for the young men who are, for the first time, no longer under parental supervision. In short, Lady Wisdom takes over the job of parenting, instructing and guiding the young person as she or he confronts life’s opportunities and risks. Her principal target in ancient Israel was the young male who often had far greater freedom and was exposed to far greater dangers than was a young woman.

In Proverbs 8, Lady Wisdom’s message is much more universal and is directed to all who will listen. The birth of Wisdom is described, as is her role as witness to God’s acts of creation. Many of Israel’s neighbors had goddesses as well as gods, but that is not the function Wisdom portrays in Proverbs. She is not a goddess but the first of God’s creations and a witness to all that follows. She sees and understands all God is doing but is not an active agent in that creation.

In Proverbs 9:1–6, Lady Wisdom is portrayed as a wealthy woman with a large mansion and many servants; she prepares a feast for all who are willing to partake. Lady Folly is her chief competitor for the attentions of young men who pass. Folly also offers a feast; however, its attraction is not that the food is special but that it is illicit and mysterious. “Stolen waters are sweet; food eaten in secret is delicious!” The description of the two feasts distinguishes the wise from the foolish. The wise willingly accept correction, discipline, and even rebuke, but the foolish admit to no wrongdoing. A teachable spirit, then, is the key to gaining wisdom.

The Virtuous Woman

Although much of the wisdom training offered in Proverbs is directed toward young men, in chapter 31 we see the outcome of a woman who pursues wisdom in her own life. In that time and culture, her own journey was far more likely to take place in the home than in public life. She trained from an early age to take on the many tasks necessary to running a household. The wife of “noble character” (v. 10) is the one who has taken up her position with its responsibilities. The Hebrew word translated “noble” or “virtuous” is hayil , which when applied to a warrior is translated “strong” or “valiant.”

In many ways, the godly household is at the center of society. The valiant wife is essential to her husband’s success. She provides for the needs of the family, adds to their economic success, and gains honor for them in the eyes of the community. In short, the woman of valor performs for the family many of the same things Lady Wisdom offers to the community at large.

**Q:** How is Proverbs’ perspective on good and evil different from that of Job or Ecclesiastes?

**A:** The Proverbs collections tend to oversimplify the choice between good and evil, focusing instead on life’s “either/or” tendencies. Both Job and Ecclesiastes address the exceptions and ambiguities in life, but Proverbs does not.

**Q:** What character traits lead to successful living?

**A:** Those successful in life exhibit such characteristics as honor for parents, discipline, fidelity, maturity, self-control, industry, and integrity.

**Q:** What behavior is destructive to life?

**A:** Behavior destructive to life includes rebellion, adultery, lack of self-control, drunkenness, and laziness.

**Q:** What was the typical life of women at the time Proverbs was written?

**A:** In Bible times, a woman’s domain was primarily the home, not public life. From her youth, she was trained in the many tasks of managing a household. She provided for the needs of the family, adding to their finances, and gaining honor for them in the community. 7 CHAPTER

#### 6.4.3 — Discuss the results of destructive life choices.

Central to the book of Proverbs is the idea of choice. Readers are presented with two paths between which they are constantly asked to choose: one leads to life, and the other to death (Proverbs 2:21–22). This notion that our choices set the direction of our lives echoes Moses’ summation of the terms of the Torah in Deuteronomy. After laying out all the conditions of the covenant before the people, he called them to make a decision in response: “I have set before you life and death, blessings and curses. Now choose life, so that you and your children may live” (Deuteronomy 30:19).

Reducing life to such simple terms as the choice between good and evil brings basic truths about human nature into sharp relief. However, this reduction also tends to oversimplify. That is, because Proverbs speaks in broad terms, it tends to gloss over the uncertainties and ambiguities of life. While we may categorize people as introverts or extroverts, realists or idealists, optimists or pessimists, dog lovers or cat lovers, emotional individuals or intellectuals, rarely is a person always one or the other. Thus, while Job and Ecclesiastes explore life’s exceptions and ambiguities, Proverbs provides the foundation for those explorations by observing the “either/or” tendencies in life. In Proverbs, a person simply chooses either conduct that leads to life or conduct that leads to death.

Choices That Lead to Life

Honoring Parents

The process of learning wisdom begins in the home with parents preparing their children for life. Proverbs 1–9 takes the form of parents instructing a son, with the phrase “my son” recurring throughout. The central issue in determining the effectiveness of teaching wisdom in the home is that of authority. According to Proverbs, wisdom can be acquired only by children who are obedient and respectful. Honoring one’s parents is a foundation stone of the admonition, “Listen, my son, to your father’s instruction and do not forsake your mother’s teaching” (1:8). Wisdom cannot be passed on if the source of the wisdom is not honored and listened to.

Discipline

A related theme is discipline (Berry 1995, 136). Discipline, including physical punishment, was seen as beneficial for ending folly and promoting wisdom. Acceptance or rejection of discipline, in great part, determines whether a person will be wise or foolish, successful or unsuccessful in life (Proverbs 10:17; 15:10; 19:25).

Fidelity

Fundamental to the process of training a child to be wise is a stable home environment. How can parents teach what they do not know? Proverbs devotes a great deal of time to the relationship between a husband and wife and their need to continue maturing in their relationship. Marital fidelity is central because nothing is more destructive to the marriage relationship than the lack of fidelity. A major portion of Proverbs focuses on helping a young man successfully transition from being an obedient child, to being a wise adult, to choosing and remaining loyal to a wise wife. “He who finds a wife finds what is good and receives favor from the Lord” (18:22; see also 19:14). “May your fountain be blessed, and may you rejoice in the wife of your youth. A loving doe, a graceful deer—may her breasts satisfy you always, may you ever be captivated by her love” (5:18–19). Without fidelity, the home is destroyed and wisdom training breaks down.

Maturity and Self-Control

To be successful in both marriage and life, self-control and subordination of overly zealous passions are essential. “Better a patient man than a warrior, a man who controls his temper than one who takes a city” (16:32). Particularly difficult is the control of the tongue: “When words are many, sin is not absent, but he who holds his tongue is wise” (10:19). “He who guards his mouth and his tongue keeps himself from calamity” (21:23; see also 15:4; 18:21). Much of life comes down to the basic issue of personal maturity and exercise of self-control. This suggests that when we have consistent problems in our relationships, we should look at ourselves as the root cause.

Industry and Integrity

In addition to a successful home life, functioning in society is important. The wise person is commended for honesty: “Truthful lips endure forever, but a lying tongue lasts only a moment” (12:19). The reputation for being a person who keeps his or her word is foundational to prosperity in business. The wise are also encouraged to work hard and be industrious: “Diligent hands will rule, but laziness ends in slave labor” (12:24).

Conduct That Leads to Death

Rebellion

As we might expect from Proverbs’ emphasis on choosing between two ways, many behaviors that lead to life find their opposites in ways that promote death. While honoring parents and accepting godly discipline are crucial for children to acquire wisdom, rebellion is the most destructive sin in a child: “A wise son heeds his father’s instruction, but a mocker does not listen to rebuke” (13:1).

Adultery

Adultery is one of the most destructive sins because it attacks the family, thus undermining the foundation on which society is built. Not surprisingly, in Proverbs the adulterous woman is directly contrasted with Lady Wisdom. “A prostitute is a deep pit and a wayward wife is a narrow well. Like a bandit she lies in wait, and multiplies the unfaithful among men” (23:27–28). Adultery also stirs up jealousy and revenge: “Jealousy arouses a husband’s fury, and he will show no mercy when he takes revenge” (6:34). Even if the couple remain married, trust and mutual respect have been destroyed and are nearly impossible to fully rebuild. In the New Testament, Paul talks about the unique nature of sexual immorality:

Do you not know that he who unites himself with a prostitute is one with her in body? For it is said, “The two will become one flesh.” . . . Flee from sexual immorality. All other sins a man commits are outside his body, but he who sins sexually sins against his own body. (1 Corinthians 6:16, 18)

We should not be surprised that Proverbs gives much attention to adultery. Sexual sin violates the deepest expression of human covenant and relationship, that is, marriage. The person who yields to adultery is “like a bird darting into a snare, little knowing it will cost him his life” (Proverbs 7:23). Solomon says of the adulterous woman, “Her house is a highway to the grave, leading down to the chambers of death” (7:27).

Lack of Self-control

The inability to control emotions, particularly anger, is also highly destructive to a person’s relationships. Because of his lack of self-control, “a hot-tempered man must pay the penalty; if you rescue him, you will have to do it again” (19:19). The wise person is the one who develops self-control.

Drunkenness

Drunkenness also results from a lack of self-restraint. Like wrath, drunkenness has its roots in an unwillingness to exercise self-control.

Laziness

Laziness places a burden on the family and the society as a whole and makes prosperity and blessing impossible. “A little sleep, a little slumber, a little folding of the hands to rest—and poverty will come on you like a bandit and scarcity like an armed man” (6:10–11). “As vinegar to the teeth and smoke to the eyes, so is a sluggard to those who send him” (10:26). If wrath and drunkenness reveal a lack of self-control, laziness shows a lack of self-discipline.

Where Heaven Meets Earth: Lady Wisdom and the Virtuous Woman

Lady Wisdom

One of the most unique and interesting features of Proverbs is the personification of wisdom. When we are first introduced to Lady Wisdom in Proverbs 1:20–33, she is found in public life, waiting for the young men who are, for the first time, no longer under parental supervision. In short, Lady Wisdom takes over the job of parenting, instructing and guiding the young person as she or he confronts life’s opportunities and risks. Her principal target in ancient Israel was the young male who often had far greater freedom and was exposed to far greater dangers than was a young woman.

In Proverbs 8, Lady Wisdom’s message is much more universal and is directed to all who will listen. The birth of Wisdom is described, as is her role as witness to God’s acts of creation. Many of Israel’s neighbors had goddesses as well as gods, but that is not the function Wisdom portrays in Proverbs. She is not a goddess but the first of God’s creations and a witness to all that follows. She sees and understands all God is doing but is not an active agent in that creation.

In Proverbs 9:1–6, Lady Wisdom is portrayed as a wealthy woman with a large mansion and many servants; she prepares a feast for all who are willing to partake. Lady Folly is her chief competitor for the attentions of young men who pass. Folly also offers a feast; however, its attraction is not that the food is special but that it is illicit and mysterious. “Stolen waters are sweet; food eaten in secret is delicious!” The description of the two feasts distinguishes the wise from the foolish. The wise willingly accept correction, discipline, and even rebuke, but the foolish admit to no wrongdoing. A teachable spirit, then, is the key to gaining wisdom.

The Virtuous Woman

Although much of the wisdom training offered in Proverbs is directed toward young men, in chapter 31 we see the outcome of a woman who pursues wisdom in her own life. In that time and culture, her own journey was far more likely to take place in the home than in public life. She trained from an early age to take on the many tasks necessary to running a household. The wife of “noble character” (v. 10) is the one who has taken up her position with its responsibilities. The Hebrew word translated “noble” or “virtuous” is hayil , which when applied to a warrior is translated “strong” or “valiant.”

In many ways, the godly household is at the center of society. The valiant wife is essential to her husband’s success. She provides for the needs of the family, adds to their economic success, and gains honor for them in the eyes of the community. In short, the woman of valor performs for the family many of the same things Lady Wisdom offers to the community at large.

**Q:** How is Proverbs’ perspective on good and evil different from that of Job or Ecclesiastes?

**A:** The Proverbs collections tend to oversimplify the choice between good and evil, focusing instead on life’s “either/or” tendencies. Both Job and Ecclesiastes address the exceptions and ambiguities in life, but Proverbs does not.

**Q:** What character traits lead to successful living?

**A:** Those successful in life exhibit such characteristics as honor for parents, discipline, fidelity, maturity, self-control, industry, and integrity.

**Q:** What behavior is destructive to life?

**A:** Behavior destructive to life includes rebellion, adultery, lack of self-control, drunkenness, and laziness.

**Q:** What was the typical life of women at the time Proverbs was written?

**A:** In Bible times, a woman’s domain was primarily the home, not public life. From her youth, she was trained in the many tasks of managing a household. She provided for the needs of the family, adding to their finances, and gaining honor for them in the community. 7 CHAPTER

**A:** The Proverbs collections tend to oversimplify the choice between good and evil, focusing instead on life’s “either/or” tendencies. Both Job and Ecclesiastes address the exceptions and ambiguities in life, but Proverbs does not.

**Q:** What character traits lead to successful living?

**A:** Those successful in life exhibit such characteristics as honor for parents, discipline, fidelity, maturity, self-control, industry, and integrity.

**Q:** What behavior is destructive to life?

**A:** Behavior destructive to life includes rebellion, adultery, lack of self-control, drunkenness, and laziness.

**Q:** What was the typical life of women at the time Proverbs was written?

**A:** In Bible times, a woman’s domain was primarily the home, not public life. From her youth, she was trained in the many tasks of managing a household. She provided for the needs of the family, adding to their finances, and gaining honor for them in the community. 7 CHAPTER

## 7 - The Book of Ecclesiastes

### Introduction to Ecclesiastes

#### 7.1.1

Purpose

Of all of the Old Testament wisdom books, Ecclesiastes undertakes by far the most ambitious task. The writers of Proverbs set out to explore and describe the ways to find and maintain life-securing knowledge. The writer of Job provides a resource for those enduring extreme suffering. Both the writers of Proverbs and the writer of Job have ultimate confidence in (1) their relationship with God (even though for Job this is severely tested) and (2) human wisdom and understanding as a means of gaining success in life (again, Job shares this view and, in the end, is justified).

However, the writer of Ecclesiastes rejects the ideas that God may be known and that a person can have confidence in the ability of human wisdom to bring happiness or sustain life. He explores the limits of human wisdom and experience. All three writers agree that God is in control of all that happens, but for the writer of Ecclesiastes, God’s ways are mysterious and unknowable.

Authorship

The title Ecclesiastes is the Greek equivalent of the Hebrew Qoheleth . Both the Greek root word ekklesia and the Hebrew root word qahal mean a “gathering” or “assembly” and are the words that we sometimes translate as church . So the title Ecclesiastes is literally the “one who assembles” or the “one who calls together.” Both Jerome in the fourth century and Martin Luther in the sixteenth century translated the title of the book as simply “the Preacher.”

The writer of Ecclesiastes is never directly identified. According to rabbinic tradition, Solomon wrote the Song of Solomon in his youth, Proverbs in his middle age, and Ecclesiastes when he was old. We are told that the Preacher is a son of David (1:1) and king over Jerusalem (1:12), but we are not told which son of David he is. We are also told he had greater wisdom (1:16) and wealth (2:7, 9) than “all that were in Jerusalem before me.” Such a statement seems odd for Solomon since he was only the third king of Israel and the second to occupy Jerusalem. On the other hand, it would also be strange for any king following Solomon to claim to have surpassed him in these virtues. Certainly the references to wealth, building, wisdom, and sexual pleasure all point to the reign of Solomon and the writer seems, at the very least, to be using Solomon as his model.

It is possible that the writer of Ecclesiastes is King Solomon, a later king of Israel, or a combination of writers whose work was later collected and edited. But regardless of authorship, the Preacher is intended to remind the reader of the wisdom and wealth of King Solomon.

Structure

Attempts to identify an overall structure to the book of Ecclesiastes have been largely unsuccessful, although it does contain a prologue (1:1–11) and an epilogue (12:9–14). The prologue sets the tone for Ecclesiastes, addressing three principles explored throughout the book:

1. Everything in life is finally meaningless and without real purpose.

2. Nothing under the sun is truly new.

3. Nothing we do in life will endure or be remembered.

The epilogue places the whole enterprise of seeking to understand the mysteries of life in doubt and concludes, in light of such uncertainty, that the best course is for us to “fear God and keep His commandments.”

Kathleen Farmer has suggested that the book can be roughly divided into two sections (1991, 151). Chapters 1–6 ask the question, “What is good in life?” and then set out to test different things thought to bring contentment. Chapters 7–12 are more general and look at the question of human knowing. Farmer understands the basic message of the first part of the book to be, “Hard work and wisdom are good,” while that of the second part is, “Who may know God? No one!” At the center of the book, acting as a transition from one part to the next, is Ecclesiastes 6:12: “Who knows what is good for a man in life, during the few and meaningless days he passes through like a shadow? Who can tell him what will happen under the sun after he is gone?” Farmer notes the basic pattern of Ecclesiastes as moving from specific studies of what gives life meaning to more general conclusions about life.

Most contemporary commentators have abandoned attempts to devise a more detailed analysis of the structure of the book, opting instead for a development of themes that run throughout the work.

Content

While Ecclesiastes does not have a structured layout, we can identify principal elements of the study. The first part of the book considers all the things thought to bring meaning and happiness to life. The writer explores the rewards of wisdom (1:12–18; 2:12–16), of pursuing pleasure (2:1–11), of hard work (2:17–26), and of wealth (5:8–6:11). The problem with these pursuits is that they cannot provide contentment and satisfaction. It is not that they are bad. On the contrary, the Preacher affirms that we can do nothing better than to enjoy them (2:24; 5:18–19). But by themselves, they cannot give meaning to life.

A second topic the writer addresses is the orderliness of creation. Everything is in God’s hands. Life is filled with the rhythms of God’s creation (1:3–11), and everything has its appointed time and season (3:1–17). Despite our doubts about its meaningfulness, life is not chaotic and purposeless, for God ordains all that happens. The problem is that, from our limited perspective, we often cannot see God’s plan or understand His purposes. “He has made everything beautiful in its time. He has also set eternity in the hearts of men; yet they cannot fathom what God has done from the beginning to end” (3:11). There may be a time for everything, but it is not always possible for human beings to discern those times.

Then, beginning with chapter 7, the writer changes his focus to consider the value of wisdom (7:1–8:17). Much of what he says would sound perfectly natural in the book of Proverbs. But, finally, even the pursuit of wisdom is limited because it does not always seem to work. “There is something else meaningless that occurs on earth: righteous men who get what the wicked deserve, and wicked men who get what the righteous deserve. This too, I say, is meaningless” (8:14).

A fourth topic of the book is death. Death ends a person’s plans and work. Everyone dies—the rich and the poor, the wise and the foolish, the good and the bad (3:18–22; 9:1–12). If life simply ends in death, then it is meaningless.

We have already alluded to a fifth theme, that of finding enjoyment in everyday activities. Because pleasure is temporary, God’s plans are veiled, wisdom is limited, and death ends it all, we should enjoy what we do see and what we are given to the best of our ability. This is at least a partial solution to the meaninglessness the Preacher finds in life.

The Preacher closes his study by directing us back to the limitless One: “Now all has been heard; here is the conclusion of the matter: Fear God and keep his commandments, for this is the whole duty of man. For God will bring every deed into judgment, including every hidden thing, whether it is good or evil” (12:13–14).

**Q:** Why was Ecclesiastes written?

**A:** To explore the limits of human wisdom and experience and to show that, in the writer’s opinion, God’s ways are unknowable

**Q:** What are the varying views regarding who wrote Ecclesiastes?

**A:** Rabbinic tradition points to Solomon as the unidentified author. Some references support this, but others do not. The writer of Ecclesiastes could be King Solomon, a later king of Israel, or a combination of writers whose work was later collected and edited.

**A:** Answers will vary.

### Interpretation of Ecclesiastes

#### 7.2.1 — Explain the contradiction of Ecclesiastes.

The Contradiction of Ecclesiastes

More than any other biblical writer, the Preacher explores both sides of the blessings promised by wisdom. As with the book of Job, the writer of Ecclesiastes struggles to understand experiences that seem to contradict the teachings of traditional wisdom. Whereas Job questions conventional wisdom while his friends defend it, however, the Preacher seems to argue for both sides of the issue.

This addressing of both sides of an argument is the essential contradiction of the book. On one hand, the Preacher concludes that pursuing wisdom brings only sorrow and grief (1:12–18) and hard work causes pain and steals away rest (2:17–23). On the other hand, he affirms that wisdom “is better than folly,” (2:13) and “a man can do nothing better than . . . find satisfaction in his work” (2:24). The writer seems uncertain whether pursuing wisdom and working hard have value in making life happy and meaningful.

The writer of Ecclesiastes is conflicted even about the material blessings that result from following the teachings of wisdom. “As goods increase, so do those who consume them. And what benefit are they to the owner except to feast his eyes on them? The sleep of a laborer is sweet, whether he eats little or much, but the abundance of a rich man permits him no sleep” (5:11–12).

Interpreting Ecclesiastes

Interpreting and applying the book of Ecclesiastes have traditionally been extremely difficult. Michael Eaton has surveyed a number of different interpretations of the book (1983, 36–38), including the following four.

Spiritualized Interpretation

First, Jewish commentators and early Christian interpreters often opted for a spiritualized interpretation. For example, the Targum (a paraphrased translation used by rabbis in the synagogue) interprets Ecclesiastes 9:7 as a reference to the reward of the righteous for charity to the poor. The Targum paraphrases the verse as follows:

Solomon said by the spirit of prophecy before Jehovah, “The Lord of the world shall say to all the righteous one by one, Go taste with joy thy bread which has been given to thee on account of the bread which thou hast given to the poor and the unfortunate who were hungry, and drink with good heart thy wine, which is hidden for thee in the Garden of Eden, for the wine which thou hast mingled for the poor and needy who were thirsty, for already thy good work has been pleasing before Jehovah.”

Ecclesiastes as a Debate

A second possible interpretation is to understand the book as a dialogue between two people with opposing views. According to this understanding, the book takes the form of a debate between two sages or between a teacher and his student. Michael V. Fox sees in Ecclesiastes a direct challenge to conventional wisdom using a “Yes, but” style (Fox 1989, 19–28). However, Fox does not explain why such a challenge would be included in the canon.

Evangelistic Purpose

A third suggestion is that the Preacher’s pessimism serves an evangelistic purpose. Speaking to those who pursue worldly gain, the Preacher asks, “What would life really be like if you had everything the world has to offer?” The clear message is that worldly riches are empty and meaningless. In the fourth century, Jerome wrote a commentary on Ecclesiastes for the express purpose of convincing a wealthy Roman woman to exchange the pleasures offered by the world for a monastic life. In a variation of that theme, Bible teacher Charles Swindoll (1985) has suggested that Ecclesiastes is the journal of a successful man. That is, the writer of Ecclesiastes was in a position to try every good thing in life, but he concluded that all of it was “chasing after the wind” (Ecclesiastes 1:14). The author’s discovery was not that these things were evil or did not come from God or brought no pleasure, but that, in themselves, they gave no ultimate meaning to life.

Christians generally interpret Ecclesiastes evangelistically. By exploring human wisdom and the things of life that bring pleasure and a sense of purpose, the writer concludes that nothing in this world has ultimate meaning in the face of eternity. Death limits all earthly pursuits. Ralph Wardlaw maintains that Ecclesiastes points to the cross in anticipation of that which transcends present life. According to such a view, the writer is not so much a skeptic as one who takes this world’s point of view in order to point beyond it.

The Limitations of Human Reasoning

A fourth idea is that the book presents the limitations of human reasoning. For example, the Scofield Study Bible introduces the book of Ecclesiastes by saying,

Ecclesiastes is the book of man “under the sun” reasoning about life. The philosophy it sets forth, which makes no claim to revelation but which inspiration records for our instruction, presents the world-view of one of the wisest of men, who knows that there is a holy God and that He will bring everything into judgment.

According to this view, the value of the book is that it shows our understanding to be limited, so we are called to trust and obey God by faith, even when it seems He is not working in our lives. The phrase under the sun refers to the purely human perspective on truth. However, while the book does underscore the limits that death places on our understanding, the writer, far from advocating blind faith, continues to question and probe the meaning of life.

A basic question Ecclesiastes raises, then, is, “What role do experience and observation of the world around us play in shaping our faith?” No matter how we finally understand this internal tension in Ecclesiastes, one important conclusion we can draw is that there is more to a blessed life than simply applying the wisdom tradition as though it were some kind of magic formula. Also, as the Preacher explores the seeming contradiction between faith and experience, it is important to note that he neither compromises his faith nor is willing to be dishonest about what he sees around him. Perhaps the most challenging aspect of Ecclesiastes is the author’s unwillingness to provide us with clean, easy answers.

Pessimist or Preacher of Joy?

J. Stafford Wright suggested that the key to understanding Ecclesiastes is looking at its opening and conclusion (1946, 18–34). Ecclesiastes opens by saying everything is meaningless. It concludes by saying, “Fear God and keep his commandments, for this is the whole duty of man” (12:13). In addition, several strategically placed refrains are repeated throughout the book. Wright identifies two such refrains: All of life’s pursuits are “meaningless,” and “A man can do nothing better than to eat and drink and find satisfaction in his work” (2:24; 3:12–13; 3:22; 5:18–19; 8:15; 9:7–9).

If everything in life is meaningless, why does the Preacher tell us to enjoy them? Wright argues that worldly pursuits neither reveal God’s hidden plan nor explain the meaning of life. That mystery lies with God. However, they are gifts from God and, as such, should be enjoyed.

R. N. Whybray points out that Ecclesiastes is not completely negative. At the end of the discourse on the meaninglessness of life are seven exhortations to enjoy life (2:24; 3:12; 3:22; 5:17; 8:15; 9:7–9; 11:7–12:1). Furthermore, the call to joy becomes more elaborate and demanding as one moves through the book (1982, 87–98). The first call to joy is a simple injunction: “A man can do nothing better than to eat and drink and find satisfaction in his work” (2:24). The final and most elaborate expression makes it clear that we should take joy in God’s blessings even though life is filled with vanity:

Light is sweet, and it pleases the eye to see the sun. However many years a man may live, let him enjoy them all. But let him remember the days of darkness, for they will be many. Everything to come is meaningless. . . . So then, banish anxiety from your heart and cast off the troubles of your body, for youth and vigor are meaningless. (11:7–8, 10)

Although the Preacher is frustrated by the inability to see beyond death, which limits efforts “under the sun,” God’s creation is still full of good things to enjoy.

**Q:** How are the Preacher’s arguments different from those of most debaters?

**A:** The Preacher seems to argue from both sides of the issues. This is the contradiction of Ecclesiastes.

**Q:** Which interpretation of Ecclesiastes do you favor?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How does the Preacher balance the futility of life with joy in life?

**A:** Although Ecclesiastes opens by saying everything is meaningless, it concludes by saying, “Fear God and keep his commandments, for this is the whole duty of man.” One of the refrains repeated throughout the book is, “Man should eat and drink and find satisfaction in his work.” Ecclesiastes includes seven exhortations to enjoy life. It maintains that although we cannot see beyond death, God’s creation is still full of good things to enjoy.

#### 7.2.2 — Summarize four possible interpretations of Ecclesiastes.

The Contradiction of Ecclesiastes

More than any other biblical writer, the Preacher explores both sides of the blessings promised by wisdom. As with the book of Job, the writer of Ecclesiastes struggles to understand experiences that seem to contradict the teachings of traditional wisdom. Whereas Job questions conventional wisdom while his friends defend it, however, the Preacher seems to argue for both sides of the issue.

This addressing of both sides of an argument is the essential contradiction of the book. On one hand, the Preacher concludes that pursuing wisdom brings only sorrow and grief (1:12–18) and hard work causes pain and steals away rest (2:17–23). On the other hand, he affirms that wisdom “is better than folly,” (2:13) and “a man can do nothing better than . . . find satisfaction in his work” (2:24). The writer seems uncertain whether pursuing wisdom and working hard have value in making life happy and meaningful.

The writer of Ecclesiastes is conflicted even about the material blessings that result from following the teachings of wisdom. “As goods increase, so do those who consume them. And what benefit are they to the owner except to feast his eyes on them? The sleep of a laborer is sweet, whether he eats little or much, but the abundance of a rich man permits him no sleep” (5:11–12).

### Theology of Ecclesiastes, Part 1

#### 7.3.1 — Describe the Preacher’s view of the world, knowing God, and God’s justice.

Assumptions Challenged

The writers of wisdom literature make a number of basic assumptions about God and the world He has created and then base their reflections on these assumptions. Alan Jenks identifies three theological presuppositions that provide the orthodox perspective found in Proverbs:

1. The world is orderly, ruled by Yahweh, its wise Creator.

2. Knowledge of this order is possible if one opens oneself to wisdom.

3. The wise person who thus aligns with God’s order will experience good things, while the fool suffers for his folly. (1985, 44)

The writer of Ecclesiastes challenges these assumptions, taking a different perspective on each. Much of the rest of the Bible views the order in God’s world as being part of His linear plan that will be realized only at the end of time. Yet the Preacher focuses on the cyclical nature of the world (a characteristic common to wisdom writers)—the repeating patterns—and finds “nothing new under the sun.” Life is not chaotic, and everything that happens may be assigned to a specific time and season, but people cannot always know what these are. Ecclesiastes questions the usefulness of the divine order of creation for planning our lives if we are unable to discern that order.

Another of the Preacher’s perspectives is that knowing God seems impossible. In this life, God is mysterious and hidden. Death ends any search to learn about Him, and the Preacher has no idea what happens after death.

Whereas Job questions God’s justice in regard to his particular case, the Preacher doubts God’s justice in general. He has seen “righteous men who get what the wicked deserve, and wicked men who get what the righteous deserve” (8:14). Although the cause for the injustice can be traced to human sin, human sin does not accurately predict human experience. Those who sin do not always experience bad things. Those who do good do not always experience good things. God’s justice simply does not function as a mechanical law so that certain actions always result in certain outcomes. Although the Preacher does not deny the assumption of the book of Proverbs, he qualifies it.

Life: A Puff of Air

The perspective that life is meaningless pervades all the Preacher says in Ecclesiastes. The Hebrew word translated “meaningless” in the New International Version and “vanity” in the King James Version is hebel . It literally means “a puff of air,” “a breath,” or “a vapor.” It can also refer to the transitory nature of human life (Proverbs 31:30) and the idea of something being false or deceptive (Job 21:34). In Ecclesiastes, it is frequently paired with the Hebrew word translated “spirit,” “breath,” or “wind.” Thus, hebel is like “chasing after the wind” (Ecclesiastes 1:14; 2:11, 17, 26). It means something uncertain and unreliable. The Preacher thinks the promised security and order that wisdom offers always stays just beyond our grasp, disappearing when examined too closely.

The Problem of Death

When reading the Bible, it is important not to assume that the writers understood the world in the same way we do or that they had the same degree of insight into God’s plans and purposes. For example, the Old Testament presents no fully developed belief in an afterlife. Life and death were seen as opposite poles on a continuum and people could move freely between them. The psalmists often spoke of going down toward the grave when they were sick or of the Lord lifting them from the tomb, restoring them to health and safety (Psalms 30:3; 107:17–19). Biblical writers had little to say about resurrection, a belief that was uncommon until the intertestamental period (the four hundred years between the end of the Old Testament and the time of Christ). Even in Jesus’ day, a great deal of debate continued concerning resurrection. Paul used it as a point of contention at his trial before the Sanhedrin in Jerusalem (Acts 23:6–8), and it was the reason many in Athens rejected the gospel (17:31–32).

Parts of the Old Testament show that a person was believed to die and descend into Sheol , a common “tomb” or “grave.” This was considered a place of forgetfulness, not punishment or reward. For this reason, the present life became supremely important. People in Old Testament times felt that if God is just, He must balance the scales of justice in our lives before we die. In Sheol, there was no remembrance nor praise nor communion with God.

However, some Old Testament passages speak of both a resurrection and an afterlife. The clearest and most complete teaching is in Daniel 12:2: “Multitudes who sleep in the dust of the earth will awake: some to everlasting life, others to shame and everlasting contempt.” Isaiah 26:19 speaks of a resurrection of the dead, but not specifically of an afterlife: “But your dead will live; their bodies will rise. You who dwell in the dust, wake up and shout for joy. Your dew is like the dew of the morning; the earth will give birth to her dead.” Among the biblical passages suggesting the idea of resurrection, without clearly describing what happens after death, are Psalms 16:9–11; 49:15; 73:24; and Job 19:25–27. Derek Kidner suggests that both Job and Proverbs envisioned justice toward the righteous and a redress of wrong after death (Proverbs 11:7; 12:28; 14:32; Kidner 1985, 118–119). The idea of life after death unfolds gradually and rather late in the Old Testament.

The Preacher, while possibly aware of belief in resurrection and an afterlife, is nevertheless uncertain about what happens after death. His central problem is his ignorance of what lies beyond death (Ecclesiastes 2:16, 21–22; 3:11, 14, 21; 4:7–8; 5:15–16; 6:6, 12; 8:6–7, 17; 9:4, 6, 10; 11:5; 12:7). He sees death as limiting all achievement and the satisfaction it brings, so accomplishments in life lose their meaning (3:19–20; 4:3; 9:3–6, 10–12; 8:8; 11:8–12:7). Furthermore, death ends any opportunity to right wrongs or correct injustice.

Thus, the prospect of life becomes loathsome for the Preacher. “I hated life, because the work that is done under the sun was grievous to me. All of it is meaningless, a chasing after the wind” (2:17). “Such reflection thrust The Preacher forward into radical denial of life’s goodness. After a lifetime of toil, no profit remains, and the sum of things resembles one mighty act of breathing” (Crenshaw 1981, 129). The Preacher prefers the day of death more than the day of birth because, at death, all of life’s illusions are dispelled and everyone is equal again (Ecclesiastes 4:1–3; 6:1–6).

In the end, the Preacher is forced to accept the limits to revelation that God imposes. “A man can do nothing better than to eat and drink and find satisfaction in his work. This too, I see, is from the hand of God” (2:24; 3:13; 5:18; 12:13–14).

**Q:** How does the Preacher’s view of the order of God’s creation differ from that of most biblical writers?

**A:** The writers of wisdom literature assume the world is orderly and linear and that knowledge of this order is possible through wisdom. In contrast, the Preacher focuses on the cyclical nature of the world. He does not believe life is chaotic, but neither does he believe people can know the time and seasons of the things that happen. He questions the usefulness of the divine order of creation for planning one’s life since we are unable to know that order.

**Q:** How does the Preacher use the word meaningless to describe life?

**A:** The whole book of Ecclesiastes includes the perspective that life is meaningless. The Preacher calls life “a puff of air,” “a breath,” or “a vapor,” something uncertain and unreliable. He feels the promised security and order that wisdom offers always stays just out of a person’s reach.

**Q:** How is the New Testament’s view of the afterlife different from the Old Testament’s?

**A:** When Jesus came, He brought with Him the promise of both resurrection and eternal life, either in or out of God’s presence.

#### 7.3.2 — Explain the Preacher’s view of life in general.

Assumptions Challenged

The writers of wisdom literature make a number of basic assumptions about God and the world He has created and then base their reflections on these assumptions. Alan Jenks identifies three theological presuppositions that provide the orthodox perspective found in Proverbs:

1. The world is orderly, ruled by Yahweh, its wise Creator.

2. Knowledge of this order is possible if one opens oneself to wisdom.

3. The wise person who thus aligns with God’s order will experience good things, while the fool suffers for his folly. (1985, 44)

The writer of Ecclesiastes challenges these assumptions, taking a different perspective on each. Much of the rest of the Bible views the order in God’s world as being part of His linear plan that will be realized only at the end of time. Yet the Preacher focuses on the cyclical nature of the world (a characteristic common to wisdom writers)—the repeating patterns—and finds “nothing new under the sun.” Life is not chaotic, and everything that happens may be assigned to a specific time and season, but people cannot always know what these are. Ecclesiastes questions the usefulness of the divine order of creation for planning our lives if we are unable to discern that order.

Another of the Preacher’s perspectives is that knowing God seems impossible. In this life, God is mysterious and hidden. Death ends any search to learn about Him, and the Preacher has no idea what happens after death.

Whereas Job questions God’s justice in regard to his particular case, the Preacher doubts God’s justice in general. He has seen “righteous men who get what the wicked deserve, and wicked men who get what the righteous deserve” (8:14). Although the cause for the injustice can be traced to human sin, human sin does not accurately predict human experience. Those who sin do not always experience bad things. Those who do good do not always experience good things. God’s justice simply does not function as a mechanical law so that certain actions always result in certain outcomes. Although the Preacher does not deny the assumption of the book of Proverbs, he qualifies it.

Life: A Puff of Air

The perspective that life is meaningless pervades all the Preacher says in Ecclesiastes. The Hebrew word translated “meaningless” in the New International Version and “vanity” in the King James Version is hebel . It literally means “a puff of air,” “a breath,” or “a vapor.” It can also refer to the transitory nature of human life (Proverbs 31:30) and the idea of something being false or deceptive (Job 21:34). In Ecclesiastes, it is frequently paired with the Hebrew word translated “spirit,” “breath,” or “wind.” Thus, hebel is like “chasing after the wind” (Ecclesiastes 1:14; 2:11, 17, 26). It means something uncertain and unreliable. The Preacher thinks the promised security and order that wisdom offers always stays just beyond our grasp, disappearing when examined too closely.

The Problem of Death

When reading the Bible, it is important not to assume that the writers understood the world in the same way we do or that they had the same degree of insight into God’s plans and purposes. For example, the Old Testament presents no fully developed belief in an afterlife. Life and death were seen as opposite poles on a continuum and people could move freely between them. The psalmists often spoke of going down toward the grave when they were sick or of the Lord lifting them from the tomb, restoring them to health and safety (Psalms 30:3; 107:17–19). Biblical writers had little to say about resurrection, a belief that was uncommon until the intertestamental period (the four hundred years between the end of the Old Testament and the time of Christ). Even in Jesus’ day, a great deal of debate continued concerning resurrection. Paul used it as a point of contention at his trial before the Sanhedrin in Jerusalem (Acts 23:6–8), and it was the reason many in Athens rejected the gospel (17:31–32).

Parts of the Old Testament show that a person was believed to die and descend into Sheol , a common “tomb” or “grave.” This was considered a place of forgetfulness, not punishment or reward. For this reason, the present life became supremely important. People in Old Testament times felt that if God is just, He must balance the scales of justice in our lives before we die. In Sheol, there was no remembrance nor praise nor communion with God.

However, some Old Testament passages speak of both a resurrection and an afterlife. The clearest and most complete teaching is in Daniel 12:2: “Multitudes who sleep in the dust of the earth will awake: some to everlasting life, others to shame and everlasting contempt.” Isaiah 26:19 speaks of a resurrection of the dead, but not specifically of an afterlife: “But your dead will live; their bodies will rise. You who dwell in the dust, wake up and shout for joy. Your dew is like the dew of the morning; the earth will give birth to her dead.” Among the biblical passages suggesting the idea of resurrection, without clearly describing what happens after death, are Psalms 16:9–11; 49:15; 73:24; and Job 19:25–27. Derek Kidner suggests that both Job and Proverbs envisioned justice toward the righteous and a redress of wrong after death (Proverbs 11:7; 12:28; 14:32; Kidner 1985, 118–119). The idea of life after death unfolds gradually and rather late in the Old Testament.

The Preacher, while possibly aware of belief in resurrection and an afterlife, is nevertheless uncertain about what happens after death. His central problem is his ignorance of what lies beyond death (Ecclesiastes 2:16, 21–22; 3:11, 14, 21; 4:7–8; 5:15–16; 6:6, 12; 8:6–7, 17; 9:4, 6, 10; 11:5; 12:7). He sees death as limiting all achievement and the satisfaction it brings, so accomplishments in life lose their meaning (3:19–20; 4:3; 9:3–6, 10–12; 8:8; 11:8–12:7). Furthermore, death ends any opportunity to right wrongs or correct injustice.

Thus, the prospect of life becomes loathsome for the Preacher. “I hated life, because the work that is done under the sun was grievous to me. All of it is meaningless, a chasing after the wind” (2:17). “Such reflection thrust The Preacher forward into radical denial of life’s goodness. After a lifetime of toil, no profit remains, and the sum of things resembles one mighty act of breathing” (Crenshaw 1981, 129). The Preacher prefers the day of death more than the day of birth because, at death, all of life’s illusions are dispelled and everyone is equal again (Ecclesiastes 4:1–3; 6:1–6).

In the end, the Preacher is forced to accept the limits to revelation that God imposes. “A man can do nothing better than to eat and drink and find satisfaction in his work. This too, I see, is from the hand of God” (2:24; 3:13; 5:18; 12:13–14).

**Q:** How does the Preacher’s view of the order of God’s creation differ from that of most biblical writers?

**A:** The writers of wisdom literature assume the world is orderly and linear and that knowledge of this order is possible through wisdom. In contrast, the Preacher focuses on the cyclical nature of the world. He does not believe life is chaotic, but neither does he believe people can know the time and seasons of the things that happen. He questions the usefulness of the divine order of creation for planning one’s life since we are unable to know that order.

**Q:** How does the Preacher use the word meaningless to describe life?

**A:** The whole book of Ecclesiastes includes the perspective that life is meaningless. The Preacher calls life “a puff of air,” “a breath,” or “a vapor,” something uncertain and unreliable. He feels the promised security and order that wisdom offers always stays just out of a person’s reach.

**Q:** How is the New Testament’s view of the afterlife different from the Old Testament’s?

**A:** When Jesus came, He brought with Him the promise of both resurrection and eternal life, either in or out of God’s presence.

#### 7.3.3 — Describe the Old Testament’s understanding of what happens after death.

Assumptions Challenged

The writers of wisdom literature make a number of basic assumptions about God and the world He has created and then base their reflections on these assumptions. Alan Jenks identifies three theological presuppositions that provide the orthodox perspective found in Proverbs:

1. The world is orderly, ruled by Yahweh, its wise Creator.

2. Knowledge of this order is possible if one opens oneself to wisdom.

3. The wise person who thus aligns with God’s order will experience good things, while the fool suffers for his folly. (1985, 44)

The writer of Ecclesiastes challenges these assumptions, taking a different perspective on each. Much of the rest of the Bible views the order in God’s world as being part of His linear plan that will be realized only at the end of time. Yet the Preacher focuses on the cyclical nature of the world (a characteristic common to wisdom writers)—the repeating patterns—and finds “nothing new under the sun.” Life is not chaotic, and everything that happens may be assigned to a specific time and season, but people cannot always know what these are. Whereas Job questions God’s justice in regard to his particular case, the Preacher doubts God’s justice in general. Those who do good do not always experience good things. God’s justice simply does not function as a mechanical law so that certain actions always result in certain outcomes. You who dwell in the dust, wake up and shout for joy.
