### Theology of Ecclesiastes, Part 2

#### 7.4.1 — Describe the Preacher’s relationship with God.

Distance from God

The Preacher never uses the name Yahweh when referring to God. Still, God is mentioned forty times in Ecclesiastes, even more than the key term meaningless . God is first and foremost the Creator. He both “makes” and “gives.” The problem is that God’s gifts are often ambiguous. He gives wisdom, joy, riches, and glory, but these seem a mixed blessing, at times bringing more torment than assistance. God’s creation is majestic but unfathomable. Nowhere does the Preacher pray to God or address Him directly, and he believes God should be approached only with the greatest caution. “God is in heaven, and you are on earth; so let your words be few” (5:1). The idea seems to be, “If you must bother God, get in and get out again as quickly as you can. Say as little as possible, for God is irritable and you never know what may anger Him.”

The God of the Preacher is essentially a distant, unfathomable God with whom it is impossible to have a close relationship. The Preacher can never be sure whether God looks on humanity with interest or disdain. God tests people to show them they are like animals (3:18). In the most powerful poem in the book (3:1–11), God is credited with giving the gift of times and seasons but of denying people the knowledge that would allow them to take advantage of His gift. In the face of God’s power and our uncertainty about His relationship with His creation, the Preacher counsels caution.

Once again, the Preacher seems identical to Solomon in his later life. He has not abandoned God altogether. Nevertheless, his understanding of God involves distance and fear. He sees God as an angry, unpredictable, and mysterious figure with whom only a distant and guarded relationship is possible. In the Preacher, we see religious belief but little in the way of personal relationship.

In other Old Testament passages that challenge the traditional understanding of wisdom—such as the book of Job, Psalm 73, and Habakkuk—the writers’ questions are not even directly considered in God’s “responses.” In each case, the “resolution” for the writer comes through a fresh encounter with God Himself. Ecclesiastes is the most pessimistic of the wisdom writings because the writer does not seek God personally. He has no expectation or hope of a personal encounter with the living God. He suffers from a crisis of presence. Unlike others who challenge the wisdom tradition, he neither asks for nor receives a fresh revelation from God.

Wisdom Fails

The Hebrew word for wisdom, hokma , occurs fifty-two times in Ecclesiastes. While folly is condemned and the writer uses the methodology of the wisdom sages of his day, his quest for understanding fails. Wisdom is for him a burdensome task that God has assigned, but the writer cannot fulfill it. “I devoted myself to study and to explore by wisdom all that is done under heaven. What a heavy burden God has laid on men!” (1:13). Wisdom seems to fail in the Preacher’s life because of three issues:

1. His problem with death. Why should he work so hard to live a wise and prudent life if his end is the same as that of a fool?

2. His frustration at the inability to know everything about “what God has done from beginning to end” (3:11). We cannot fully discern God’s creation. Wisdom can neither predict nor control the future. “When times are good, be happy; but when times are bad, consider: God has made the one as well as the other. Therefore, a man cannot discover anything about his future” (7:14; 8:7).

3. His view that, although God is generous, there seems to be no reason guiding the distribution of His gifts to people (Crenshaw 1981, 133–136). “The race is not to the swift or the battle to the strong, nor does food come to the wise or wealth to the brilliant or favor to the learned; but time and chance happen to them all” (Ecclesiastes 9:11).

In the end, wisdom seems unable to achieve its goal (7:15–17; 8:14; 9:2–4). It can neither secure life nor assure happiness.

The Preacher and Paul

Because Ecclesiastes is one of the most difficult biblical books to understand in light of the remainder of the canon, it is tempting to view it as no longer relevant under the new covenant. However, John Custer points out a number of similarities between the Preacher’s theology and Paul’s in his letter to the Romans. Custer compares Paul’s consideration of hope and the meaning of life in Romans 8 with the observations of the Preacher (Custer 1994, 20–22).

In fact, Paul shares a number of common perspectives with the Preacher. He understands that, even in light of the revelation of Christ, the innocent continue to suffer (Romans 8:17–18). Paul also acknowledges a degree of mystery in our relationship with God, so that we need help communicating with Him (8:26). Most notably, Paul affirms in verse 20 that “the creation was subjected to frustration.” The Greek word translated “frustration” is mataiotes . The Septuagint used this word to translate the Hebrew hebel or “meaningless.” Paul recognizes that creation has also been subjected to the meaninglessness of life.

Yet Paul’s conclusions about life are different. Unlike the Preacher, he affirms, “We know that in all things God works for the good of those who love him” (8:28). The difference between Paul’s perspective and the Preacher’s lies in what they knew. Paul knew that (1) all of creation is waiting in travail and suffering (8:22); (2) we do not know how to pray for ourselves (8:26–27); and (3) God is actively working in the midst of our suffering and frustration to bring good to those who love Him (8:28). Custer observes, “What Paul knows and the Preacher cannot know is Christ, crucified and risen” (Custer 1994, 22). The Preacher views the world as being without a hope that transcends death. Paul sees both the promise of life after death and God’s working even in the midst of the seeming “meaninglessness” of life.

Conclusion of the Matter

In Ecclesiastes 12, the Preacher makes some final conclusions. Because the world does not always function as it should and God does not dispense reward according to merit, it is good to enjoy what pleasures are available while they last, receiving them as gifts from God.

The main themes of Ecclesiastes are interrupted by the development of at least two “intrusions”: (1) the attempts to salvage a traditional view of retributional justice (3:17; 7:18; 8:12–13; 11:9), and (2) the two epilogues (12:9–12; 12:13–14) that conclude the book on a positive note. But do these endings contradict what has gone before? Do they suggest later attempts to “fix” the book, as some scholars have suggested? The answer to this question is still a matter of interpretation. What we do know is that the book of Ecclesiastes confronts us with questions that cannot be answered with wisdom alone.

**Q:** What does it mean to say the Preacher suffered from “a crisis of presence”?

**A:** In other Old Testament passages, challenges to the traditional understanding of wisdom were resolved through a fresh encounter with God Himself. However, the writer of Ecclesiastes did not seek God personally. He did not expect or hope for a personal encounter with the living God. He neither asked for nor received a fresh revelation from God.

**Q:** What is the Preacher’s final view of wisdom?

**A:** For the Preacher, wisdom became a “burdensome task” which God assigned but which he could not fulfill. Wisdom could not make life secure or assure individuals’ happiness.

**Q:** How does Paul’s relationship with Christ affect his view of the meaninglessness of life?

**A:** Paul understands that the innocent suffer; there is a degree of mystery in our relationship with God so we need help communicating with Him; and creation is also subjected to the meaninglessness of life. However, his conclusions about life are more optimistic. He sees both the promise of life after death and God’s working even in the midst of the seeming “meaninglessness” of life.

**Q:** Why do some scholars suggest the conclusion of Ecclesiastes was added to “fix” it?

**A:** Ecclesiastes ends on a positive note. Some scholars believe this contradicts the rest of the book, thus suggesting that the ending was added later, perhaps by a different writer. 8 CHAPTER

#### 7.4.3 — Compare and contrast the perspectives of the Preacher and the apostle Paul.

In the most powerful poem in the book (3:1–11), God is credited with giving the gift of times and seasons but of denying people the knowledge that would allow them to take advantage of His gift. In the face of God’s power and our uncertainty about His relationship with His creation, the Preacher counsels caution.

Once again, the Preacher seems identical to Solomon in his later life. He has not abandoned God altogether. Nevertheless, his understanding of God involves distance and fear. He sees God as an angry, unpredictable, and mysterious figure with whom only a distant and guarded relationship is possible. In the Preacher, we see religious belief but little in the way of personal relationship.

In other Old Testament passages that challenge the traditional understanding of wisdom—such as the book of Job, Psalm 73, and Habakkuk—the writers’ questions are not even directly considered in God’s “responses.” In each case, the “resolution” for the writer comes through a fresh encounter with God Himself. Ecclesiastes is the most pessimistic of the wisdom writings because the writer does not seek God personally. He has no expectation or hope of a personal encounter with the living God. He suffers from a crisis of presence. Unlike others who challenge the wisdom tradition, he neither asks for nor receives a fresh revelation from God.

Wisdom Fails

The Hebrew word for wisdom, hokma , occurs fifty-two times in Ecclesiastes. While folly is condemned and the writer uses the methodology of the wisdom sages of his day, his quest for understanding fails. Wisdom is for him a burdensome task that God has assigned, but the writer cannot fulfill it. “I devoted myself to study and to explore by wisdom all that is done under heaven. What a heavy burden God has laid on men!” (1:13). Wisdom seems to fail in the Preacher’s life because of three issues:

1. His problem with death. Why should he work so hard to live a wise and prudent life if his end is the same as that of a fool?

2. His frustration at the inability to know everything about “what God has done from beginning to end” (3:11). We cannot fully discern God’s creation. Wisdom can neither predict nor control the future. “When times are good, be happy; but when times are bad, consider: God has made the one as well as the other. Therefore, a man cannot discover anything about his future” (7:14; 8:7).

3. His view that, although God is generous, there seems to be no reason guiding the distribution of His gifts to people (Crenshaw 1981, 133–136). “The race is not to the swift or the battle to the strong, nor does food come to the wise or wealth to the brilliant or favor to the learned; but time and chance happen to them all” (Ecclesiastes 9:11).

In the end, wisdom seems unable to achieve its goal (7:15–17; 8:14; 9:2–4). It can neither secure life nor assure happiness.

The Preacher and Paul

Because Ecclesiastes is one of the most difficult biblical books to understand in light of the remainder of the canon, it is tempting to view it as no longer relevant under the new covenant. However, John Custer points out a number of similarities between the Preacher’s theology and Paul’s in his letter to the Romans. Custer compares Paul’s consideration of hope and the meaning of life in Romans 8 with the observations of the Preacher (Custer 1994, 20–22).

In fact, Paul shares a number of common perspectives with the Preacher. He understands that, even in light of the revelation of Christ, the innocent continue to suffer (Romans 8:17–18). Paul also acknowledges a degree of mystery in our relationship with God, so that we need help communicating with Him (8:26). Most notably, Paul affirms in verse 20 that “the creation was subjected to frustration.” The Greek word translated “frustration” is mataiotes . The Septuagint used this word to translate the Hebrew hebel or “meaningless.” Paul recognizes that creation has also been subjected to the meaninglessness of life.

Yet Paul’s conclusions about life are different. Unlike the Preacher, he affirms, “We know that in all things God works for the good of those who love him” (8:28). The difference between Paul’s perspective and the Preacher’s lies in what they knew. Paul knew that (1) all of creation is waiting in travail and suffering (8:22); (2) we do not know how to pray for ourselves (8:26–27); and (3) God is actively working in the midst of our suffering and frustration to bring good to those who love Him (8:28). Custer observes, “What Paul knows and the Preacher cannot know is Christ, crucified and risen” (Custer 1994, 22). The Preacher views the world as being without a hope that transcends death. Paul sees both the promise of life after death and God’s working even in the midst of the seeming “meaninglessness” of life.

Conclusion of the Matter

In Ecclesiastes 12, the Preacher makes some final conclusions. Because the world does not always function as it should and God does not dispense reward according to merit, it is good to enjoy what pleasures are available while they last, receiving them as gifts from God.

The main themes of Ecclesiastes are interrupted by the development of at least two “intrusions”: (1) the attempts to salvage a traditional view of retributional justice (3:17; 7:18; 8:12–13; 11:9), and (2) the two epilogues (12:9–12; 12:13–14) that conclude the book on a positive note. But do these endings contradict what has gone before? Do they suggest later attempts to “fix” the book, as some scholars have suggested? The answer to this question is still a matter of interpretation. What we do know is that the book of Ecclesiastes confronts us with questions that cannot be answered with wisdom alone.

**Q:** What does it mean to say the Preacher suffered from “a crisis of presence”?

**A:** In other Old Testament passages, challenges to the traditional understanding of wisdom were resolved through a fresh encounter with God Himself. However, the writer of Ecclesiastes did not seek God personally. He did not expect or hope for a personal encounter with the living God. He neither asked for nor received a fresh revelation from God.

**Q:** What is the Preacher’s final view of wisdom?

**A:** For the Preacher, wisdom became a “burdensome task” which God assigned but which he could not fulfill. Wisdom could not make life secure or assure individuals’ happiness.

**Q:** How does Paul’s relationship with Christ affect his view of the meaninglessness of life?

**A:** Paul understands that the innocent suffer; there is a degree of mystery in our relationship with God so we need help communicating with Him; and creation is also subjected to the meaninglessness of life. However, his conclusions about life are more optimistic. He sees both the promise of life after death and God’s working even in the midst of the seeming “meaninglessness” of life.

**Q:** Why do some scholars suggest the conclusion of Ecclesiastes was added to “fix” it?

**A:** Ecclesiastes ends on a positive note. Some scholars believe this contradicts the rest of the book, thus suggesting that the ending was added later, perhaps by a different writer. 8 CHAPTER

#### 7.4.4 — Summarize the Preacher’s conclusions.

Distance from God

The Preacher never uses the name Yahweh when referring to God. Still, God is mentioned forty times in Ecclesiastes, even more than the key term meaningless . God is first and foremost the Creator. He both “makes” and “gives.” The problem is that God’s gifts are often ambiguous. He gives wisdom, joy, riches, and glory, but these seem a mixed blessing, at times bringing more torment than assistance. God’s creation is majestic but unfathomable. Nowhere does the Preacher pray to God or address Him directly, and he believes God should be approached only with the greatest caution. “God is in heaven, and you are on earth; so let your words be few” (5:1). The idea seems to be, “If you must bother God, get in and get out again as quickly as you can. Say as little as possible, for God is irritable and you never know what may anger Him.”

The God of the Preacher is essentially a distant, unfathomable God with whom it is impossible to have a close relationship. The Preacher can never be sure whether God looks on humanity with interest or disdain. God tests people to show them they are like animals (3:18). In the most powerful poem in the book (3:1–11), God is credited with giving the gift of times and seasons but of denying people the knowledge that would allow them to take advantage of His gift. In the face of God’s power and our uncertainty about His relationship with His creation, the Preacher counsels caution.

## 8 - The Song of Solomon (Song of Songs)

### Introduction to the Song of Solomon

#### 8.1.1 — Summarize the debate over whether to put the Song of Solomon in the canon.

Canonicity

The Song of Solomon is unique among the books of the Bible. In it, the writer celebrates the passion and excitement of being in love, describing in detail both physical attraction and sexual pleasure. Because of its content, the Song of Solomon’s place in the Bible was still being debated at the Council of Jamnia in AD 90 when the Old Testament canon was affirmed by Jewish rabbis in the form in which we now have it. Some considered human sexuality to be an inappropriate topic for holy Scripture. But sexuality is not inherently evil. In fact, in the Bible, particularly in the Song of Solomon, it is seen not as something neutral but as God’s good gift to be enjoyed in the context of marriage. The Bible as a whole focuses on right relationships, both with God and with our neighbor. The Song of Solomon exalts the most intimate relationship possible between a man and woman.

Title and Authorship

The Hebrew title for the book is translated “Song of Songs which is Solomon’s.” The first verse says literally that it is a poem “to Solomon,” which can mean “written by,” “dedicated to,” or simply “according to the tradition of.” The phrase song of songs, sometimes used as the title of the book, is a superlative form meaning literally the “best of songs.” The book is also called by the Latin word for song, Canticum.

Song of Solomon is included among the wisdom writings because of its association with the person of Solomon. Solomon had an extensive harem, with seven hundred wives and three hundred concubines (1 Kings 11:3), so it would be perfectly natural for him to have penned the love poetry of the book. In fact, ancient Jewish tradition identifies Solomon as author of the book bearing his name. Solomon is mentioned by name in several places within the book (1:1, 5; 3:7, 9, 11; 8:11–12), and there is no compelling reason to doubt that part, if not all, of the book originated during his time. As we shall see, the structure of the Song of Solomon is somewhat confusing, and it is possible that Solomon contributed portions of the book that were subsequently edited and to which additions were made.

Interpretation

A number of suggestions have been made as to how to best interpret the Song of Solomon. Some are motivated by discomfort with the explicitly sexual language of the book, and others by its somewhat disjointed structure.

One of the earliest theories among Jews and Christians alike is that the book should be interpreted allegorically. In an allegory, each detail of the story is given a spiritual significance. According to this understanding, the book represents Yahweh’s love for Israel (which is why Jews recite it at Passover) or Christ’s love for the church. One problem with this understanding is that we are still left with the somewhat erotic details. For example, it is difficult to imagine Christ “resting between the breasts” of the church (1:13).

A second approach sees the details as less relevant to the overall message. This view understands the book to present a basic type and not the point-by-point comparison of an allegory. That is, Song of Solomon may represent Christ’s love for the church as a general analogy , not a detailed allegory. Certainly the representations of God’s relationship with Israel and Christ’s relationship with the church are frequently depicted in terms of a marriage relationship (Isaiah 50:1; Jeremiah 2:1; Ezekiel 23; Hosea 2:2; Matthew 22:1–14; Ephesians 5:22–33; Revelation 19:7–8).

The different speaking parts and the presence of a group or chorus have led some to suggest that the book was originally a drama acted out on stage, perhaps as part of a wedding festival (for example, Song of Solomon 5:8–10).

Related to this is the suggestion that the book is a wedding song and part of the seven-day feast in which the bride and groom are treated like a king and queen. According to this view, Song 3:6–11 pictures a royal wedding procession.

The book may also be read as love poetry, celebrating God’s gift of love. It may be a collection of individual poems that were written separately, or it may have been composed as a single work. If it is a collection, it has been edited with repeated refrains (2:7; 3:5; 8:4) and a chain-like structure that brings a certain unity to the book as a whole.

Of the various choices, the allegorical seems to be the least satisfactory, being motivated more by an embarrassment with the overly explicit sexual references than by anything arising from the book’s content. The Song of Solomon can certainly be a type of Jesus’ love for the church, and this does not prevent it from also having been written as a drama, song, or poem. Still, the best approach is probably to understand Song of Solomon as a collection of love poems, while not excluding the idea that it also functions as a type of God’s love for His people.

The Story

The Song of Solomon has no clear plot. Attempts to reconstruct a story line require a great deal of filling in and reading between the lines. But the nature of poetry is to communicate emotions and images, not necessarily to tell a coherent story. The images need not appear in chronological order, and an underlying story may inspire the poetry but not be woodenly followed. This has led to three basic views about the story behind the Song of Solomon: (1) it is an anthology or collection of independent poems, (2) it has three central characters, or (3) it has two central characters (Curtis 1988).

Anthology: Collection of Love Poems

First is the idea that the Song of Solomon is an anthology. According to this view, the book is a loose collection of poems unified by their theme of human love rather than by any single story line. In this way, it is much like the book of Psalms. However, a structural problem exists with the anthology hypothesis. A number of phrases and ideas are repeated throughout the book that suggest literary unity. “The daughters of Jerusalem and/or Zion,” for example, appear throughout (1:5; 2:7; 3:5, 10, 11; 5:8, 16; 8:4). They call on the young woman to avoid arousing love before the appropriate time (2:7; 3:5; 8:4). The term “my lover” appears thirty-one times. Also, Solomon is mentioned seven times in the book, and five of those times identify him as king. If the finished book is an anthology, it has been edited and additions made so the resulting collection has a degree of unity.

Three-Character View

The three-character view or “shepherd hypothesis” points out what appears to be a third figure in the book: that of a shepherd. Some resist the idea that Solomon is being represented as an ideal lover because of his multiple marriages and unholy political and religious alliances. According to this view, Solomon, traveling in a distant country, saw a beautiful young girl and forcibly added her to his harem. He tried many ploys to win her love, but she steadfastly resisted his advances because of her love for a shepherd boy. Finally, Solomon released her, and she was reunited with her true love (Clarke 1970, 9–11). Supporters of this view appeal to Song 8:6–7 as a climax for the book:

Place me like a seal over your heart, like a seal on your arm; for love is as strong as death, its jealousy unyielding as the grave. It burns like blazing fire, like a mighty flame. Many waters cannot quench love; rivers cannot wash it away. If one were to give all the wealth of his house for love, it would be utterly scorned.

They view the song as, at least in part, a critique of the king’s corrupt, fleshly life in contrast to the young woman’s pure, steadfast love. If this were the case, the story could have been popularly circulated in the Northern Kingdom as a partial justification for breaking away from the Davidic monarchy.

One problem with this hypothesis is that it is far from obvious to the casual reader. Song 1:15–2:3, in which the words of love exchanged are reciprocal, would have to be directed to an absent lover or to speak of a time before Solomon took the young woman into his harem. Song 4:12–5:1 seems to speak of the physical consummation of the relationship, but the shepherd hypothesis would understand this as Solomon’s trying to convince the girl to accept him or that she is merely dreaming of her absent lover. In short, this interpretation requires a great deal of filling in gaps, speculating, and reading nonliterally.

Two-Character View

According to the two-character view, Solomon is the lover wooing a northern girl. One interpretation is that this is Solomon’s true love, in contrast to his many politically motivated marriages. Another suggestion is that Solomon inherited the harem from his father while he was still a young man and did not have sexual relations with them. This young woman, then, would be his first and ideal love. Another way to understand the song is as an idealized picture of love written by Solomon in contrast to his own less-than-ideal relationships.

A problem with this view is that we cannot take references such as Song 1:7 literally: “Tell me, you whom I love, where you graze your flock and where you rest your sheep at midday. Why should I be like a veiled woman beside the flocks of your friends?” It is hard to imagine the king of Israel spending his days tending a single flock of sheep.

Again, the view that the book is a collection of love poems offers the easiest resolution to the lack of clarity in the story’s plot. If such is the case, we need not look for a single, coherent story line; and the imagery of king, shepherd, gardens, city streets, and so forth is appropriate to express the experience of new love.

Story or History? Poetry or Dream?

Efforts to reconstruct a clear order of events in the Song of Solomon are doomed from the start by the constantly shifting images. Whether we start with the idea that it contains a collection of poems, a story with three main characters, or a story with two characters, we inevitably have difficulty making all the pieces fit. Rather than trying to outline the whole book, let us take an example of its content and consider how we are to understand it. Songs 3:1–5 and 5:2–8 both open with the beloved woman asleep in her bed and end with a charge to the daughters of Jerusalem. In both, the beloved arises from her bed, searches the city streets for her lover, and encounters the soldiers of the watch. In the first account, they are friendly, and soon after meeting them, she finds her lover. In the second, they are hostile, beat her, and she fails to find him.

How should we understand these two passages? Is the beloved dreaming, or does she actually search through the city at night for her lover? What does her frantic searching tell us about her fears and worries? Why can she not find him in the second account? What do these two accounts, so similar in form and content, tell us about the woman’s love? Certainly the book contains elements of a story, but the Song of Solomon is also poetry. As with all poetry, the Song of Solomon gives us images and paints pictures of the experience of love rather than tells us a simple narrative.

**Q:** How does the Song of Solomon view human sexuality?

**A:** a) a comparison. b) the wise saying. c) a godly word. d) one’s enduring.

**Q:** What does the first verse’s phrase to Solomon suggest?

**A:** a) contemplative wisdom. b) proverbial wisdom. c) Solomon’s writings. d) a mixture of styles.

**Q:** Why is viewing the Song of Solomon as a collection of love poems probably the best approach to understanding the book?

**A:** a) four mashals. b) five collections. c) seven sections. d) nine chapters.

**Q:** Why is it difficult to establish a story line for the Song of Solomon?

**A:** a) are arranged by prominent literary devices. b) focus on home life and marital relationships. c) make extensive use of chiasm and acrostic poems. d) tend to be more organized than his other collections.

**Q:** How do the three-character and the two-character views of the Song of Solomon differ?

**A:** a) home, temple, and king’s court. b) king’s court, home, and marketplace. c) marketplace, temple, and home of the wise. d) court of the wise and the place of the foolish.

Three-Character View

The three-character view or “shepherd hypothesis” points out what appears to be a third figure in the book: that of a shepherd. Some resist the idea that Solomon is being represented as an ideal lover because of his multiple marriages and unholy political and religious alliances. According to this view, Solomon, traveling in a distant country, saw a beautiful young girl and forcibly added her to his harem. He tried many ploys to win her love, but she steadfastly resisted his advances because of her love for a shepherd boy. Finally, Solomon released her, and she was reunited with her true love (Clarke 1970, 9–11). Supporters of this view appeal to Song 8:6–7 as a climax for the book:

Place me like a seal over your heart, like a seal on your arm; for love is as strong as death, its jealousy unyielding as the grave. It burns like blazing fire, like a mighty flame. Many waters cannot quench love; rivers cannot wash it away. If one were to give all the wealth of his house for love, it would be utterly scorned.

They view the song as, at least in part, a critique of the king’s corrupt, fleshly life in contrast to the young woman’s pure, steadfast love. If this were the case, the story could have been popularly circulated in the Northern Kingdom as a partial justification for breaking away from the Davidic monarchy.

One problem with this hypothesis is that it is far from obvious to the casual reader. Song 1:15–2:3, in which the words of love exchanged are reciprocal, would have to be directed to an absent lover or to speak of a time before Solomon took the young woman into his harem. Song 4:12–5:1 seems to speak of the physical consummation of the relationship, but the shepherd hypothesis would understand this as Solomon’s trying to convince the girl to accept him or that she is merely dreaming of her absent lover. In short, this interpretation requires a great deal of filling in gaps, speculating, and reading nonliterally.

Two-Character View

According to the two-character view, Solomon is the lover wooing a northern girl. One interpretation is that this is Solomon’s true love, in contrast to his many politically motivated marriages. Another suggestion is that Solomon inherited the harem from his father while he was still a young man and did not have sexual relations with them. This young woman, then, would be his first and ideal love. Another way to understand the song is as an idealized picture of love written by Solomon in contrast to his own less-than-ideal relationships.

A problem with this view is that we cannot take references such as Song 1:7 literally: “Tell me, you whom I love, where you graze your flock and where you rest your sheep at midday. Why should I be like a veiled woman beside the flocks of your friends?” It is hard to imagine the king of Israel spending his days tending a single flock of sheep.

Again, the view that the book is a collection of love poems offers the easiest resolution to the lack of clarity in the story’s plot. If such is the case, we need not look for a single, coherent story line; and the imagery of king, shepherd, gardens, city streets, and so forth is appropriate to express the experience of new love.

Story or History? Poetry or Dream?

Efforts to reconstruct a clear order of events in the Song of Solomon are doomed from the start by the constantly shifting images. Whether we start with the idea that it contains a collection of poems, a story with three main characters, or a story with two characters, we inevitably have difficulty making all the pieces fit. Rather than trying to outline the whole book, let us take an example of its content and consider how we are to understand it. Songs 3:1–5 and 5:2–8 both open with the beloved woman asleep in her bed and end with a charge to the daughters of Jerusalem. In both, the beloved arises from her bed, searches the city streets for her lover, and encounters the soldiers of the watch. In the first account, they are friendly, and soon after meeting them, she finds her lover. In the second, they are hostile, beat her, and she fails to find him.

How should we understand these two passages? Is the beloved dreaming, or does she actually search through the city at night for her lover? What does her frantic searching tell us about her fears and worries? Why can she not find him in the second account? What do these two accounts, so similar in form and content, tell us about the woman’s love? Certainly the book contains elements of a story, but the Song of Solomon is also poetry. As with all poetry, the Song of Solomon gives us images and paints pictures of the experience of love rather than tells us a simple narrative.

**Q:** How does the Song of Solomon view human sexuality?

**A:** a) a comparison. b) the wise saying. c) a godly word. d) one’s enduring.

**Q:** What does the first verse’s phrase to Solomon suggest?

**A:** a) contemplative wisdom. b) proverbial wisdom. c) Solomon’s writings. d) a mixture of styles.

**Q:** Why is viewing the Song of Solomon as a collection of love poems probably the best approach to understanding the book?

**A:** a) four mashals. b) five collections. c) seven sections. d) nine chapters.

**Q:** Why is it difficult to establish a story line for the Song of Solomon?

**A:** a) are arranged by prominent literary devices. b) focus on home life and marital relationships. c) make extensive use of chiasm and acrostic poems. d) tend to be more organized than his other collections.

**Q:** How do the three-character and the two-character views of the Song of Solomon differ?

**A:** a) home, temple, and king’s court. b) king’s court, home, and marketplace. c) marketplace, temple, and home of the wise. d) court of the wise and the place of the foolish.

#### 8.1.3 — Discuss ways the Song of Solomon has been interpreted historically.

Canonicity

The Song of Solomon is unique among the books of the Bible. In it, the writer celebrates the passion and excitement of being in love, describing in detail both physical attraction and sexual pleasure. Because of its content, the Song of Solomon’s place in the Bible was still being debated at the Council of Jamnia in AD 90 when the Old Testament canon was affirmed by Jewish rabbis in the form in which we now have it. Some considered human sexuality to be an inappropriate topic for holy Scripture. But sexuality is not inherently evil. In fact, in the Bible, particularly in the Song of Solomon, it is seen not as something neutral but as God’s good gift to be enjoyed in the context of marriage. The Bible as a whole focuses on right relationships, both with God and with our neighbor. The Song of Solomon exalts the most intimate relationship possible between a man and woman.

Title and Authorship

The Hebrew title for the book is translated “Song of Songs which is Solomon’s.” The first verse says literally that it is a poem “to Solomon,” which can mean “written by,” “dedicated to,” or simply “according to the tradition of.” The phrase song of songs, sometimes used as the title of the book, is a superlative form meaning literally the “best of songs.” The book is also called by the Latin word for song, Canticum.

Song of Solomon is included among the wisdom writings because of its association with the person of Solomon. Solomon had an extensive harem, with seven hundred wives and three hundred concubines (1 Kings 11:3), so it would be perfectly natural for him to have penned the love poetry of the book. In fact, ancient Jewish tradition identifies Solomon as author of the book bearing his name. Solomon is mentioned by name in several places within the book (1:1, 5; 3:7, 9, 11; 8:11–12), and there is no compelling reason to doubt that part, if not all, of the book originated during his time. As we shall see, the structure of the Song of Solomon is somewhat confusing, and it is possible that Solomon contributed portions of the book that were subsequently edited and to which additions were made.

Interpretation

A number of suggestions have been made as to how to best interpret the Song of Solomon. Some are motivated by discomfort with the explicitly sexual language of the book, and others by its somewhat disjointed structure.

One of the earliest theories among Jews and Christians alike is that the book should be interpreted allegorically. In an allegory, each detail of the story is given a spiritual significance. According to this understanding, the book represents Yahweh’s love for Israel (which is why Jews recite it at Passover) or Christ’s love for the church. One problem with this understanding is that we are still left with the somewhat erotic details. For example, it is difficult to imagine Christ “resting between the breasts” of the church (1:13).

A second approach sees the details as less relevant to the overall message. This view understands the book to present a basic type and not the point-by-point comparison of an allegory. That is, Song of Solomon may represent Christ’s love for the church as a general analogy , not a detailed allegory. Certainly the representations of God’s relationship with Israel and Christ’s relationship with the church are frequently depicted in terms of a marriage relationship (Isaiah 50:1; Jeremiah 2:1; Ezekiel 23; Hosea 2:2; Matthew 22:1–14; Ephesians 5:22–33; Revelation 19:7–8).

The different speaking parts and the presence of a group or chorus have led some to suggest that the book was originally a drama acted out on stage, perhaps as part of a wedding festival (for example, Song of Solomon 5:8–10).

Related to this is the suggestion that the book is a wedding song and part of the seven-day feast in which the bride and groom are treated like a king and queen. According to this view, Song 3:6–11 pictures a royal wedding procession.

The book may also be read as love poetry, celebrating God’s gift of love. It may be a collection of individual poems that were written separately, or it may have been composed as a single work. If it is a collection, it has been edited with repeated refrains (2:7; 3:5; 8:4) and a chain-like structure that brings a certain unity to the book as a whole.

Of the various choices, the allegorical seems to be the least satisfactory, being motivated more by an embarrassment with the overly explicit sexual references than by anything arising from the book’s content. The Song of Solomon can certainly be a type of Jesus’ love for the church, and this does not prevent it from also having been written as a drama, song, or poem. Still, the best approach is probably to understand Song of Solomon as a collection of love poems, while not excluding the idea that it also functions as a type of God’s love for His people.

The Story

The Song of Solomon has no clear plot. Attempts to reconstruct a story line require a great deal of filling in and reading between the lines. But the nature of poetry is to communicate emotions and images, not necessarily to tell a coherent story. The images need not appear in chronological order, and an underlying story may inspire the poetry but not be woodenly followed. This has led to three basic views about the story behind the Song of Solomon: (1) it is an anthology or collection of independent poems, (2) it has three central characters, or (3) it has two central characters (Curtis 1988).

Anthology: Collection of Love Poems

First is the idea that the Song of Solomon is an anthology. According to this view, the book is a loose collection of poems unified by their theme of human love rather than by any single story line. In this way, it is much like the book of Psalms. However, a structural problem exists with the anthology hypothesis. A number of phrases and ideas are repeated throughout the book that suggest literary unity. “The daughters of Jerusalem and/or Zion,” for example, appear throughout (1:5; 2:7; 3:5, 10, 11; 5:8, 16; 8:4). They call on the young woman to avoid arousing love before the appropriate time (2:7; 3:5; 8:4). The term “my lover” appears thirty-one times. Also, Solomon is mentioned seven times in the book, and five of those times identify him as king. If the finished book is an anthology, it has been edited and additions made so the resulting collection has a degree of unity.

Three-Character View

The three-character view or “shepherd hypothesis” points out what appears to be a third figure in the book: that of a shepherd. Some resist the idea that Solomon is being represented as an ideal lover because of his multiple marriages and unholy political and religious alliances. According to this view, Solomon, traveling in a distant country, saw a beautiful young girl and forcibly added her to his harem. He tried many ploys to win her love, but she steadfastly resisted his advances because of her love for a shepherd boy. Finally, Solomon released her, and she was reunited with her true love (Clarke 1970, 9–11). Supporters of this view appeal to Song 8:6–7 as a climax for the book:

Place me like a seal over your heart, like a seal on your arm; for love is as strong as death, its jealousy unyielding as the grave. It burns like blazing fire, like a mighty flame. Many waters cannot quench love; rivers cannot wash it away. If one were to give all the wealth of his house for love, it would be utterly scorned.

They view the song as, at least in part, a critique of the king’s corrupt, fleshly life in contrast to the young woman’s pure, steadfast love. If this were the case, the story could have been popularly circulated in the Northern Kingdom as a partial justification for breaking away from the Davidic monarchy.

One problem with this hypothesis is that it is far from obvious to the casual reader. Song 1:15–2:3, in which the words of love exchanged are reciprocal, would have to be directed to an absent lover or to speak of a time before Solomon took the young woman into his harem. Song 4:12–5:1 seems to speak of the physical consummation of the relationship, but the shepherd hypothesis would understand this as Solomon’s trying to convince the girl to accept him or that she is merely dreaming of her absent lover. In short, this interpretation requires a great deal of filling in gaps, speculating, and reading nonliterally.

Two-Character View

According to the two-character view, Solomon is the lover wooing a northern girl. One interpretation is that this is Solomon’s true love, in contrast to his many politically motivated marriages. Another suggestion is that Solomon inherited the harem from his father while he was still a young man and did not have sexual relations with them. This young woman, then, would be his first and ideal love. Another way to understand the song is as an idealized picture of love written by Solomon in contrast to his own less-than-ideal relationships.

A problem with this view is that we cannot take references such as Song 1:7 literally: “Tell me, you whom I love, where you graze your flock and where you rest your sheep at midday. Why should I be like a veiled woman beside the flocks of your friends?” It is hard to imagine the king of Israel spending his days tending a single flock of sheep.

Again, the view that the book is a collection of love poems offers the easiest resolution to the lack of clarity in the story’s plot. If such is the case, we need not look for a single, coherent story line; and the imagery of king, shepherd, gardens, city streets, and so forth is appropriate to express the experience of new love.

Story or History? Poetry or Dream?

Efforts to reconstruct a clear order of events in the Song of Solomon are doomed from the start by the constantly shifting images. Whether we start with the idea that it contains a collection of poems, a story with three main characters, or a story with two characters, we inevitably have difficulty making all the pieces fit. Rather than trying to outline the whole book, let us take an example of its content and consider how we are to understand it. Songs 3:1–5 and 5:2–8 both open with the beloved woman asleep in her bed and end with a charge to the daughters of Jerusalem. In both, the beloved arises from her bed, searches the city streets for her lover, and encounters the soldiers of the watch. In the first account, they are friendly, and soon after meeting them, she finds her lover. In the second, they are hostile, beat her, and she fails to find him.

How should we understand these two passages? Is the beloved dreaming, or does she actually search through the city at night for her lover? What does her frantic searching tell us about her fears and worries? Why can she not find him in the second account? What do these two accounts, so similar in form and content, tell us about the woman’s love? Certainly the book contains elements of a story, but the Song of Solomon is also poetry. As with all poetry, the Song of Solomon gives us images and paints pictures of the experience of love rather than tells us a simple narrative.

**A:** a) contemplative wisdom. b) proverbial wisdom. c) Solomon’s writings. d) a mixture of styles.

### Sex in Christian Theology and the Bible

#### 8.2.1 — Contrast basic biblical teachings on human sexuality with cultural views.

Human Sexuality

Sex and the Church

A long tradition exists in Christianity of associating sexual desire with sin. Many early Christian theologians were heavily influenced by a concept in Greek philosophy known as dualism. Dualism, whose most influential proponent was Plato, is the understanding that all of reality can be divided into two parts: the spiritual world and the physical world. In dualism, these two worlds are completely separate and, while the spiritual is holy and pure, the physical is degraded and sinful. Although the church formally rejected dualism as heresy, it continued to exercise a great deal of influence on Christian thinking. Augustine of Hippo, one of the most influential Christian thinkers of all time, believed that sexual desire was the direct result of original sin. According to Augustine, sexual desires not under the control of the will and mind are evil. Adam and Eve recognized they were naked only after the Fall, that is, after they became aware of their sexuality. Augustine taught that procreation is the only justifiable reason for sex and that sexual desire, even between a married couple, is a sin.

In our day, sexuality is often taken to the opposite extreme and seen as having little value or importance. The spread of pornography promotes the assumption that sexual desire is simply a mechanical urge, like hunger or stress, and that it has no spiritual component. The lure of pornography is the lie that we can have sexual gratification without the commitment of a relationship. One of the most devastating results of addiction to pornography is that it destroys the ability to find sexual fulfillment and satisfaction in a committed, monogamous relationship. Whereas Augustine saw sexual desire as a chief sin closely associated with the Fall, secular societies today often seek to empty human sexuality of its relational and spiritual content, making it of no real worth or significance.

However, the Bible speaks of the goodness of sexuality and of the virtue of enjoying sexual gratification within the confines of marriage. Genesis 2:20–25 establishes both the institution of marriage and the physical and spiritual union that results. Referring to faithfulness in marriage, Proverbs 5:19 reads, “A loving doe, a graceful deer—may her breasts satisfy you always, may you ever be captivated by her love.” Paul teaches that even sex with a prostitute, a relationless sexual act, has a spiritual component so that “he who unites himself with a prostitute is one with her in body” (1 Corinthians 6:16). Paul also affirms that marriage is the gift of God (1 Timothy 4:3–5) and that both husband and wife have an obligation to meet the sexual needs of his or her partner (1 Corinthians 7:3–5).

Still, although the Bible presents sex as the good creation of God and commands its enjoyment within the context of marriage, a strong tradition persists, particularly among more conservative cultures, to view sex as unclean and sexual desire as sinful. Furthermore, because some think that men feel sexual desire more strongly, they frequently assume that women, the object of men’s desire, are somehow at fault for arousing such passion. In many cultures, women are portrayed as temptresses, leading men astray. As a result, sexual restrictions in those cultures are often far more severe for women than they are for men. For instance, consider the woman caught in adultery (John 8:1–11). Though the woman was caught in the very act of adultery and the law demanded that both the man and the woman be punished, only the woman was taken to Jesus for condemnation.

The Role of Women in Christian Marriage

Today, in traditional Indian culture, a woman is understood to be under the authority of the men in her society, including her father, her brothers, the male leaders in the community, and her husband. Marriages are arranged, and girls can be given in marriage at a very young age. The woman’s duty is to produce children and to satisfy her husband’s sexual needs; however, society does not consider her right to have her own sexual needs met by her husband. The culture of India often sees the woman as a source of impurity, temptation, and illicit desire. As a result, conservative teaching, even conservative Christian teaching, often demands that she subjugate her own needs and wishes and surrender her body to the wishes of her husband (Melanchthon 2004, 180–185).

Whereas Job and Ecclesiastes raise questions about the wisdom tradition in other parts of Scripture, Song of Solomon questions placing women in a secondary role in sexual relations and the notion that it is somehow unseemly for wives to derive the same pleasure and fulfillment that their husbands do from God’s gift of sexuality. The woman in the Song of Solomon does not fit this traditional interpretation of women’s role in marriage. Monica Melanchthon points out two striking features of the book: (1) God is not mentioned anywhere, and (2) the central character is a woman. The book celebrates sexual desire and enjoyment for its own sake without suggesting that procreation is the primary purpose for sexuality. The woman seeks permission from neither her father nor her brothers and actively seeks her lover. She takes pleasure both in his beauty and in his appreciation of her own beauty (Melanchthon 2004, 182). In short, Song of Solomon celebrates the woman’s enjoyment and full participation in sexuality, on equal terms with her lover. This challenges us to think carefully about the ways our cultures affect our ideas and values about sexuality.

The Image of the Garden

The garden is an important symbol in the Old Testament. The Garden of Eden not only is the focal point of creation but also represents God’s presence, blessing, and protection throughout the Old Testament (Genesis 13:10; Isaiah 51:3; Ezekiel 28:13; 31:8–9; 36:35). In Song of Solomon, a number of scenes are set in the countryside, vineyards, and gardens. But the symbol takes on a second meaning, as the garden represents both the woman’s physical beauty and the shared act of lovemaking. As is the nature of poetic imagery, the garden represents the woman (Song 4:12), is watered by the woman (4:15), and is a euphemism (the substitution of an inoffensive term for one that may offend) for lovemaking. “You are a garden locked up, my sister, my bride; you are a spring enclosed, a sealed fountain” (4:12).

Francis Landy suggests an intentional connection between the Garden of Eden and the Song of Solomon’s symbolic use of the garden to represent enjoyment of a sexual relationship between a husband and wife. The story ends with the ruin brought on by sin: death, the loss of Paradise, and the loss of community, reflective of God’s image. God described what sin had done to the relationship of the man and woman. To the woman He said, “Your desire will be for your husband, and he will rule over you” (Genesis 3:16). While sin ruled, the woman would no longer be a full partner in the relationship. Instead, she would be subordinated to the will of her husband. The consequences of their sin sent Adam and Eve forth from Paradise into the world with little more than their altered relationship with one another. Song of Solomon is a partial reversal of this lost Paradise: it brings a return to the garden—a garden of love that again becomes a place of blessing, protection, fruitfulness, and security. As we have already seen, the woman is a full partner in the love relationship with her husband. In 7:10, she says, “I belong to my lover, and his desire is for me” (Landy 1979, 513–528).

Therefore, among its other roles, Song of Solomon provides a commentary on the life experience that brings us closest to a return to Paradise—namely, an intimate, committed love relationship. It provides a counterbalance to Genesis 2–3 that Paul also reflects in the New Testament: “There is neither Jew nor Greek, slave nor free, male nor female, for you are all one in Christ Jesus” (Galatians 3:28). The love shared by a husband and wife is, potentially at least, a mirror of God’s love for us. Although humans are imperfect and even a Christian couple is capable of selfishness and sin, the experience of intimate relationship grants us a brief glimpse of what it will mean to live forever with God in Paradise.

Solomon’s Message to the Church

At the beginning of this chapter, we considered why Song of Solomon is included in the Bible. It is so different from the other books and focuses on a subject that seems to be the opposite of spirituality. However, the Bible, and particularly Paul, makes it clear that the sexual relationship between a husband and wife is intended to be one of the most profoundly spiritual encounters we are capable of this side of heaven. The fact that our sinfulness can empty sex of this spiritual content, even in the context of marriage, in no way changes this basic truth.

But what does this book add to the Bible? Do we really need it? Tremper Longman (2001) invites us to turn these questions around. Instead, he asks, what would the Bible look like without Song of Solomon? The Song of Solomon insists that we reevaluate our notions of sex, understanding it to be good, the creation of God. It challenges us to critique our cultural values and the roles we assign, particularly to women, in marriage. The Song refuses to let us separate the physical from the spiritual and to insist that one is somehow less important or less godly than the other. Finally, it calls on us to see, in the intent of Christian marriage, a reflection of our relationship with Jesus Christ and a glimpse of Paradise where none of our relationships will be ruined by our own selfishness, insecurity, and sin. Sex is an important part of what it means to be a person in relationship, and the Song of Solomon belongs in holy Scripture.

**Q:** What is the source of dualism, and how has it affected Christian views of the sexual relationship?

**A:** a) the setting of later proverbs was the home. b) a woman compiled and edited the book. c) they were the major breadwinners in that culture. d) women were primarily responsible for educating children.

**Q:** What is the Bible’s view of human sexuality?

**A:** a) Genesis. b) Deuteronomy. c) Kings. d) Esther.

**Q:** How does the Song of Solomon help restore women to their God-given place in creation?

**A:** a) the love of money. b) laziness. c) rebellion. d) dishonesty.

**Q:** Why does Landy believe the Song of Solomon intentionally uses the garden image?

**A:** a) an enhanced relationship with one’s spouse. b) damage to character development. c) greater acceptance in the community. d) minimal consequences in enlightened societies.

**Q:** How does Song of Solomon challenge us to more biblical ways of thinking about human sexuality?

**A:** BIB322 Poetic Books, Second Edition UNIT 1 PROGRESS EVALUATION UNIT 2 PROGRESS EVALUATION

#### 8.2.2 — Compare and contrast traditional views of women’s role in marriage with the teachings of Scripture.

Human Sexuality

Sex and the Church

A long tradition exists in Christianity of associating sexual desire with sin. Many early Christian theologians were heavily influenced by a concept in Greek philosophy known as dualism. Dualism, whose most influential proponent was Plato, is the understanding that all of reality can be divided into two parts: the spiritual world and the physical world. In dualism, these two worlds are completely separate and, while the spiritual is holy and pure, the physical is degraded and sinful. Although the church formally rejected dualism as heresy, it continued to exercise a great deal of influence on Christian thinking. Augustine of Hippo, one of the most influential Christian thinkers of all time, believed that sexual desire was the direct result of original sin. According to Augustine, sexual desires not under the control of the will and mind are evil. Adam and Eve recognized they were naked only after the Fall, that is, after they became aware of their sexuality. Augustine taught that procreation is the only justifiable reason for sex and that sexual desire, even between a married couple, is a sin.

In our day, sexuality is often taken to the opposite extreme and seen as having little value or importance. The spread of pornography promotes the assumption that sexual desire is simply a mechanical urge, like hunger or stress, and that it has no spiritual component. The lure of pornography is the lie that we can have sexual gratification without the commitment of a relationship. One of the most devastating results of addiction to pornography is that it destroys the ability to find sexual fulfillment and satisfaction in a committed, monogamous relationship. Whereas Augustine saw sexual desire as a chief sin closely associated with the Fall, secular societies today often seek to empty human sexuality of its relational and spiritual content, making it of no real worth or significance.

However, the Bible speaks of the goodness of sexuality and of the virtue of enjoying sexual gratification within the confines of marriage. Genesis 2:20–25 establishes both the institution of marriage and the physical and spiritual union that results. Referring to faithfulness in marriage, Proverbs 5:19 reads, “A loving doe, a graceful deer—may her breasts satisfy you always, may you ever be captivated by her love.” Paul teaches that even sex with a prostitute, a relationless sexual act, has a spiritual component so that “he who unites himself with a prostitute is one with her in body” (1 Corinthians 6:16). Paul also affirms that marriage is the gift of God (1 Timothy 4:3–5) and that both husband and wife have an obligation to meet the sexual needs of his or her partner (1 Corinthians 7:3–5).

Still, although the Bible presents sex as the good creation of God and commands its enjoyment within the context of marriage, a strong tradition persists, particularly among more conservative cultures, to view sex as unclean and sexual desire as sinful. Furthermore, because some think that men feel sexual desire more strongly, they frequently assume that women, the object of men’s desire, are somehow at fault for arousing such passion. In many cultures, women are portrayed as temptresses, leading men astray. As a result, sexual restrictions in those cultures are often far more severe for women than they are for men. For instance, consider the woman caught in adultery (John 8:1–11). Though the woman was caught in the very act of adultery and the law demanded that both the man and the woman be punished, only the woman was taken to Jesus for condemnation.

The Role of Women in Christian Marriage

Today, in traditional Indian culture, a woman is understood to be under the authority of the men in her society, including her father, her brothers, the male leaders in the community, and her husband. Marriages are arranged, and girls can be given in marriage at a very young age. The woman’s duty is to produce children and to satisfy her husband’s sexual needs; however, society does not consider her right to have her own sexual needs met by her husband. The culture of India often sees the woman as a source of impurity, temptation, and illicit desire. As a result, conservative teaching, even conservative Christian teaching, often demands that she subjugate her own needs and wishes and surrender her body to the wishes of her husband (Melanchthon 2004, 180–185).

Whereas Job and Ecclesiastes raise questions about the wisdom tradition in other parts of Scripture, Song of Solomon questions placing women in a secondary role in sexual relations and the notion that it is somehow unseemly for wives to derive the same pleasure and fulfillment that their husbands do from God’s gift of sexuality. The woman in the Song of Solomon does not fit this traditional interpretation of women’s role in marriage. Monica Melanchthon points out two striking features of the book: (1) God is not mentioned anywhere, and (2) the central character is a woman. The book celebrates sexual desire and enjoyment for its own sake without suggesting that procreation is the primary purpose for sexuality. The woman seeks permission from neither her father nor her brothers and actively seeks her lover. She takes pleasure both in his beauty and in his appreciation of her own beauty (Melanchthon 2004, 182). In short, Song of Solomon celebrates the woman’s enjoyment and full participation in sexuality, on equal terms with her lover. This challenges us to think carefully about the ways our cultures affect our ideas and values about sexuality.

The Image of the Garden

The garden is an important symbol in the Old Testament. The Garden of Eden not only is the focal point of creation but also represents God’s presence, blessing, and protection throughout the Old Testament (Genesis 13:10; Isaiah 51:3; Ezekiel 28:13; 31:8–9; 36:35). In Song of Solomon, a number of scenes are set in the countryside, vineyards, and gardens. But the symbol takes on a second meaning, as the garden represents both the woman’s physical beauty and the shared act of lovemaking. As is the nature of poetic imagery, the garden represents the woman (Song 4:12), is watered by the woman (4:15), and is a euphemism (the substitution of an inoffensive term for one that may offend) for lovemaking. “You are a garden locked up, my sister, my bride; you are a spring enclosed, a sealed fountain” (4:12).

Francis Landy suggests an intentional connection between the Garden of Eden and the Song of Solomon’s symbolic use of the garden to represent enjoyment of a sexual relationship between a husband and wife. The story ends with the ruin brought on by sin: death, the loss of Paradise, and the loss of community, reflective of God’s image. God described what sin had done to the relationship of the man and woman. To the woman He said, “Your desire will be for your husband, and he will rule over you” (Genesis 3:16). While sin ruled, the woman would no longer be a full partner in the relationship. Instead, she would be subordinated to the will of her husband. The consequences of their sin sent Adam and Eve forth from Paradise into the world with little more than their altered relationship with one another. Song of Solomon is a partial reversal of this lost Paradise: it brings a return to the garden—a garden of love that again becomes a place of blessing, protection, fruitfulness, and security. As we have already seen, the woman is a full partner in the love relationship with her husband. In 7:10, she says, “I belong to my lover, and his desire is for me” (Landy 1979, 513–528).

Therefore, among its other roles, Song of Solomon provides a commentary on the life experience that brings us closest to a return to Paradise—namely, an intimate, committed love relationship. It provides a counterbalance to Genesis 2–3 that Paul also reflects in the New Testament: “There is neither Jew nor Greek, slave nor free, male nor female, for you are all one in Christ Jesus” (Galatians 3:28). The love shared by a husband and wife is, potentially at least, a mirror of God’s love for us. Although humans are imperfect and even a Christian couple is capable of selfishness and sin, the experience of intimate relationship grants us a brief glimpse of what it will mean to live forever with God in Paradise.

Solomon’s Message to the Church

At the beginning of this chapter, we considered why Song of Solomon is included in the Bible. It is so different from the other books and focuses on a subject that seems to be the opposite of spirituality. However, the Bible, and particularly Paul, makes it clear that the sexual relationship between a husband and wife is intended to be one of the most profoundly spiritual encounters we are capable of this side of heaven. Instead, he asks, what would the Bible look like without Song of Solomon? The Song of Solomon insists that we reevaluate our notions of sex, understanding it to be good, the creation of God. It challenges us to critique our cultural values and the roles we assign, particularly to women, in marriage. The Song refuses to let us separate the physical from the spiritual and to insist that one is somehow less important or less godly than the other. Finally, it calls on us to see, in the intent of Christian marriage, a reflection of our relationship with Jesus Christ and a glimpse of Paradise where none of our relationships will be ruined by our own selfishness, insecurity, and sin. Sex is an important part of what it means to be a person in relationship, and the Song of Solomon belongs in holy Scripture.

**A:** a) Genesis. b) Deuteronomy. c) Kings. d) Esther.

The story ends with the ruin brought on by sin: death, the loss of Paradise, and the loss of community, reflective of God’s image. God described what sin had done to the relationship of the man and woman. To the woman He said, “Your desire will be for your husband, and he will rule over you” (Genesis 3:16). While sin ruled, the woman would no longer be a full partner in the relationship. Instead, she would be subordinated to the will of her husband. The consequences of their sin sent Adam and Eve forth from Paradise into the world with little more than their altered relationship with one another. Song of Solomon is a partial reversal of this lost Paradise: it brings a return to the garden—a garden of love that again becomes a place of blessing, protection, fruitfulness, and security. As we have already seen, the woman is a full partner in the love relationship with her husband. In 7:10, she says, “I belong to my lover, and his desire is for me” (Landy 1979, 513–528).

Therefore, among its other roles, Song of Solomon provides a commentary on the life experience that brings us closest to a return to Paradise—namely, an intimate, committed love relationship. It provides a counterbalance to Genesis 2–3 that Paul also reflects in the New Testament: “There is neither Jew nor Greek, slave nor free, male nor female, for you are all one in Christ Jesus” (Galatians 3:28). The love shared by a husband and wife is, potentially at least, a mirror of God’s love for us. Although humans are imperfect and even a Christian couple is capable of selfishness and sin, the experience of intimate relationship grants us a brief glimpse of what it will mean to live forever with God in Paradise.

Solomon’s Message to the Church

At the beginning of this chapter, we considered why Song of Solomon is included in the Bible. It is so different from the other books and focuses on a subject that seems to be the opposite of spirituality. However, the Bible, and particularly Paul, makes it clear that the sexual relationship between a husband and wife is intended to be one of the most profoundly spiritual encounters we are capable of this side of heaven. The fact that our sinfulness can empty sex of this spiritual content, even in the context of marriage, in no way changes this basic truth.

But what does this book add to the Bible? Do we really need it? Tremper Longman (2001) invites us to turn these questions around. Instead, he asks, what would the Bible look like without Song of Solomon? The Song of Solomon insists that we reevaluate our notions of sex, understanding it to be good, the creation of God. It challenges us to critique our cultural values and the roles we assign, particularly to women, in marriage. The Song refuses to let us separate the physical from the spiritual and to insist that one is somehow less important or less godly than the other. Finally, it calls on us to see, in the intent of Christian marriage, a reflection of our relationship with Jesus Christ and a glimpse of Paradise where none of our relationships will be ruined by our own selfishness, insecurity, and sin. Sex is an important part of what it means to be a person in relationship, and the Song of Solomon belongs in holy Scripture.
